->**Front Cover of the Manuscript**<-

This historical binding <term>legatura: the process of securing the pages of a book within a cover</term> is crafted from rich brown leather <term>pelle: animal hide treated for use in bookbinding</term>. The cover is adorned with extensive gold-tooled <term>oro: gold leaf applied using heated brass tools</term> ornamentation, indicating that this was a high-status or valuable volume.

A decorative border frames the edges, consisting of three parallel gold lines with small floral stamps at each corner. 

In the center of the cover is a large, oval heraldic crest <term>stemma: a coat of arms belonging to a specific family or noble house</term> stamped in gold leaf. The crest features a central shield with a single horizontal <term>fess: a broad, horizontal band across the center of a heraldic shield</term>. The shield is topped by an elaborate crown and is surrounded by two concentric, ornate decorative borders consisting of intricate scrollwork, floral patterns, and stylized leaf motifs.

<summary>The front cover of a luxury historical manuscript, featuring brown leather decorated with gold tooling and a central heraldic crest featuring a horizontal fess.</summary>

It is truly a thing (O most noble Knight) belonging to a low, base, and filthy mind, to have made oneself a constant student of, and to have fixed a curious thought upon or about, the beauty of a woman's body. What spectacle (O good God) more vile and ignoble can present itself to an eye of clear perception <note>original: "terso sentimento"; meaning a refined or clear-sighted judgment</note>, than a man pensive, afflicted, tormented, sad, and melancholy; to become now cold, now

### Heroic Frenzies.

<sig>* . f.</sig>

placed before the eyes, and sounded in the ears, a noise, a din, a crash of banners, of emblems, of mottos, of epistles, of sonnets, of epigrams, of books, of prolix scribblings <note>original: "prolissi scarfazzi"; a derogatory term for wasteful or messy writing.</note>, of extreme labors, of lives consumed, with shrieks that deafen the stars, laments that make the infernal caves re-echo, pains that stun living souls, sighs to exhaust and move the gods to pity, for those eyes, for those cheeks, for that bust, for that white, for that vermilion, for that tongue, for that tooth, for that lip, that hair, that dress, that cloak, that glove, that little shoe, that slipper, that parsimony, that little smile, that little disdainful look, that widowed window <note>Likely a metaphor for an eye or a specific vantage point of a lover.</note>, that eclipsed sun, that hammer <note>original: "martello"; used here in the sense of a persistent torment or "beating" of the heart/mind.</note>: that disgust, that stench, that tomb, that latrine, that menstrual flow, that carcass, that <term>quartan fever</term> <note>A form of malaria where the fever recurs every third day; here used to describe the "sickness" of obsessive love.</note>, that extreme injury and wrong of nature: which with a surface, a shadow, a phantom, a dream, a <term>Circean enchantment</term> <note>Referring to the sorceress Circe from the Odyssey, who turned men into beasts; Bruno uses this to describe how physical lust degrades human reason.</note> ordained for the service of procreation, deceives us under the appearance of beauty; which at one and the same time comes and passes, is born and dies, flourishes and rots: and is beautiful only for a tiny moment on the outside, while in its inner self there is truly and permanently contained a ship, a shop, a customs-house, a market of whatever filth, toxins, and poisons our <term>stepmother nature</term> <note>original: "madrigna natura"; a common trope in Renaissance philosophy suggesting that nature is sometimes a cruel or deceptive provider rather than a nurturing mother.</note> could produce: who, after having collected that seed for which she makes use of them, often...

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Argument" of the work, addressed to Sir Philip Sidney. The previous page ended with a description of the decay and filth hidden beneath the surface of physical beauty, which one is often forced...</meta>

->**Argument concerning the**<-

...frequently to pay the price of a stench, of a repentance, of a sadness, of a weakness, of a headache, of a lassitude, and of various other misfortunes that are manifest to the whole world; all so that it may bitterly ache where it previously sweetly itched <note>original: "proriua"; literally "to itch" or "to tingle," referring to the physical stimulation of lust.</note>.

But what am I doing? What am I thinking? Am I perhaps an enemy of procreation? Do I perhaps hate the sun? Am I perhaps sorry that I and others were brought into the world? Do I perhaps want to lead men to not gather that sweetest fruit <note>original: "pomo"; literally "apple," a common reference to the fruit of the Garden of Eden.</note> which the garden of our earthly paradise can produce? Am I here to impede the holy institution of nature? Ought I to attempt to subtract myself or another from the bittersweet yoke that <term>divine providence</term> <note>The protective care of God or nature as a spiritual power; here, Bruno refers to the natural instinct to reproduce.</note> has placed upon our necks? Must I perhaps persuade myself and others that our predecessors were born for us, and we are not born for our successors? May God not permit, may God not permit that this could ever have entered my mind! On the contrary, I add that for however many kingdoms and beatitudes might be proposed and named to me, I was never so wise or good that the desire to castrate myself or become a <term>Eunuch</term> <note>A castrated man; Bruno uses this term to reject the idea of total asceticism or the denial of one's physical nature.</note> could come to me. 

Indeed, I would be ashamed if—just as I appear—I should yield even a hair to anyone who worthily eats bread to serve nature and blessed God. And as to whether the "instruments" and the "labors" <note>Bruno uses these terms as euphemisms for the reproductive organs and the act of procreation.</note> can or do assist that good will, I leave it to be considered only by those who can judge such matters and pass sentence.

<meta>catchword: I [Io]</meta>

<summary>Bruno clarifies that his critique of obsessive physical love is not a rejection of procreation or the natural order, affirming that the biological "yoke" is a divine institution. He humorously asserts that he has no desire for ascetic castration and respects the natural role of serving life.</summary>

<keywords>procreation, nature, divine providence, eunuch, earthly paradise, biological duty, Giordano Bruno, masculinity</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Argument" or preface addressed to Sir Philip Sidney. In the previous section, Bruno clarified that he does not reject nature or procreation, but rather the irrational, obsessive enslavement to physical beauty that leads to misery.</meta>

# The Heroic Frenzies.

I do not believe I am bound, for I am certain that all the strings and all the bonds that all the string-makers and net-makers <note>original: "stringhari et lacciauoli"; those who craft the laces or nets used metaphorically here for the snares of romantic infatuation.</note> who ever were or are could ever know how to weave and knot would not suffice—even if death itself were with them—should they wish to bewitch me. Nor do I believe I am cold, for I do not think the snows of Mount **Caucasus** <term>Caucasus</term> <note>A mountain range between the Black and Caspian Seas, used here as a classical symbol of extreme cold.</note> or **Rhiphean** <term>Rhiphean Mountains</term> <note>A legendary mountain range in the far north of the world, synonymous in Renaissance literature with eternal ice and wind.</note> would be enough to cool my heat. Now see, then, if it is reason or some defect that makes me speak. What then do I wish to say? What do I wish to conclude? What do I wish to determine?

What I wish to conclude and say (O illustrious Knight) <note>Referring to Sir Philip Sidney, the English poet and statesman to whom Bruno dedicated this work.</note> is that **what is Caesar’s should be given to Caesar, and what is God’s should be rendered to God.** <note>original: "quel ch’é di Cesare sia donato á Cesare..."; Bruno adapts the biblical command from Matthew 22:21 to argue that every being should receive only the level of devotion appropriate to its nature.</note> I mean to say that to women—though at times divine honors and devotions are not enough for them—nevertheless, divine honors and devotions should not be given. I want women to be so honored and loved as women ought to be loved and honored: for such a cause, I say, and for as much as is owed to that small amount, to that time, and to that occasion, if they have no other virtue than the natural—that is, of that beauty, of that splendor, and of that service. Without which, they should be esteemed as having been born into the world more in vain than a diseased mushroom which occupies the earth to the prejudice of better plants; and more annoyingly than any **monkshood** <term>monkshood</term> <note>original: "napello"; a highly toxic flowering plant (Aconitum), often used as a symbol of poison or lethality.</note> or viper that thrusts its head out of the ground. I mean to say that all things in the universe, so that they may have firm—

<meta>catchword: -ness (completing the word "firmness" or "fermezza")</meta>

<summary>Bruno argues that while he is not immune to passion, he is not "bound" by it, advocating for a rational love where women are respected as humans rather than worshipped as divine idols.</summary>
<keywords>Sir Philip Sidney, Mount Caucasus, Rhiphean Mountains, reason vs passion, Caesar and God, nature, beauty, monkshood</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Argument" or preface addressed to Sir Philip Sidney. The previous page concluded with Bruno arguing that different degrees of devotion are owed to different subjects, and that women, while deserving of honor as women, should not be worshipped as divine beings.</meta>

### Argument regarding the

...means and consistency, have their weights, numbers, orders, and measures, <note>A reference to the biblical Book of Wisdom 11:20: "Thou hast ordered all things in measure and number and weight," implying a rational, divine structure to the universe.</note> so that they may be dispensed and governed with all justice and reason. For this reason **Silenus**, **Bacchus**, **Pomona**, **Vertumnus**, the god of **Lampsacus**, <note>Priapus, the god of fertility, often associated with the city of Lampsacus.</note> and others like them—who are gods of the pantry, of strong beer, and of spilled wine—do not sit in heaven to drink nectar and taste ambrosia at the table of **Jove**, **Saturn**, **Pallas**, **Phoebus**, and their peers; likewise, their shrines, temples, sacrifices, and cults must be different from those of the higher gods.

I wish to say, finally, that these **heroic frenzies** <term>heroic frenzies</term> <note>original: "furori heroici"; the central concept of the book, referring to a divine or intellectual madness that drives the soul toward the contemplation of the infinite, rather than mere physical lust.</note> possess a heroic subject and object; therefore, they can no more be held in the same esteem as vulgar and naturalistic loves than one might see dolphins in the trees of the woods or wild boars beneath the rocks of the sea. <note>Bruno uses a classical trope of the "impossible" (adynaton), popularized by the Roman poet Horace, to illustrate how out of place it would be to confuse spiritual love with physical obsession.</note> To free everyone from such a suspicion, I had first thought to give this book a title similar to that of **Solomon**, <note>King Solomon, traditionally credited with writing the Song of Songs (Canticle of Canticles).</note> which, beneath the outer shell of ordinary loves and affections, similarly contains divine and heroic frenzies, as the mystic and cabalistic doctors <note>Scholars of Jewish mysticism (Kabbalah) and allegorical theology.</note> interpret it; I wanted, to be plain, to call it the **CANTICLE**. <note>original: "CANTICA"; the Latin name for the Song of Songs.</note> But for several reasons I refrained in the end, of which I wish to mention only two. The first is the fear I conceived from the rigorous brow of certain **Pharisees**, <note>A derogatory reference to rigid religious authorities or censors whom Bruno expects will judge his work harshly.</note> who would judge me profane for usurping sacred and supernatural titles for my natural and physical discourse; just as

<summary>Bruno argues that spiritual "heroic frenzies" are distinct from common lust, comparing the two to the hierarchy of gods. He explains that he considered titling the work after Solomon's "Song of Songs" but feared the backlash from religious censors.</summary>
<keywords>heroic frenzies, Solomon, Canticle, allegorical love, divine order, censorship, Pharisees, classical mythology</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues Bruno's "Argument" or preface to the Heroic Frenzies. He is contrasting his work with others and defending his philosophical approach against those who might misinterpret his use of romantic language for spiritual concepts.</meta>

<header>Heroic Frenzies.</header>

these most wicked men and ministers of every villainy <note>Bruno is likely referring to corrupt members of the clergy or pedantic scholars who hide behind religious titles while lacking true virtue.</note> usurp more loftily than words can say the titles of sacred, holy, and divine orators, of sons of God, of priests, and of kings: while we await that divine judgment which will make manifest their malignant ignorance and the learning of others—our simple liberty and the malicious rules, censures, and institutions of others.

The other <note>This "other" reason refers to why he chose his specific title or style, continuing a list of reasons from the previous page.</note> is because of the great dissimilarity seen between the face of this work and that other one, <note>He is referring to the biblical *Song of Songs* (or Canticle of Canticles), attributed to Solomon.</note> although the same mystery and substance of soul is contained under the shadow of both. In that work [the Song of Songs], no one doubts that the first intention of the Wise Man <note>A traditional epithet for King Solomon.</note> was rather to represent divine things than to present anything else; for there the figures of speech are open and manifestly figures, and the metaphorical sense is recognized in such a way that it cannot be denied as metaphorical. There you hear of those **dove’s eyes**, that **neck like a tower**, that **tongue of milk**, that **fragrance of incense**, those **teeth** that look like **flocks of sheep** descending from the washing-place, those **hairs** that seem like the **goats** coming down from the mountain of **Gilead**. <note>These are direct quotations of the highly descriptive, often surreal imagery found in the Song of Songs (Chapters 4 and 6), which theologians interpreted as an allegory for the love between God and the Soul or the Church.</note>

But in this poem of mine, one does not see a face that so vividly pushes you to seek a latent and hidden meaning; for it uses the ordinary mode of speaking and similes more suited to common senses, such as clever lovers ordinarily use, and such as accustomed poets are wont to put into verses and rhymes. They are similar to the sentiments of those who spoke to Ci- <note>The text breaks off here; it likely intends to refer to *Cino da Pistoia* or a similar stylized poetic subject.</note>

<summary>Bruno defends his philosophical liberty against corrupt critics and explains that while his poems use the "ordinary" language of lovers, they contain hidden divine mysteries similar to the biblical Song of Songs.</summary>
<keywords>Solomon, Song of Songs, allegory, religious hypocrisy, poetic metaphor, divine judgment, Mount Gilead</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Argument" or preface of *The Heroic Frenzies*. Bruno is addressing the potential criticism that his work is merely a collection of conventional love poems masquerading as philosophy. He contrasts his work with the famous muses of classical and Renaissance literature and defends his unique authorial intent.</meta>

# Argument on the

...thereida, <note>Likely a reference to a classical poetic subject or a specific beloved name now obscured by the page break.</note> or Lycoris, to Doris, to Cynthia, to Lesbia, to Corinna, to Laura, <note>Bruno lists famous poetic muses: Lycoris (Gallus), Cynthia (Propertius), Lesbia (Catullus), Corinna (Ovid), and Laura (Petrarch). He argues his work is not like theirs.</note> and other such names; whereby one could easily be persuaded that my fundamental and primary intention was directed by **ordinary love** which dictated such concepts to me; which afterwards, by the force of disdain, took wing and became **heroic**. <note>The term <term>heroic</term> here refers to "heroic love" (*eroico furore*), a Platonic concept of the soul's passionate intellectual striving toward the divine, rather than physical romance.</note> 

It is indeed possible to convert any fable, romance, dream, or prophetic enigma, and transfer them—by virtue of metaphor and the pretext of allegory—to signify whatever pleases the one who is most conveniently able to **stretch the meanings**; <note>original: *stiracchiar gli sentimenti*</note> and thus make "everything of everything," just as the profound **Anaxagoras** <note>Anaxagoras (c. 500–428 BC) was a Pre-Socratic philosopher who famously claimed that "in everything there is a portion of everything."</note> said that **everything is in everything**. 

But let whoever wants think what seems right and pleases them; for in the end, whether they wish it or not, by justice everyone must understand and define this work as I understand and define it, and not as I must follow how they understand and define it. This is because, just as the frenzies of that **wise Hebrew** <note>Referring to King Solomon and his *Song of Songs*.</note> have their own proper modes, orders, and titles that no one has been able to understand—and which no one could better declare than him, were he present—so these **Canticles** <note>The poems contained within this volume.</note> have their own proper title, order, and mode that no one can better declare and understand than I myself, when I am not absent. 

Of one thing I want the world to be certain: that for which I **strive** <note>original: *efflagito*, a Latinism for "earnestly entreat" or "demand."</note> in this introductory argument, where I speak singularly to you, Excellent Lord, <note>Sir Philip Sidney, to whom the work is dedicated.</note> and in the **Dialogues** formed upon the following articles, **sonnets**, and **stanzas**, is that I want everyone to know

<summary>Bruno defends his work against being misread as a conventional romance, asserting his authority as the author to define its philosophical and "heroic" intent. He contrasts his "Canticles" with the Song of Songs, claiming they require his own specific interpretation.</summary>
<keywords>Anaxagoras, authorial intent, allegory, heroic love, Solomon, Canticles, Philip Sidney, sonnets, interpretation</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Argument" or preface of *The Heroic Frenzies*. In the preceding text, Bruno asserted that his work should be interpreted according to his own philosophical intent rather than through the lens of common romantic poetry. He now begins to distance himself from the "Orphic" tradition of obsessive devotion to a single woman.</meta>

# Heroic Frenzies.

...for I would consider myself very shameful and a complete beast, if with much thought, study, and labor I had ever taken delight or did take delight in imitating (as they say) an **Orpheus** <note>The legendary Greek musician who descended into the Underworld to retrieve his wife, Eurydice; Bruno uses him here as a symbol of obsessive, earthly romantic devotion that he finds beneath a philosopher.</note> regarding the cult of a woman in life; and after death, if possible, to recover her from hell. I would hardly consider her worthy—without my face blushing—of being loved on the natural level of that instant of the flower of her beauty, and the capacity to produce children for nature and God.

Far be it from me to want to appear like certain poets and versifiers who make a triumph of a perpetual perseverance of such love, as if of such a stubborn madness, which surely can compete with all the other species that can reside in a human brain. I say I am so far from that most vain, most vile, and most shameful glory, that I cannot believe a man who possesses a grain of sense and spirit could spend more love on such a thing than I have spent in the past and could spend at present.

And by my faith, if I wish to bring myself to defend as noble the talent of that **Tuscan poet** <note>A reference to Petrarch (Francesco Petrarca, 1304–1374), the famous Italian poet known for his sonnets to Laura. Bruno is critiquing the Petrarchan tradition of unrequited, courtly love.</note> who showed himself so full of longing on the banks of the **Sorgue** for a lady of **Vaucluse** <note>The Sorgue river and the valley of Vaucluse in France, where Petrarch lived and famously wrote his poems to his muse, Laura.</note>—and I do not want to say he was a madman fit for chains—I will lead myself to believe, and force myself to persuade others, that he, because he did not have a talent suited for better things, intentionally sought to nourish that **melancholy** <note>In the Renaissance, "melancholy" was considered one of the four humors; it was often associated with the contemplative life and poetic inspiration, but also with depression and madness.</note> to celebrate his own talent upon that tangle of themes, by explaining the feelings of an ostinate **vulgar, animal, and beastly love**; which they have...

<summary>Bruno critiques the tradition of romantic obsession, specifically targeting Petrarch's devotion to Laura as a "stubborn madness" or a calculated poetic exercise. He argues that a man of intellect should not waste his spirit on such "vulgar" animal love when there are higher things to contemplate.</summary>

<keywords>Orpheus, Petrarch, Sorgue, Vaucluse, melancholy, vulgar love, poetic madness, philosophical intent</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues Bruno's "Argument" or preface. Having previously dismissed obsessive Petrarchan love as "animalistic" and unworthy of a philosopher, he now addresses the literary tradition of "paradoxical encomium"—the praise of trivial things—and clarifies that his critique of women is a general philosophical one, making a specific exception for the noblewomen of England and their Queen.</meta>

### Argument regarding the

...[as] others have done who have spoken in praise of the
fly, of the beetle, of the donkey, of Silenus, of
Priapus <note>These are examples of "paradoxical encomium," a classical literary genre where an author displays wit by praising something typically considered trivial or repulsive.</note>; their apes are those who have writ-
ten poetry in our times in praise of chamber pots,
of the bagpipe, of the bean, of the bed, of lies,
of dishonor, of the oven, of the hammer, of
famine, of the plague; which perhaps ought
to go about no less proud and haughty for the cele-
brated mouths of their singers, than the afore-
mentioned ladies and others should and could for theirs.

-># The Heroic Frenzies.<-

...considering that if there is fault or error in this, it must be attributed to the species and to nature, and not specifically to individuals. Certainly, what I abominate regarding such matters is that studious and disordered Venereal love <note>Refers to lustful or physical love associated with the goddess Venus, as opposed to intellectual or divine love.</note> which some are accustomed to spend upon it, in such a way that they make themselves slaves with their intellect, and come to imprison the most noble powers and acts of the intellectual soul. If this intent is considered, no chaste and honest woman will want to be saddened by our natural and truthful discourse, nor be angry with me, but rather subscribe to it and love me even more; for I passively condemn that love in women toward men which I actively reprove in men toward women. Such then being my mind, talent, opinion, and determination, I protest that my first and principal, intermediate and accessory, ultimate and final intent in this weaving <note>original: "tessitura" - Bruno uses a textile metaphor for the structure of his written work.</note>, was and is to bring about divine contemplation, and to set before the eyes and ears of others the frenzies <note>The "frenzies" (furori) refer to the Platonic idea of divine madness—an ecstatic state where the soul transcends the senses to reach God.</note> not of vulgar, but of heroic loves, explained in two parts; each of which is divided into five dialogues.

### ARGUMENT OF THE FIVE DIALOGUES OF THE FIRST PART.

In the First dialogue of the first part there are five articles, where in order: in the first, the causes and internal moving principles are shown under the name and figure of the mountain, and the river, and the muses who declare themselves present—not because they were called, invoked, or sought, but rather as those who often offer themselves importunately...

...are offered: whereby it is signified that the **divine light** is always present, always offers itself, and always calls and knocks at the doors of our senses and other cognitive and apprehensive powers. This is likewise signified in the *Song of Solomon* <note>original: "Cantica di Salomone"</note> where it is said: *Behold, he stands behind our wall, looking through the lattices, and peering through the windows* <note>original Latin: "EN IPSE STAT POST PARIETEM NOSTRUM RESPICIENS PER CANCELLOS, ET PROSPICIENS PER FENESTRAS" — Song of Solomon 2:9.</note>. It often happens, through various occasions and impediments, that this light remains excluded and held outside.

In the **second article**, it is shown what those subjects, objects, affects, instruments, and dispositions <note>original: "affetti" — used here to mean the emotional or spiritual states that prepare the soul.</note> are, through which this divine light introduces itself, reveals itself, and takes possession of the soul, so that it may raise the soul and convert it toward God.

In the **third**, the resolution <note>original: "proponimento"</note>, definition, and determination that the well-informed soul makes concerning the one, perfect, and ultimate end.

In the **fourth**, the **civil war** <note>This refers to the internal struggle between the soul's higher intellectual aspirations and its lower sensory appetites.</note> that follows and reveals itself against the spirit after such a resolution; whence the *Song [of Solomon]* said: *Do not marvel because I am dark, for the sun has discolored me, because my brothers have fought against me, they who have placed me as a keeper in the vineyards* <note>original Latin: "NOLI MIRARI QVIA NIGRA SVM, DECOLORAVIT ENIM ME SOL, QVIA FRATRES MEI PVGNAVERVNT CONTRA ME, QVAM POSVERVNT CVSTODEM IN VINEIS" — Song of Solomon 1:5-6.</note>. There, only four standard-bearers <note>original: "antesignani" — leaders of the vanguard in a Roman legion.</note> are explained: **Affect**, **Fatal Impulse**, the **Appearance of Good**, and **Remorse**. These are followed by many military cohorts of so many contrary, various, and diverse powers, with their ministers, means, and organs which...

In the **fifth [article]**, a natural contemplation is explained in which it is shown that every opposition is reduced to **friendship** <note>original: "amicitia" — used here in the philosophical sense of a harmonious joining of opposites.</note> either through the victory of one of the opposites, or through harmony and tempering, or through some other reason of **vicissitude** <note>original: "uiciſſitudine" — the concept that everything in the universe is in a constant state of flux and cyclic change.</note>; every strife into concord, every diversity into unity: which doctrine has been expanded upon by us in the discourses of other dialogues.

## IN THE SECOND dialogue
there is described more explicitly the order and act of the **struggle** <note>original: "militia" — literally "warfare" or "military service," representing the soul's active striving and inner conflict.</note> which is found in the substance of this composition of the frenzied lover, and therein, in the **first article**, three sorts of opposition are shown. The **First**, of one affect and act against the other, such as where there are cold hopes and hot desires. The **Second**, of the same affects and acts within themselves, not only at different times, but even at the same moments: as when one is not content with oneself, but attends to another; and at once, at once <note>The repetition of "inſieme inſieme" emphasizes the simultaneity of conflicting emotions.</note> loves and hates. The **Third**, between the power that follows and aspires, and the object that flees and withdraws. In the **second article**, the opposition is revealed which is like that of two contrary impulses in general; to which all particular and subordinate oppositions are related: while, as if to two contrary places and seats, one ascends or descends: indeed, the whole composite being <note>The human person, viewed as a "composite" of body and soul, or matter and spirit.</note>, through the diversity of the inclinations found in its various parts, and the variety of dispositions that occur within them, becomes

## IN THE THIRD Dialogue
it is revealed how much power the **will** has in this **spiritual warfare** <note>original: "militia" — Bruno uses military terminology to describe the disciplined, active struggle of the soul as it strives toward divine intellectual beauty.</note>, as it is to the will alone that it belongs to order, to begin, to execute, and to complete; to which is intoned in the **Song of Songs** <note>original: "Cantica" — referring to the biblical Song of Solomon, which Renaissance philosophers often interpreted as an allegory for the soul's desire for God.</note>: **"RISE UP, MAKE HASTE, MY DOVE, AND COME, FOR NOW THE WINTER HAS PASSED, THE RAIN IS GONE, FLOWERS HAVE APPEARED IN OUR LAND, THE TIME OF PRUNING HAS ARRIVED."** <note>original Latin: "SVRGE PROPERA COLVMBA MEA ET VENI, IAM ENIM HIEMS TRANSIIT, IMBER ABIIT, FLORES APPARVERVNT IN TERRA NOSTRA, TEMPVS PVTATIONIS ADVENIT." — Song of Solomon 2:10-12.</note> This will provides strength to others in many ways, and especially to itself when it reflects upon itself and doubles; at that moment when it *wants to want*, and it pleases it that it wants what it wants; or it retracts itself, when it *does not want what it wants*, and it is displeased that it wants what it wants. Thus, in everything and through everything, it approves that which is good and that which **natural law and justice** <note>original: "natural legge et giuſtitia" — the inherent moral and rational order of the universe.</note> define for it; and never at all does it approve that which is otherwise. And this is what is explained in the **first and second articles**. In the **third**, the twin fruit of such efficacy is seen, according to how (as a consequence of the affection that attracts and ravishes them) high things become low, and low things become high—as if by the force of a **dizzying impulse** <note>original: "vertiginoſo appulſo" — describing the ecstatic, whirling motion of the soul caught in divine frenzy.</note> and the success of **vicissitude** <note>original: "viciſſitudiniual" — relating to the inevitable cycle of change and the turning of Fortune's wheel that defines the physical world.</note>, they say that the fl-

# Heroic Frenzies

...flame thickens into air, vapor, and water; and water thins into vapor, air, and flame.

**IN SEVEN** articles of the **fourth dialogue**, the impulse and vigor of the **intellect** <note>original: "intelletto" — for Bruno, the intellect is the highest faculty of the soul, capable of perceiving divine truths beyond the reach of mere reason.</note> are contemplated, which ravishes the affection along with it, as well as the progress of the thoughts of the **frenzied one** <note>original: "furioſo" — refers to the "heroic lover" who is in a state of divine madness or intense spiritual longing for the infinite, rather than literal insanity.</note>, and of the passions of the soul which finds itself governing this so turbulent **Republic** <note>original: "Republica" — a metaphor for the internal government of the soul, where various emotions and faculties must be managed like citizens in a state.</note>. There, it is not obscure who is the hunter, the fowler, the wild beast, the puppies, the chicks, the den, the nest, the rock, the prey, the fulfillment of so many labors, the peace, rest, and the desired end of such a troublesome conflict.

**IN THE FIFTH** dialogue, the state of the frenzied one during this time is described, and the order, reason, and condition of his pursuits and **fortunes** <note>original: "fortune" — refers to the external circumstances or "fates" that affect the seeker's journey.</note> are shown. In the **first article**, regarding what pertains to pursuing the object that makes itself scarce. In the **second**, regarding the continuous and unyielding concurrence of the **affections** <note>original: "affetti" — the deep emotional and spiritual drives or "inclinations" of the heart.</note>. In the **third**, regarding the high and burning—though vain—resolutions. In the **fourth**, regarding the voluntary will. In the **fifth**, regarding the ready and strong defenses and aids. In those that follow, the condition of his fortune, study, and state is shown in various ways, along with their reason and fitness, through the **antitheses, similarities, and comparisons** expressed in each of these articles.

### ARGUMENT OF THE FIVE DIALOGUES
### OF THE SECOND PART.

IN THE FIRST Dialogue of the second part, a **nursery** <note>original: "seminario" — here used in the sense of a "seedbed" or a foundational collection of ideas.</note> of the manners and reasons for the state of the **heroic frenzied one** <term>heroico furioso: a seeker driven by a "divine madness" or intellectual passion to perceive the infinite, rather than one suffering from literal insanity.</term> is presented. Where, in the first **sonnet**, the state of that person under the **wheel of time** <note>A common Renaissance motif representing the cyclical nature of human life and the inevitable changes brought by fate and the heavens.</note> is described. In the second, he excuses himself from the reputation of an ignoble occupation and the unworthy loss caused by the narrowness and brevity of time. In the third, he accuses the impotence of his studies which, although internally illuminated by the excellence of the **Object** <note>In Bruno’s philosophy, the "Object" is the ultimate Truth or the Divine Unity that the soul longs to grasp.</note>, on the contrary, come to be darkened and clouded by them. In the fourth is the lament of the profitless effort of the **faculties of the soul** <note>original: "facultadi de l’ anima" — refers to the powers of the mind, such as memory, intellect, and will, which must work in harmony to reach spiritual heights.</note> while it seeks to rise—despite the inequality of its powers—to that state which it claims and aims for. In the fifth, the contrariety and domestic conflict found within a single subject are recounted, whereby it cannot entirely cling to one goal or end. In the sixth, the aspiring affection is expressed. In the seventh, the poor correspondence found between the one who aspires and that to which they aspire is taken into consideration. In the eighth, the distraction of the soul is set before the eyes, resulting from the conflict between external and internal things, and of internal things within themselves, and of external things within themselves. In the ninth, the age and time of the ordinary course of life are explained in relation to the act of high and profound **contemplation**: regarding that which does not disturb the

To which purpose it was said by the wise man: **HE WHO IS A SEARCHER OF MAJESTY SHALL BE OVERWHELMED BY GLORY** <note>original: "QUI SCRVTATOR EST MAIESTATIS, OPPRIMETVR A’ GLORIA" — a Latin citation from the Book of Proverbs 25:27.</note>. In the last, what is shown in the twelfth by similitude and figure is more manifestly expressed.

IN THE SECOND Dialogue, in a sonnet and a dialogical discourse upon it, there is specified the first motive that tamed the strong, softened the hard, and brought it under the amorous empire of a **superior Cupid** <note>In Renaissance Neoplatonism, "Cupid" often represents the force of desire; the "superior" Cupid is the divine desire for beauty and truth, rather than carnal lust.</note>, celebrating such vigilance, study, choice, and goal.

IN THE FOURTH Dialogue, there are figured and to some extent explained the nine reasons for the inability, lack of proportion, and deficiency of the human gaze and the **apprehensive power** <note>the faculty of the mind that grasps or perceives information</note> regarding divine things. Where, in the first blind man, who is so from birth, is noted the reason which is due to the nature that humbles and lowers us. In the second blind man, through the poison of Jealousy, is noted that which is due to the **irascible and concupiscible** <term>irascible and concupiscible: categories of the "appetitive" soul in medieval psychology; the former deals with anger and courage, the latter with desire and pleasure.</term> powers which divert and mislead us. In the third blind man, through the sudden appearance of intense light, is shown that which proceeds from the clarity of the object that dazzles us. In the fourth, raised and nourished for a long time in the sight of the sun, is shown that which comes from too high a contemplation of unity, which steals us away from the multitude. In the fifth, who always has his eyes full of thick tears, is designated the lack of proportion of means between the power and the object which hinders us. In the sixth, who through much weeping has dissipated the organic visual humor <note>original: "humor organico visivo" — in early modern medicine, this refers to the fluids within the eye necessary for sight.</note>, is figured the lack of true intellectual nourishment that weakens us. In the seventh, whose eyes are turned to ash by the ardor of the heart, is noted the burning affection that disperses, thins out, and sometimes devours the power of discernment. In the eighth, blind because of the wound from an arrow's point, is noted that which comes from the very act of the union with the **form** <note>original: "specie" — in Scholastic philosophy, this refers to the mental image or "species" through which an object is understood by the mind.</note> of the object; which overcomes, alters, and corrupts the...

--- Page 28 ---
<meta>This page completes the enumeration of the nine types of metaphorical blindness discussed in the Fourth Dialogue and begins the "Argument" or introduction to the Fifth Dialogue.</meta>

**apprehensive power** <note>the faculty of the mind that grasps or perceives information</note>, which is suppressed by the weight and falls under the force of the presence of that object; hence, not without reason, its sight is sometimes figured as the appearance of a penetrating lightning bolt. In the ninth blind man, who, being mute, cannot explain the cause of his blindness, is signified the "reason of reasons," which is the hidden divine judgment. This judgment has given to men this pursuit and thought of investigating in such a way that one can never reach higher than the cognition of one's own blindness and ignorance, and must value silence more than speech. 

By this, ordinary ignorance is neither excused nor favored; for he who does not see his own blindness is doubly blind. And this is the difference between those who are truly studious and the idle fools: the latter are buried in the lethargy of being deprived of judgment regarding their own lack of sight; while the former are alert, awake, and prudent judges of their own blindness. Therefore, they are in the process of inquiry and at the gates of the acquisition of light, from which the others have long been banished.

### ARGUMENT AND ALLEGORY
#### OF THE FIFTH DIALOGUE,

IN THE Fifth dialogue, because two women are introduced—to whom (according to the custom of my country) <note>The author refers to the social expectations for women in 16th-century Nola and the Kingdom of Naples.</note> it is not considered fitting to comment, argue, decipher, know too much, or be "learned ladies" <note>original: "dottoresse"</note> so as to usurp the office of teaching and providing instruction, rules, and doctrine to men. But they may indeed divine and prophesy sometimes when they find the spirit...

->### Heroic Frenzies.<-

in person; however, it has been enough for them to act merely as reciters of the figures <note>In this context, "figures" refers to the poetic images or metaphors used in the dialogue.</note>, leaving to some masculine intellect the thought and business of clarifying the matter signified. To this intellect (to lighten or indeed remove the labor), I make it known how these **nine blind men**—just as they function in terms of duty and external causes, so too with many other subjective differences—proceed with a different significance than the nine in the previous dialogue. This is because, according to the common imagination of the **nine spheres** <note>The nine celestial spheres of the traditional geocentric universe (Moon, Mercury, Venus, Sun, Mars, Jupiter, Saturn, Fixed Stars, and the Primum Mobile).</note>, they show the number, order, and diversity of all things existing within **absolute unity**; in which, and above which, all the proper **intelligences** <note>In Renaissance philosophy, "intelligences" were spiritual beings or minds believed to guide the movement of the celestial spheres.</note> are ordered, which, according to a certain analogical similarity, depend on the first and unique One. 

These are distinguished by **Kabbalists**, **Chaldeans**, **Mages**, **Platonists**, and **Christian theologians** into nine orders because of the perfection of the number [nine] which dominates the universality of things and, in a certain way, gives form to the whole. Therefore, by simple reason, they signify the divinity; and according to its reflection and squaring within itself, they signify the number and substance of all dependent things. All the most illustrious contemplators, whether they be philosophers or theologians, whether they speak by reason and their own light, or by faith and a superior light, understand in these intelligences the **circle of ascent and descent** <note>The Neoplatonic concept of *emanatio* and *remissio*: the way all things flow out from God and strive to return to Him.</note>. Hence the **Platonists** say that through a certain conversion <term>conversion: a turning or transformation of the soul’s orientation</term>, it happens that those who are above **fate** become subject to the fate of time and

This same conversion is signified by the Pythagorean poet <note>The "Pythagorean poet" refers to the Roman poet Virgil, whose *Aeneid* (Book VI) describes the reincarnation of souls—a doctrine central to Pythagorean thought.</note>, where he says:

> God calls all these in a great column to the river Lethe <note>Lethe is the mythical river of forgetfulness in the underworld; drinking its waters caused souls to forget their past lives before reincarnating.</note> when they have rolled the wheel through a thousand years, <note>original: "Has omnes vbi mille rotā voluere per annos, / Lethæū ad fluviū deus euocat agmine magno;"</note>
> so that they may begin to wish to return again into bodies. <note>original: "Rursus vt incipiant in corpora velle reuerti."</note>

This (say some) is what is meant where it is said in Revelation <note>Referring to the biblical Book of Revelation 20:2-3.</note> that the dragon shall stay bound in chains for a thousand years, and once those have passed, he shall be released. To this same meaning, they intend many other passages to point, where the <term>millennium</term> <note>The "millenary" or thousand-year period (from the Latin *mille*).</note> is sometimes expressed, sometimes signified by a single year, sometimes by an age, sometimes by a <term>cubit</term> <note>A cubit was an ancient unit of length based on the forearm; here Bruno suggests it is used metaphorically as a unit of time.</note>, now in one way and now in another. Furthermore, this millennium itself is certainly not taken according to the revolutions defined by the years of the sun, but according to the various ratios of the different measures and orders by which various things are dispensed; because just as the years of the stars are different, so the species of particulars are not the same. 

Now as for the matter of the <term>revolution</term> <note>Here meaning a cyclic return or a "turning over" of souls from one state to another.</note>, it is common knowledge among Christian theologians that from each of the nine orders of spirits, multitudes of legions have been cast down to these low and dark regions; and so that those seats do not remain vacant, divine providence wills that from these souls living in human bodies, some should be raised up to that eminence. But among the philosophers, I have seen only Plotinus <note>Plotinus (c. 204–270 AD) was the central figure of Neoplatonism; Bruno frequently draws on his theories of the soul's ascent and descent.</note> say expressly

<summary>Bruno discusses the cyclic nature of existence, connecting the "thousand-year" cycles in Virgil's poetry and the Book of Revelation to the migration of souls. He explains that these time periods are symbolic rather than literal solar years and notes the theological belief that human souls fill the heavenly places vacated by fallen angels.</summary>

# Heroic Frenzies.

as all great theologians [say], that such a revolution <note>Here, "revolution" refers to a cosmic cycle or the turning of the wheel of time/souls.</note> is not for everyone, nor does it happen always: but only once. And among theologians, Origen <note>Origen of Alexandria (c. 185–253 AD) was an early Christian scholar whose views on the pre-existence and eventual restoration of all souls (apocatastasis) were later deemed heretical.</note> alone—like all great philosophers after the <term>Sadducees</term> <note>A Jewish sect active in Judea during the Second Temple period; they were known for denying the immortality of the soul and the resurrection.</note> and many others who were rejected—has dared to say that the revolution is cyclical and eternal; and that everything which ascends must descend back down: as is seen in all the elements and things that are on the surface, the lap, and the womb of nature.

And I, by my faith, say and confirm that their [the theologians'] sense is most fitting for those who oversee the laws and institutions of peoples; just as I do not fail to affirm and accept the sense of those who speak according to <term>natural reason</term> <note>Natural reason refers to knowledge gained through observation and logic, independent of divine revelation.</note>, who are among the few, the good, and the wise. The opinion of these latter has been deservedly rejected when published before the eyes of the multitude, who can hardly be restrained from vice and spurred to virtuous acts by the faith in eternal punishments: what would they do if they were persuaded of some lighter condition in rewarding heroic and human deeds, and in punishing crimes and villainies?

But to come to the conclusion of this my progress. I say that from here one takes the reason and discourse of the blindness and light of these nine, who are now seeing, now blind, now illuminated: who are rivals now in the shadows and vestiges of divine beauty, now are completely blind, and now in the most open light peacefully enjoy themselves. At the time when

<meta>catchword: are</meta>

<summary>Bruno contrasts the theological view of a single judgment with the philosophical view of eternal cycles, arguing that while cyclical recurrence aligns with natural reason, the doctrine of eternal punishment is necessary for maintaining social order among the masses.</summary>
<keywords>Origen, Sadducees, natural reason, social order, divine beauty, cycles of time, reincarnation, eternal punishment</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the "Argument" of the Fifth Dialogue, moving from the philosophical debate on the soul's cyclical nature to the specific allegorical figures used in the text. It begins to interpret the myth of Circe as a symbol for physical matter and the process of reincarnation.</meta>

### Argument on the

those who are in the first condition are reduced to the chamber of *Circe*, who signifies omnipotent matter, and is called the daughter of the sun, because from that father of forms <note>In Renaissance philosophy, the "Sun" often represents the Divine Intellect or the source of "forms"—the spiritual blueprints of all things.</note> she holds the inheritance and possession of all those things which, with the sprinkling of the waters—that is, with the act of generation: by the power of enchantment, that is, of hidden harmonic reason—she changes entirely, making those who see become blind: because generation and corruption <note>The process of being born into a body and the eventual decay of that body.</note> are the cause of oblivion and blindness, as the ancients explain through the figure of the souls who bathe and intoxicate themselves in *Lethe* <note>In Greek mythology, Lethe is the river of forgetfulness in the underworld; souls drank from it to forget their previous lives before being reborn.</note>.

Hence, where the blind lament, saying, *Daughter and mother of darkness and horror:* it signifies the perturbation and sadness of the soul that has lost its wings <note>A reference to the Platonic idea that the soul originally had wings to fly in the divine realms but lost them when descending into a physical body.</note>, which is mitigated only when it is given hope of recovering them. Where *Circe* says: **TAKE** another of my fatal vessels, it signifies that they carry within themselves the decree and destiny of their own change, which is nevertheless said to be offered to them by *Circe* herself; for one contrary is originally contained within the other, even if it is not there effectively <note>The philosophical principle that opposites (like life and death, or form and matter) exist potentially within one another.</note>: which is why she said that her own hand is not able to open it, but only to entrust it.

It also signifies that there are two sorts of waters: the lower ones beneath the firmament <note>The "firmament" refers to the celestial sphere or the sky; lower waters represent material, sensory knowledge.</note> which blind, and the higher ones above the firmament which illuminate: those which are signified by the <term>Pythagoreans</term> and <term>Platonists</term> in the descent from one tropic, and the ascent from another <note>Ancient philosophers believed souls entered the world through the "Gate of Cancer" (one tropic) and exited/ascended through the "Gate of Capricorn" (the other tropic).</note>. There where it says: **THROUGH THE BREADTH, AND THROUGH THE DEPTH** wander the world, seek all the numerous kingdoms; it signifies that there is no progress <note>Suggesting that wandering through the material world is a horizontal movement that does not lead to spiritual "ascent" or true progress of the soul.</note>.

<summary>This page provides an allegorical interpretation of the myth of Circe, identifying her as "matter" which blinds the soul through the process of physical birth (generation), and discusses the soul's struggle to regain its spiritual "wings."</summary>
<keywords>Circe, matter, generation and corruption, Lethe, soul's wings, firmament, Pythagoreans, Platonists, tropics of the soul</keywords>

### Heroic Frenzies.

immediately from one contrary form to another, nor an immediate return from one form to the same one; therefore, it is necessary to pass through—if not all the forms that exist within the wheel of natural species—certainly many and many of them. There, they are understood to be illuminated by the sight of the object, in which the **triad** <note>original: "ternario." A group of three. Bruno refers to the Neoplatonic idea that perfection consists of the union of Beauty, Wisdom, and Truth.</note> of perfections meets—which are **beauty**, **wisdom**, and **truth**—through the sprinkling of the waters which in the sacred books are called waters of wisdom, and rivers of the water of eternal life. These are not found on the world’s continent, but **ENTIRELY DIVIDED FROM THE WHOLE WORLD** <note>original: "PÆNITVS TOTO DIVISIM AB ORBE." This is a variation of a line from Virgil’s first *Eclogue* describing Britain, used here by Bruno to describe the transcendent realm of the divine.</note>, in the bosom of the Ocean, of the **Amphitrite** <note>In Greek mythology, Amphitrite is the goddess of the sea. Here, Bruno uses her as a metaphor for the vast, infinite "ocean" of the divine substance.</note> of divinity, where there is that river which appeared in revelation proceeding from the divine throne, which has a flow other than the ordinary natural one.

There are the **Nymphs**, that is, the blessed and divine **intelligences** <note>In Renaissance philosophy, "intelligences" were spiritual beings or angels that moved the celestial spheres and acted as mediators between God and the world.</note> that assist and minister to the first intelligence, which is like **Diana** among the nymphs of the deserts. She alone among all the others is, by her triple power <note>Likely referring to Diana’s three aspects: Luna in the heavens, Diana on earth, and Hecate in the underworld.</note>, able to open every seal, to loosen every knot, to uncover every secret, and to unlock whatever thing is enclosed. She, with her presence alone and the twin splendor of the good and the true, of goodness and beauty, satisfies all wills and all intellects: sprinkling them with the life-giving waters of purification. Here follows the song and sound, where there are nine intelligences, nine **muses**, according to the order of the **nine spheres** <note>The traditional Aristotelian/Ptolemaic model of the universe consisted of nine concentric celestial spheres (the Moon, Mercury, Venus, the Sun, Mars, Jupiter, Saturn, the Fixed Stars, and the Primum Mobile).</note>; where first the harmony of each is contemplated, which is continued with the

<meta>This page continues the Argument (Preface) of the *Heroic Frenzies*. It follows the discussion of how the soul passes through various forms on its journey toward the divine, eventually reaching a state of cosmic harmony.</meta>

### Argument concerning the...

harmony of the other; because the end and furthest limit of the higher is the beginning and head of the lower, so that there is no middle ground or vacuum between one and the other. And the furthest part of the last, by way of circulation, coincides with the beginning of the first. This is because the same thing is both most clear and most hidden, beginning and end, highest light and deepest abyss, infinite **power** and infinite **act** <note>In philosophy, "power" (or potentiality) is the capacity to become something, while "act" (or actuality) is the fulfillment of that capacity. Bruno argues that in God, these are the same thing.</note>, according to the reasons and methods explained by us in other places. 

Next, one contemplates the harmony and consonance of all the spheres, **intelligences** <note>Spiritual beings thought to move the celestial bodies.</note>, muses, and instruments together: where the heaven, the motion of the worlds, the works of nature, the discourse of intellects, the contemplation of the mind, and the decree of divine providence, all in accord celebrate the high and magnificent **vicissitude** <term>vicissitude: the constant, orderly cycle of change where things turn into their opposites (e.g., day into night, life into death)</term> that equals the lower waters to the higher, changes night with day, and day with night, to the end that divinity may be in all things, in the manner in which everything is capable of everything, and infinite goodness communicates itself infinitely according to the full capacity of all things. 

These are those discourses which have seemed to no one more fitting to be addressed and recommended than to you, excellent lord <note>Bruno dedicated this work to Sir Philip Sidney, the English poet and courtier, whom he met during his stay in London.</note>; so that I do not happen to do—as I think I have done sometimes through lack of attention, and as many others do almost habitually—like one who presents a **lyre** to a deaf man, and a **mirror** to a blind man. To you, then, they are presented, so that the Italian may reason with one who understands him.

# Heroic Frenzies.

The verses shall be under the judgment and protection of a poet. Philosophy shall show herself naked to an intellect as clear as yours. Heroic matters shall be directed toward a heroic and generous soul, such as you show yourself to be gifted with. Duties are offered to a subject so welcome, and homage to a lord as worthy as you have always proven yourself to be. And in my own particular case, I perceive that you have anticipated me in kindness with greater magnanimity than others have followed me with gratitude. FAREWELL <note>original Latin: "VALE" — a traditional closing for letters and formal addresses.</note>

### *A Notice to the Readers.*

Friend reader, it occurs to me—in order to forestall the strictness of anyone who might wish that three of the sonnets found in the first dialogue of the second part of the *Heroic Frenzies* were similar in form to the others in that same dialogue—I would like you to be pleased to add to all three their **concluding refrains** <term>tornelli: concluding couplets or short stanzas added to a poem to provide a final resolution or summary.</term>. To the one that begins: **THAT WHICH MY HEART**, add at the end:

->Thus let it be said of me:<-
->This man, now that he has fixed his eyes upon the sun,<-
->Laments that he was once a rival to **Endymion** <note>In Greek mythology, Endymion was a beautiful youth who was granted eternal sleep so that the Moon (Cynthia) could gaze upon him forever. Bruno uses this to contrast the "sleep" of earthly beauty with the "wakefulness" of the divine sun.</note>.<-

<meta>This page marks the transition from the technical "Printing Errors" list found on the previous page to the formal opening of the literary work. # The Nolan’s Apology.
<note>The "Nolan" is the name Giordano Bruno (1548–1600) used for himself, referring to his birthplace of Nola, Italy.</note>

### To the most virtuous and graceful ladies.

**D**el' England, O lovely and beautiful Nymphs,
Our spirit does not hold you in loathing or disdain,
Nor does its style strive to disparage you,
Even if it is not fitting to call you "women."
Neither to count you among, nor except you from those [others],
I am certain that it is right for me to call you goddesses,
Since the common influence of mortality does not reign in you,
And you are on earth what the stars are in heaven.
Of you, O Ladies, the sovereign beauty
Our rigor neither can nor wishes to bite,
For it does not take aim at a species that is superhuman.
Far from here such arsenic <note>Used here metaphorically to mean poisonous or malicious speech.</note> flies away,
Where one perceives the unique Diana,
Who is among you what the sun is among the stars.
The wit, the words,
And my (whatever it may be) marking of papers
Will make my study and art subservient to you.

---

<summary>A dedicatory sonnet where Giordano Bruno (the Nolan) addresses the noblewomen of the English court, exempting them from his general criticisms of women by elevating them to the status of goddesses and "nymphs."</summary>

1. It is to be believed that he rejected them many times and for many reasons, among which could be these. First, because as a <term>priest of the Muses<note>Bruno views the philosopher as a sacred servant of the arts and divine knowledge.</note></term> should, he could not be idle; for idleness cannot be found where one fights against the ministers and servants of envy, ignorance, and malice. <meta>This page continues the prose commentary following the opening poem of Giordano Bruno's *The Heroic Frenzies*. The text explains why the author previously resisted the Muses and now turns to them, while beginning a discussion on the symbolic meaning of poetic crowns.</meta>

A,2.
<meta>catchword: putting-</meta>

## First Dialogue.

Furthermore, because he felt obligated to the contemplation and study of philosophy; which, if they are not more mature, must nonetheless, as relatives of the Muses, be their predecessors. Moreover, because while the tragic <term>Melpomene<note>The Muse of Tragedy.</note></term> pulled him from one side with more substance than inspiration, and the comic <term>Thalia<note>The Muse of Comedy.</note></term> with more inspiration than substance from the other, it happened that as one stole him from the other, he remained in the middle, neutral and idle, rather than being usefully occupied. Finally, because of the authority of the censors who, by holding him back from more worthy and lofty things toward which he was naturally inclined, held his genius captive; so that from being free under virtue, they might render him captive under a most vile and foolish hypocrisy. At last, in the greatest fervor of the troubles he encountered, it happened that, having no other source of consolation, he accepted the invitation of those who are said to inebriate him with such frenzies, verses, and rhymes, the likes of which they have not shown to others; for in this work, the light of invention shines more brightly than that of imitation.

**CIC.** Tell me, what does he mean by those who boast of myrtles and laurels? 

**TAN.** Those who sing of love boast—and have the right to boast—of the myrtle; to them (if they conduct themselves nobly) belongs the crown of that plant consecrated to **Venus**, from whom they acknowledge their frenzy. Those who worthily sing of heroic things may boast of laurels, as they instruct heroic minds through speculative and moral philosophy, or rather by celebrating and [praising them...]

<summary>Tansillo explains that the author's shift toward poetry was a refuge from professional and personal censorship, moving from "imitation" to "invention." He begins to define the symbolic hierarchy of poetic crowns, linking myrtle to love and laurel to heroic philosophy.</summary>
<keywords>Melpomene, Thalia, censorship, hypocrisy, poetic inspiration, myrtle, laurel, Venus, speculative philosophy, moral philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 42 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Cicada and Tansillo. The previous page ended mid-sentence while discussing how heroic poets instruct the mind through speculative and practical actions.</meta>

...and practical actions, setting them as an exemplary mirror for political and civil deeds.

**CIC.** So then, are there more species of poets and crowns?

**TAN.** Not only as many as there are **Muses**, but a far greater number still; for although there may be certain general categories, the specific types and modes of human genius cannot be strictly defined.

**CIC.** There are certain pedants of poetic rules who barely accept **Homer** as a poet; they relegate **Virgil**, **Ovid**, **Martial**, **Hesiod**, **Lucretius**, and many others to the rank of mere versifiers, judging them by the rules of **Aristotle's** *Poetics*. <note>In the Renaissance, many critics used Aristotle's *Poetics* as a rigid set of laws. Bruno, through Tansillo, argues that these rules are descriptive of past poetry, not prescriptive for all future creativity.</note>

**TAN.** Know for certain, my brother, that these men are true beasts. They fail to consider that those rules serve primarily as a portrait of **Homeric** poetry or some other similar work in particular; they are meant to illustrate a heroic poet such as **Homer** was, rather than to legislate for others who might possess different inspirations, arts, and <term>frenzies<note>Original: *furori*. Referring to the concept of "divine madness" or poetic inspiration that transcends rational rules.</note></term> that are equal, similar, or even greater, born of diverse geniuses.

**CIC.** So then, just as **Homer** in his own genre was not a poet who depended on rules, he is instead the source of the rules that serve those better suited to imitation than to invention. These rules were gathered by one who was not a poet of any sort <note>A critique of Aristotle, who wrote about poetry as a philosopher/logician rather than a practitioner.</note>, but who knew how to compile the rules of that specific kind—namely, **Homeric** poetry—for the benefit of someone wishing to become not an original poet, but a facsimile of **Homer**; one who possesses not his own Muse, but is a mere ape of another’s Muse.

**TAN.** <meta>catchword: Conclud-</meta>

<summary>Tansillo and Cicada discuss the nature of poetic genius, arguing that rules of poetry (like Aristotle's) are derived from great poets after the fact and should not be used to limit new, original "frenzies" or inspirations.</summary>
<keywords>Homer, Aristotle, Poetics, divine frenzy, imitation vs. invention, Muses, literary criticism, Giordano Bruno</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Cicada (CIC) and Tansillo (TAN). The previous page ended with Tansillo explaining that Aristotle's rules were derived from existing poetry rather than being the source of poetic inspiration itself.</meta>

->### First Dialogue<-

...well, that poetry does not arise from rules, except by the slightest accident; rather, the rules derive from the poetry. Therefore, there are as many genres and species of true rules as there are genres and species of true poets.

**CIC.** Now how, then, shall true poets be recognized?

**TAN.** By the singing of their verses—provided that through their singing, they either bring delight, or bring benefit, or bring both benefit and delight together. <note>Tansillo is referencing the classical ideal from the Roman poet Horace, who argued that the best poetry should both "prodesse" (be useful/instructive) and "delectare" (be delightful).</note>

**CIC.** For whom, then, are **Aristotle’s** rules useful?

**TAN.** For those who could not poetize like **Homer**, **Hesiod**, **Orpheus**, and others without **Aristotle’s** rules; and who, having no **Muse** of their own, wish to "make love" to the Muse of **Homer**.

**CIC.** Then those wretched pedants <note>The original *pedantacci* is a pejorative term for narrow-minded teachers or critics who care more for formal rules than for the spirit of a work.</note> of our times are wrong, who exclude certain people from the ranks of poets either because they do not use conformable fables and metaphors, or because they do not begin their books and cantos in the same way as **Homer** and **Virgil**, or because they do not observe the custom of the invocation <note>The traditional opening of an epic poem where the poet calls upon a Muse or deity for inspiration.</note>, or because they weave one story or fable into another, or because they end their cantos by summarizing what was said and proposing what is to be said—and for a thousand other methods of scrutiny, censures, and rules based on that text <note>Aristotle's *Poetics*.</note>. It seems they want to conclude that they themselves, if the <term>whim<note>Original: *fantaſia*. Here meaning a creative spark or imaginative impulse.</note></term> took them, would be the true poets and reach the heights where these others struggle; and yet, in fact, they are nothing more than

A.3.
<meta>catchword: worms</meta>

<summary>Tansillo argues that poetic rules are derived from great poems, not the other way around, asserting that every true poet creates their own rules. He and Cicada critique "pedants" who use Aristotle's rules as a rigid checklist to dismiss original creative works.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Cicada (CIC) and Tansillo (TAN) from Giordano Bruno’s "The Heroic Frenzies." Tansillo is concluding his scathing critique of "pedants"—critics who value rigid rules over actual poetic genius.</meta>

...worms who do not know how to do anything good, but are born only to gnaw, defile, and manure upon the studies and labors of others; and being unable to make themselves famous through their own virtue and talent, they seek to push themselves forward, rightly or wrongly, through the faults and errors of others. 

**TAN.** Now, to return to the point from which our passion caused us to digress at such length: I say that there are, and can be, as many kinds of poets as there are types of human sentiments and inventions. To these poets, it is possible to adapt garlands made not only from every genus and species of plant, but even from other kinds and species of materials. Therefore, crowns for poets are not made only of myrtles and laurels, but also of vine leaves for **Fescennine** verses <note>original: "versi Fescennini" — ancient Roman verses characterized by ribaldry, improvised humor, and often performed at weddings or festivals</note>, of ivy for **Bacchanals** <note>Festivals held in honor of Bacchus, the god of wine and ecstasy</note>, of olive for sacrifices and laws; of poplar, elm, and stalks of grain for agriculture; of cypress for funerals; and of countless others for as many other occasions. And if you like, one might even use that material which a certain gentleman described when he said:

> O Brother Porro, poet of blunders,
> Who in **Milan** fastens upon yourself a garland
> Of blood puddings, tripe, and sausages.
<note>Tansillo quotes a satirical poem mocking a mediocre poet named Porro. Instead of laurel leaves, he is crowned with various Milanese meats: *boldoni* (blood puddings), *busecche* (tripe), and *cervellate* (a type of Milanese sausage).</note>

**CIC.** Now then, surely this man—through the various "veins" or styles he displays for different purposes and meanings—will be able to deck himself in the branches of various plants, and will be able to speak worthily with the **Muses**; so that his inspiration <note>original: "aura" — literally a breeze or breath, often used poetically to mean divine inspiration or the "breath" of the Muses</note> may be with them, with

<meta>catchword: cui (whom/which)</meta>

<summary>Tansillo argues that poetic genres are as diverse as human thought, suggesting that different "garlands" (symbols of honor) belong to different types of poetry, ranging from the sacred to the satirical. # First Dialogue

->Through such mountains, through such nymphs, and waters,<-

---

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut divider featuring symmetrical scrolling floral and vine motifs.</image-desc>

1 Here he declares, first, what his mountain is, saying it is the high affection <note>original: "affetto" — in this context, a deep, driving emotional and spiritual impulse</note> of his heart; second, who his muses are, saying they are the beauties and prerogatives of his object <note>The "object" refers to the ultimate goal of the philosopher’s desire, namely divine truth or universal beauty</note>; third, what the springs are, and these he says are his tears. In that mountain, affection is kindled. From those beauties, frenzy <note>original: "furore" — meaning "heroic frenzy," a state of divine inspiration and intellectual ecstasy</note> is conceived. And through those tears, the frenzied affection is demonstrated. 

2. Thus he considers that he can be no less illustriously crowned by way of his heart, thoughts, and tears, than others are by the hands of Kings, Emperors, and Popes. 

**CICADA.** Explain to me what he means by saying the heart is in the form of **Parnassus** <note>A mountain in Greece with two summits, sacred to Apollo and the Muses; the traditional home of poetry</note>.

**TANSILLO.** Because just as the human heart has two "heads" <note>The anatomical ventricles of the heart</note> that end at a single root, and spiritually from a single affection of the heart proceed the hatred and love of two contraries; so **Mount Parnassus** has a single base under two peaks. 

**CICADA.** To the next part. 

**TANSILLO.** He says:

<meta>catchword: Chia- (Italian "Chiare" or "Clarify")</meta>

<summary>Tansillo explains that true poetic and spiritual honors come from the internal struggle of the heart rather than external authorities. He draws a symbolic parallel between the two-peaked Mount Parnassus and the dual nature of the human heart, which experiences both love and hate from a single source.</summary>

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut horizontal band similar to the first, with stylized floral patterns.</image-desc>

<margin>1</margin> This captain is the human will <term>voluntade humana: the faculty of choice and desire</term> who sits at the stern of the soul, governing the affects <note>original: "affetti" — the emotions or dispositions of the soul</note> of certain internal powers with a small rudder of reason <term>raggione: the intellectual faculty that guides the will</term>, against the waves of natural impulses. He, with the sound of the trumpet, that is, of determined choice <term>original: "elettione" — the deliberate act of selecting a path or object of desire</term>; calls all the warriors, that is, provokes all the powers (which are called warriors because they are in continuous opposition and conflict), or rather the effects of those, which are the contrary thoughts; of which some incline toward one side, others toward the other: and he seeks to establish them all under one banner of a single object.

<summary>Tansillo uses the metaphor of a military captain to describe how the human will uses reason to organize conflicting thoughts and internal "powers" toward a single, unified spiritual goal. The soul demands total devotion to this one "object" of beauty, rejecting any fragmented or wandering desires.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Tansillo’s explanation of the sonnet from the previous page. Having established the "Captain" as the human Will, he now describes how the Will manages unruly impulses and directs the soul toward a single, divine object of love.</meta>

# First Dialogue

...determined end. Where it happens that some of them are called in vain to promptly show themselves obedient: (especially those proceeding from natural powers <term>potenze naturali: biological or instinctual drives</term> which obey reason little or not at all) at least by forcing himself to hinder their acts; and condemning those that cannot be hindered, he comes to show himself as if he killed those, and banished these: proceeding against some with the sword of wrath <term>ira: here, a disciplined or corrective anger</term>, and others with the whip of disdain.

<margin>2</margin> Here he gazes upon one object toward which he is turned with intention. By one face which satisfies him, the mind is occupied. In a single beauty he delights and finds pleasure, and is said to remain fixed there, because the work of intelligence <term>intelligenza: the highest faculty of the soul that perceives truth</term> is not an operation of motion, but of rest <note>Bruno follows the classical idea that while the *search* for knowledge involves movement, the actual state of *understanding* is a state of stillness or "rest" in the truth.</note>. And from there alone he conceives that dart which kills him, that is, which constitutes for him the ultimate end of perfection. He burns by one fire alone, that is, he is sweetly consumed in one love.

**CICADA.** Why is love symbolized by fire?

**TANSILLO.** Leaving aside many other reasons, let this one suffice for now: because just as love converts the beloved thing into the lover; so fire, being the most active among all the elements, is powerful enough to convert all other simple and composite <note>original: "ſemplici et compoſti" — referring to the basic elements and the physical objects made from their combinations</note> things into itself.

**CICADA.** Now continue.

**TANSILLO.** He knows one paradise: that is, one principal end, because "paradise" commonly signifies the end <note>In this philosophical context, "the end" refers to the *final cause* or the ultimate goal toward which a soul strives.</note>, which is distinguished into that which is absolute, in truth, and essence; and the other which is in likeness, shadow, and participation. Of the first kind there cannot be more than one, as—

<summary>Tansillo explains how the Will directs the soul's conflicting emotions toward a single divine object, comparing the transformative power of love to the element of fire and defining "paradise" as the soul's ultimate goal.</summary>

...to which he is subject by his destiny. The **Object** <term>oggetto: in this philosophical context, the "Object" refers to the target of the soul's desire, specifically the Divine or the ultimate Truth</term> is the lovable thing, and the correlative of the **Lover**. It is clear that **Jealousy** is a zeal of the lover concerning the loved thing; it is unnecessary to explain this to anyone who has tasted love, and it would be in vain for us to try to clarify it to others. **Love** satisfies; because to the one who loves, loving is itself pleasing; and he who truly loves would not wish *not* to love. Therefore, I do not want to fail to recount what I expressed in this sonnet of mine:

> Dear, sweet, and honored wound
> From the most beautiful dart that Love ever chose;
> High, graceful, and precious heat,
> That makes the soul wander, always eager to burn.
> What power of herb, or virtue of magic art
> Will ever remove you from the center of my heart;
> If the one who grants you fresh vigor at every hour,
> The more they torment me, the more they satisfy me?

> When will I ever go unburdened of your weight,
> If the remedy is a nuisance to me, and the illness a delight?
