--- Page 52 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from the previous page regarding the destructive nature of Jealousy and begins a new inquiry into the nature of Love as a "putto irrationale" (irrational boy). It bridges the transition from Jealousy's effect on the lover to the varying effects of Love on different types of human intellects.</meta>

# First Dialogue

Add to what has been said that **Jealousy** is not only sometimes the death and ruin of the lover, but often kills love itself, especially when it gives birth to **disdain** <note>original: "sdegno" — a sense of indignant scorn or resentment that arises when a lover feels mistreated</note>: for love becomes so affected by its own offspring <note>The "offspring" refers to the disdain born from jealousy</note> that it pushes love away and brings the object into contempt; indeed, it makes it no longer an object of desire.

**CIC.** Now explain the other parts that follow: that is, why is love called an **irrational boy**?

**TAN.** I will tell you everything. **IRRATIONAL BOY** love is called, not because it is such in itself, but because for the most part it makes its subjects so, and is found in such subjects. Considering that in anyone who is more intellectual and speculative, it raises the wit higher and purifies the intellect more—making it alert, studious, and circumspect, promoting it to a **heroic courage** <term>animositate heroica: a spirited, noble bravery that drives the soul toward divine heights or great deeds</term>, and an emulation of virtues and greatness; through the desire to please and make oneself worthy of the beloved thing. In others then (who are the majority), it is understood as mad and foolish, because it makes them lose their senses <note>original: "vscir de proprij sentimenti" — to go out of one's mind or lose rational control</note> and plunges them into extravagances, because it finds the spirit, soul, and body ill-disposed and unfit to consider and distinguish what is decent for them from what makes them more unseemly; making them subjects of contempt, laughter, and shame.

**CIC.** People commonly say, as a proverb, that love makes old men go mad and young men become wise.

**TAN.** This misfortune does not happen to all old men, nor does that benefit happen to all young—

<meta>catchword: men (uani/giouani)</meta>

<summary>Tansillo explains how Jealousy can transform into Disdain, destroying love itself, and argues that while Love is called an "irrational boy," it actually ennobles intellectual souls while driving baser minds to madness.</summary>
<keywords>Jealousy, Disdain, Irrational Boy, Heroic Courage, Intellect, Madness, Wisdom, Proverbs</keywords>

--- Page 53 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Cicada and Tansillo, moving from a discussion of how love affects different temperaments to an exploration of the nature of Fortune and why the lover views the beloved as a superior force that both ennobles and enslaves him.</meta>

...vainly: but it is true of those of good temperament, just as these others are of bad temperament. And by this it is certain that whoever is accustomed in youth to love circumspectly will love as an old man without going astray. But the amusement and laughter belong to those into whose hands love places the alphabet in their mature age <note>To "place the alphabet in one's hand" is a metaphor for being a novice or a beginner; it implies that older men who suddenly fall in love are behaving like foolish schoolboys learning their first lessons</note>. **CIC.** Tell me now, why is fortune or fate called blind and wicked?

**TAN.** **BLIND** and **WICKED** fortune is also called, not in itself—for it is the very order of numbers and measures of the universe—but in respect to its subjects, it is called and is blind; because it makes them blind in regard to it, since it is most uncertain to them. It is similarly called wicked because there is no mortal who, in some manner of lamenting and complaining about it, does not blame it. Hence the Apulian poet <note>original: "Puglese poeta" — refers to the Roman poet Horace, who was born in the region of Apulia</note> said:

> How comes it, Maecenas, that no one
> In the world appears content with the lot
> That either reason has given him, or heaven?
<note>original: "Che vuol dir Mecenate..." — This is a translation of the opening lines of Horace’s Satires (1.1.1), questioning why people are never satisfied with their own path in life</note>

So he calls the object **HIGH BEAUTY**; because to him it is unique, most eminent, and effective in drawing him to itself. Therefore, he esteems it more worthy and noble, and thus feels it to be predominant and superior, just as he himself is made its subject and **captive** <term>cattiuo: used here in the classical sense of "captive" or "prisoner" (from the Latin *captivus*), describing the lover's surrender to the beloved</term>. **MY DEATH ALONE** he says of **Jealousy**, because just as love has no closer companion than her, so too does it have no sense of a greater enemy; just as nothing is more an enemy to iron than rust, which is born from the iron itself.

<summary>Tansillo explains that Fortune is only perceived as "blind" or "wicked" because of human uncertainty and dissatisfaction, and concludes by describing how Jealousy acts as an internal rust that destroys Love from within.</summary>
<keywords>Fortune, Fate, Horace, Maecenas, High Beauty, Captivity of Love, Jealousy, Rust of Love, Temperament</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Cicada and Tansillo, focusing on the defense of Love against the common accusation of blindness. Tansillo argues that Love is actually a source of supreme intellectual clarity, transitioning from the previous page's discussion on Fortune to the ennobling power of the "Heroic Frenzy."</meta>

### First Dialogue.

**CICADA.** Now that you have begun to do so, continue to show part by part what remains. **TANSILLO.** So I shall do. It says next of love: **HE SHOWS ME PARADISE**; whereby he shows that love is not blind in itself, and of itself does not make lovers blind, but rather it is the result of the lowly dispositions of the subject; just as it happens that nocturnal birds become blind in the presence of the sun. As for itself, therefore, love illuminates, clarifies, opens the intellect, and makes one penetrate the whole, and rouses miraculous effects. **CICADA.** It seems to me that the Nolan <note>original: "il Nolano" — This is how Giordano Bruno refers to himself, as he was born in Nola, near Naples</note> demonstrates this very well in another of his sonnets.

---

Love, through whom I discern the truth so high,
Who opens the gates of black diamond,
Through the eyes my deity enters, and through see-
<margin>ing</margin>
He is born, lives, is nourished, and holds eternal reign;
He makes one see all that heaven, earth, and hell contain;
He makes true images of those who are absent present;
He regains strength, and with a straight aim, he strikes;
And always wounds the heart, uncovering the inner self.
O then, base crowd <note>original: "volgo vile" — Bruno uses this term not just for the poor, but for those lacking intellectual or spiritual refinement</note>, pay heed unto the truth,
Lend your ear to my words, which are not false,
Open, open your eyes if you can, you insane and squint-
<margin>eyed</margin>:
You believe him a child because you understand so little,
Because you change so quickly, he seems fleeting to you,
Because you are sightless, you call him blind.

---

Love, then, shows paradise, in order to make one understand, comprehend, and effect the highest things:

<meta>This page concludes the philosophical resolution of the speaker's inner conflict. Having previously identified the forces of Love, Fate, and Jealousy as distinct tormentors, the text now moves toward a unification of these concepts, suggesting they are all different expressions of the same spiritual experience.</meta>

### First Dialogue.

...turn your indignation elsewhere. And go away, far out of the world, you Jealousy: for either of those two others who remain can fulfill your roles and duties—if, indeed, you, my Fate, are nothing other than my Love, and you, Jealousy, are not foreign to the <term>substance</term> <note>original: "sustanza"; in Renaissance philosophy, this refers to the underlying essence of a thing that remains constant beneath its outward changes</note> of that very same thing. Let him <note>Love</note> remain then to deprive me of life, to burn me, to deal me death, and to be the weight upon my bones; on the condition that she <note>Fate/Fortune</note> might rescue me from death, give me wings, revive me, and sustain me.

Next, he reduces two principles and one opposition <note>original: "contrarietade"</note> to a single principle and a single power <note>original: "efficacia"</note>. He says: **BUT WHAT DO I SAY OF LOVE?** If this face, this object, is his empire, and appears to be nothing else but the dominion of love; if the rule of love is its very own rule; if the impression of love that appears in the substance of my heart is certainly no other impression than hers <note>The beloved's face</note>—why then, after having said **NOBLE FACE**, do I repeat by saying **BEAUTIFUL LOVE**?

->End of the First Dialogue.<-

> -> I love another, I hate myself, <-

<image-desc>A horizontal typographic ornament featuring symmetrical floral and scrollwork patterns.</image-desc>

Regarding this, I want to follow up on what I was telling you a little while ago: that it is not necessary to labor to prove what is so clearly seen—namely, that nothing is pure and simple <note>In this philosophical context, "simple" means unmixed or existing in its most basic, singular state.</note>. (Wherefore some would say that no composite thing is a true being <term>ente: a philosophical term for a "being" or "entity" in its essential form</term>, just as mixed gold is not true gold, and mixed wine is not pure, true, and unadulterated wine.) Furthermore, all things consist of contraries <note>The Renaissance philosophical idea that the universe is held in balance by opposing forces (hot/cold, love/hate, etc.).</note>, from which it follows that the outcomes of our emotions <note>original: "affetti"; the internal states or passions that move the soul</note>, because of the composition found in all things, never possess any delight without some bitterness. Rather, I say, and note further, that if there were no bitterness in things, there would be no delight, considering that labor makes us find delight in rest; separation is the cause of us finding pleasure in union; and examining things generally, one will always find that one contrary is the reason why the other contrary is desired and gives pleasure.

**CICADA.** There is, then, no delight without contrariety.

**TANSILLO.** Certainly not, just as without contrari-
-> ety <-

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of human feeling and the inherent bitterness found in all mixed things. Tansillo is explaining that pure joy is only possible in a state of ignorance or madness, whereas the "heroic" soul is defined by its awareness of opposites and future uncertainties.</meta>

### Second Dialogue.

[...a state which] is not without pain, as that Pythagorean Poet expresses when he says:
> "Hence they fear and desire, they grieve and rejoice, nor do they look back to the light, shut up in darkness and a blind prison." <note>original: "Hinc metuūt, cupiuntq; dolent, gaudētq;, nec Respiciunt, clausæ tenebris, et carcere cæco." From Virgil’s Aeneid, Book VI. Bruno calls Virgil a "Pythagorean Poet" because these verses describe the soul's entrapment in the body, a key Pythagorean and Neoplatonic concept.</note>

Here, then, is what the composition of things causes. From this it happens that no one is satisfied with their state except for some senseless fool, and all the more so the further they find themselves in the deep interval of their madness; then they have little or no perception of their own ill; they enjoy the present without fear of the future; they rejoice in what is and in the state they are in, and have no remorse or concern for what is or might be; and finally, they have no sense of the <term>contrariety: the state of being made of opposing forces or qualities (like hot and cold, or joy and pain)</term> which is symbolized by the Tree of the Knowledge of Good and Evil. **C I.** <note>CI refers to Cicada, one of the speakers in the dialogue.</note> From this, we see that ignorance is the mother of sensual happiness and bliss, and this very state is the garden of paradise for animals; as is made clear in the dialogues on the *Cabal of the Horse Pegasus* <note>A reference to another work by Giordano Bruno, *Cabala del cavallo pegaseo* (1585), which satirizes academic pedantry and praises a sort of "holy ignorance."</note>, and by what the wise Solomon says: "he who increases wisdom, increases sorrow" <note>Ecclesiastes 1:18</note>. **T A.** <note>TA refers to Tansillo, the primary speaker.</note> From this it follows that heroic love is a torment, because it does not enjoy the present like a beastly love does, but it feels ambition, emulation, suspicion, and fear regarding the future, the absent, and the contrary. Hence, when one of our neighbors said one evening after dinner: "I have never been so happy as I am now," Giovanni Bruno, the father of the Nolan <term>The Nolan: Giordano Bruno's nickname for himself, as he was from the town of Nola</term>, replied: "You were never more foolish than you are now." **C I C.** <note>CIC refers to Cicada.</note> Do you wish, then, that he who is

--- Page 61 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Tansillo and Cicada. Tansillo is further refining the definition of the "heroic" or wise soul, moving away from the idea that either joy or sorrow constitutes wisdom. He introduces the Stoic-like concept of the sage who remains unmoved by the "vicissitudes" of the material world.</meta>

### Second Dialogue.

[...is the] sad man wise, and that other who is even sadder even wiser? **T A N.** No, on the contrary, I mean that in these people there is another kind of madness, and an even worse one at that. **C I C.** Who then shall be wise, if he who is content is mad, and he who is sad is mad? **T A N.** He who is neither content nor sad. **C I C.** Who? One who sleeps? One who is deprived of feeling? One who is dead? **T A N.** No. But he who is alive, awake, and understands; who, by considering both evil and good, and judging both to be variable things consisting in motion, change, and <term>vicissitude: the natural change or succession of one thing to another</term> (so that the end of one contrary is the beginning of the other, and the extreme of one is the start of the other), does not lose heart, nor is his spirit puffed up. He becomes continent in his inclinations and temperate in his pleasures; for to him, pleasure is not pleasure, because he holds its inevitable end as if it were already present. Likewise, pain is not pain to him, because through the power of reflection he keeps the end of that pain before him. Thus, the wise man regards all mutable things as things that do not truly exist, and affirms them to be nothing but vanity and a nothing: because time relates to eternity as a point relates to a line. <note>This geometric analogy suggests that just as a mathematical point has no dimension compared to a line, a single moment or even a human lifetime is infinitesimal and "nothing" when measured against the scale of eternity.</note> **C I.** So then we can never intend to be content or dissatisfied without also intending our own madness—which we then expressly confess. Therefore, no one who reasons about these states, and consequently no one who experiences them, will be wise; and in the end, all men are mad.

<sig>B.4.</sig>
<meta>catchword: TAN.</meta>

<summary>Tansillo defines the wise man as one who remains temperate and indifferent to the fluctuations of pleasure and pain, viewing them as fleeting "vanities" compared to eternity. Cicada concludes that if joy and sorrow are forms of madness, then all of humanity must be considered mad.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion between Tansillo and Cicada regarding the nature of the wise or "heroic" soul. Tansillo is arguing that wisdom lies in a state of emotional neutrality or indifference, rather than in the extremes of joy or sorrow. He utilizes the Aristotelian concept of virtue as a middle point between two opposing vices.</meta>

### Second Dialogue.

**TAN.** I do not intend to imply this, for I would call him most wise who could truly say, at times, the opposite of what that other man said: "I was never less happy than now"; (or) "I was never less sad than now."

**CI.** How can you not see two contrary qualities where there are two contrary <term>affections: internal emotional states or passions</term>? Why do you say we should understand them as two virtues, rather than one vice and one virtue—this being "minimally happy" and "minimally sad"?

**TANS.** Because both of these contraries, when in excess (that is, insofar as they tend toward the "more"), are vices, because they cross the line <note>This "line" refers to the classical concept of the "Golden Mean," the ideal moderate middle between two extremes.</note>; and those same things, insofar as they tend toward the "less," become virtue, because they are contained and enclosed within the proper bounds.

**CIC.** So, being less content and being less sad are not one virtue and one vice, but two virtues?

**TAN.** Rather, I say they are one and the same virtue: because vice is found where there is contrariety; contrariety is greatest at the extremes; the greatest opposition is found closest to the extremes; and the least or none at all is found in the middle, where contraries meet and become one and <term>indifferent: a state of neutrality where opposing forces balance each other out</term>. It is like how between the coldest and the hottest points, there is "less hot" and "less cold." At the precise midpoint, you find that which you can call either "hot and cold" or "neither hot nor cold," without any conflict between them. In this way, whoever is minimally content and minimally happy is at the level of indifference, and finds himself in the house of

<meta>catchword: tem- (likely "temperance")</meta>

<summary>Tansillo explains that virtue is found in the "lesser" degrees of emotion, where joy and sadness meet in a state of indifference or moderation, rather than in the extremes of either.</summary>
<keywords>virtue, vice, moderation, indifference, contraries, emotions, Golden Mean</keywords>

<meta>This page completes the sentence from the previous page, where Tansillo defines the "strong spirit" as one that remains balanced between emotional extremes. ### Second Dialogue.

...temperance, and there where virtue and the condition of a strong spirit consist—a spirit that is bent neither by the <note>original: "Austro," the South wind</note> South wind nor by the <note>original: "Aquilone," the North wind</note> North wind.

Here then (to come to the point) is how this **Heroic Frenzy**, which is clarified in the present part, is different from other lower frenzies: not as virtue differs from vice, but as a vice that exists in a more divine subject or in a divine manner differs from a vice that exists in a more beast-like subject or in a beastly manner. Thus, the difference is according to the different subjects and modes, and not according to the form of being a vice itself. <note>Bruno is suggesting that because this "frenzy" is an extreme state, it technically violates the rule of the "middle way," making it a "vice" in a strictly structural sense, but its divine nature makes it superior to ordinary human moderation.</note> **CICADA.** From what you have said, I can very well conclude the condition of this heroic frenzy which says **FROZEN ARE MY HOPES**, and my burning desires: because it does not consist in the temperance of the middle ground, but in the excess of contraries. The soul is discordant; it trembles in frozen hopes, it burns in scalding desires; it is shrieking with greed, and mute with fear; it sparks from the heart out of care for another, and through self-compassion, it pours tears from the eyes; it dies in another's laughter, and lives in its own laments; and (like one who no longer belongs to himself) he loves another and hates himself: because matter (as the <term>physicists: natural philosophers who studied the laws of the physical world</term> say) in the same measure that it loves the absent form, hates the present one. And thus he concludes in the <term>octave: a poetic stanza of eight lines</term> the war that the soul has within itself; and then when he says in the <term>sestina: a poetic stanza of six lines</term> **BUT IF I TAKE WING**, another turns to stone, and that which follows; now

<summary>Tansillo and Cicada discuss "Heroic Frenzy" as a divine form of excess that transcends ordinary virtue by oscillating between extreme emotional states like hope and fear, or love and hate. They compare this psychological state to the physical principle where matter rejects its current form to seek a new one.</summary>

...shows his passions through the war he wages against external contraries. **CICADA.** I remember having read this sentence in Iamblichus, where he treats the Egyptian mysteries: <note>original: "Impius animam dissidentem habet: unde nec secum ipse convenire potest, neque cum alijs."</note> *The impious man has a discordant soul; hence he can neither agree with himself nor with others.* **TANSILLO.** Now hear another sonnet whose meaning follows from what has been said.

Ahi, what condition of nature or fate!
    In living death, a dead life I live.
Love has killed me (alas!) with such a death,
That I am deprived of life and death together.
Empty of hope at the gates of hell,

<meta>This page continues Tansillo’s explanation of the sonnet from the previous page, specifically addressing the "quartering" or internal division of the soul mentioned at the end of the last section.</meta>

### Second Dialogue.

...affection, leaving the middle path and the goal of temperance, tends toward one and the other extreme: and it is so transported on high or to the right, that it also transports itself below and to the left. **CICADA.** How is it that by this state—which belongs specifically to neither one nor the other extreme—the soul does not arrive at a state or term of virtue? **TANSILLO.** It is in a state of virtue when it holds to the middle, turning away from one and the other contrary; but when it tends toward the extremes, leaning toward both of them, it falls so far short of being virtue that it becomes a double vice. This vice consists in the fact that the thing recedes from its own nature, the perfection of which consists in unity; and it is only where the contraries agree <note>Tansillo refers to the "coincidence of opposites," a core theme in Bruno's philosophy where opposing forces find a point of reconciliation or "composition."</note> that composition and virtue exist. Behold, then, how he is a dead man living, or a living man dying, wherefore he says: **IN LIVING DEATH**, *a dead life I live.* He is not dead, because he lives in the object <note>The "object" here is the beloved or the divine ideal toward which the lover’s soul is directed.</note> of his love; he is not alive, because he is dead in himself. He is deprived of death because he gives birth to thoughts in that object; he is deprived of life because he does not grow or feel within himself. Furthermore, he is at the lowest point because of his consideration of the high "intelligible" <note>term: intelligible (the world of ideas or divine truths that can only be grasped by the intellect, not the senses)</note> and the realized weakness of his own power. He is at the highest point because of the aspiration of that heroic desire which surpasses his own limits by a great length; and he is at the highest because of the intellectual appetite which knows no limit or end in adding number to number. Yet he is also at the lowest point because of the violence done to him by the sensual contrary, which...

<meta>catchword: which</meta>

<summary>Tansillo explains the lover's paradox: by striving for the divine while still bound to the senses, the soul experiences a "double vice" of being pulled toward opposite extremes, resulting in a state of being simultaneously "dead" to oneself and "alive" in the beloved.</summary>
<keywords>temperance, virtue, heroic desire, coincidence of opposites, intelligible world, intellectual appetite, paradox of love, Tansillo, Cicada</keywords>

--- Page 66 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the soul's internal struggle between the high ("the intelligible") and the low ("the sensible"). The previous page ended with the soul at its highest point, and this page picks up with its descent.</meta>

### Second Dialogue.

...which plummets toward hell: wherefore, finding himself thus climbing and descending, he feels in his soul the greatest dissension that can be felt. And he remains confused by the rebellion of the **senses**, which spur him toward that which **reason** restrains him from, and vice versa.

&nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; The very same is demonstrated in the following sentence where Reason, in the name of Filenio <note>Filenio is a character name used by Bruno to represent the voice of Reason or philosophical inquiry.</note>, asks questions, and the Frenzied One <note>term: Furioso (the "frenzied" lover or seeker who has been transformed by a divine or heroic passion)</note> responds in the name of a **Shepherd**, who toils at the care of the flock or herd of his thoughts; these he grazes in obedience and service to his nymph, who is the affection for that object <note>The "object" refers to the target of the soul's pursuit, typically a divine or intellectual beauty.</note> to whose observance he has been made captive.

### Second Dialogue.

->**F**&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;**P**&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;**F**&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;**P**&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;**F**<-
->Does she promise? No. Does she deny? Nor that either. Does she keep silent?<-
->**P.** Yes, because such great virtue <note>original: *honestà*—this refers to both the moral integrity and the dignified beauty of the beloved, which intimidates the lover</note> takes my daring away.<-

<image-desc>An ornamental woodcut divider with symmetrical floral and scrollwork motifs.</image-desc>

Here he says that he suffers and laments of love—not because he is in love (since to no true lover is the act of loving ever displeasing), but because he loves unhappily. This happens while those arrows go forth, which are the rays of those lights <note>referring to the eyes of the beloved as a source of spiritual or intellectual light</note>; these same rays, depending on whether they are defiant and stubborn, or benign and gracious, become the gates that lead to heaven, or indeed to hell. By this, he is maintained in a state of hope for a future and uncertain reward, while in reality, he faces a present and certain martyrdom. And although he sees his own folly quite clearly, it does not follow that he corrects himself at any point, or that he can even feel displeasure for it; for he is so far from that, that he rather takes pleasure in it, as shown where he says:

> Let it never be that I lament of love,
> Without which I do not wish to be happy.

Next, he shows another species of frenzy <note>term: frenzy (Italian *furore*)—in Bruno's philosophy, this is not "madness" but a divinely inspired passion that drives the soul toward the infinite</note> born from some light of reason, which arouses fear and suppresses the aforementioned frenzy, so that it does not proceed to an act that might embitter

<meta>The catchword "aſpri" indicates the next page begins with "inasprisca" (embitter or sharpen).</meta>

<summary>The soul (the Shepherd) describes the bittersweet agony of unrequited love for a divine object, choosing to remain in a state of "folly" or "frenzy" because even the suffering of such a love is preferable to a happiness without it.</summary>
<keywords>heroic frenzy, Filenio, Shepherd, love's martyrdom, reason vs. passion, eyes as gates to heaven</keywords>

--- Page 68 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue from the previous page, where the speaker (the lover) was describing his silence and fear of offending his beloved. ### Second Dialogue.

...to embitter or offend the beloved. He says, then, that his hope is based on the future, without anything being promised or denied to him; for he remains silent and asks for nothing, fearing that he might offend her honor <note>original: *l'honeftade*—refers to both the moral integrity and the dignified beauty of the beloved</note>. He does not dare to explain himself or make a proposal, lest he be excluded by a rejection or, conversely, accepted with a promise; for in his thoughts, the weight of what might go wrong in one case is heavier than the potential good in the other. Thus, he shows himself willing to suffer his own torment forever rather than open the door to any situation that might trouble or sadden the beloved.

**CI.** With this, he demonstrates that his love is truly heroic <note>original: *amor heroico*—a "heroic" love is one that elevates the human soul toward the divine, distinguishing it from common or "bestial" passion</note>; for he takes as his principal goal the grace of the spirit and the harmony of affection, rather than the beauty of the body, which is not the final destination of a love that possesses a divine quality.

**TA.** You know well that just as the Platonic rapture <note>original: *rapto Platonico*—the soul's ascent toward divine truth through inspiration or "divine madness," as described by Plato</note> is of three kinds—one tending toward the contemplative or speculative life; another toward the active moral life; and another toward the idle and voluptuous life—so there are three kinds of love. Of these, one rises from the sight of physical beauty <note>original: *forma corporale*</note> to the contemplation of the spiritual and divine; another persists only in the pleasure of seeing and conversing; and the third goes from seeing to falling headlong into the lust <term>concupiscence: a strong physical desire or carnal longing</term> of touching. From these three modes, others are composed, such as when the first is joined with the second, or with the third, or when all three modes occur together; of which each and all...

<meta>catchword: all el-</meta>

<summary>Tansillo and Cicada discuss the selflessness of heroic love and categorize the three ways the soul can be "captured" by beauty, ranging from divine contemplation to physical desire.</summary>
<keywords>heroic love, Platonic rapture, contemplative life, active life, physical beauty, spiritual love, concupiscence, Giordano Bruno</keywords>

--- Page 69 ---
<meta>This page continues the speech from the previous page, completing the word "contemplative" (contemplati) and proceeding with the classification of the different types of lovers and their motivations.</meta>

### Second Dialogue.

...tive [ones]. Furthermore, they multiply into others, according to the impulses of those in frenzy <note>original: *furioſi*—referring to the "divine madness" or intense passion of the soul seeking higher truth</note> who tend either more toward the spiritual object, or more toward the corporal object, or equally toward both. Wherefore it happens that among those who find themselves in this warfare <note>original: *militia*—a common Renaissance metaphor for the lover’s struggle or "service" to a lady</note> and are caught in the nets of love, some tend toward the goal of the pleasure taken from gathering the fruit <note>original: *poma*—literally "apples," symbolizing the physical rewards or consummation of love</note> from the tree of physical beauty; without achieving this (or at least the hope of it), they consider every amorous effort to be laughable and vain. And in this way run all those of a barbarian nature, who neither can nor seek to magnify themselves by loving worthy things, aspiring to illustrious things, or higher still, directing their efforts and actions toward divine things—for which there is nothing that can more richly and conveniently provide wings than heroic love <note>original: *l' heroico amore*</note>. Others come forward for the sake of the fruit of delight which they take from the sight of the beauty and grace of the spirit that shines and glows through the elegance of the body; and among these, some, although they love the body and greatly desire to be united with it (complaining of its distance and grieving over their separation from it), nevertheless fear that by presuming too much in this, they might be deprived of that kindness, conversation, friendship, and harmony which is more essential to them; since such an attempt can have no more certainty of a pleasing success than it has great fear of falling from that grace which, as something so glorious and worthy, pours itself before the eyes of their thought.

<summary>This page categorizes lovers based on whether they seek physical gratification or a higher spiritual connection, identifying "heroic love" as the force that provides the soul with wings to reach for the divine.</summary>
<keywords>heroic love, furiosi, physical beauty, spiritual grace, Platonic love, desire, divine madness</keywords>

--- Page 70 ---
<meta>This page concludes the Second Dialogue. It follows the discussion on the nature of heroic love, contrasting the pursuit of spiritual beauty with the trap of base, purely physical attraction.</meta>

### Second Dialogue.

C I C A D A. It is a worthy thing, O Tansillo, because of the many virtues and perfections that derive from it for the human intellect, to seek out, accept, nourish, and preserve such a love. However, one must also take great care not to stumble into binding oneself to an unworthy and base object, so as not to become a participant in that same baseness and indignity. In this regard, I keep in mind the advice of the Ferrarese poet <note>original: *poeta Ferrareſe*—referring to Ludovico Ariosto, the author of *Orlando Furioso*</note>:

> Whoever sets his foot on the amorous snare,
> Should seek to withdraw it, and not entangle his wings;

T A N S I L L O. To tell the truth, an object that possesses no splendor beyond the beauty of the body is not worthy of being loved for any purpose other than to—as they say—produce offspring <note>original: *far la razza*—literally "to make the race" or breed. Tansillo argues that physical attraction alone serves only biological functions, not the soul.</note>. It seems to me the behavior of a pig or a horse to torment oneself over such a thing. For my part, I was never more fascinated by anything of that sort than I could now be fascinated by a statue or a painting, to which I am indifferent. It would therefore be a great disgrace to a noble soul if, of a filthy, vile, and ignoble intellect <note>The OCR reads *bardo*, but the sense implies a base or muddy nature (*fango*)</note>—no matter how excellent a figure it might be hidden under—one were to say: **I FEAR HER DISDAIN MORE THAN MY TORMENT.**

->End of the Second Dialogue.<-

<meta>catchword: Dia- [referring to Dialogo Terzo, the Third Dialogue]</meta>

<summary>Cicada and Tansillo conclude that while heroic love is a noble pursuit for the intellect, one must avoid being ensnared by purely physical beauty, which is compared to a bird-trap. Tansillo dismisses purely carnal passion as fit only for animals and procreation, unworthy of a noble spirit's suffering.</summary>
<keywords>Giordano Bruno, Tansillo, Cicada, Ariosto, heroic love, physical beauty, procreation, noble soul, Orlando Furioso, birdlime snare</keywords>

# Third Dialogue.

-> TANSILLO. <-

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'P' depicting a landscape with a person and an animal, possibly a lamb or dog.</image-desc>They propose that there are many species of frenzies <note>original: *furori*—referring to the Platonic concept of "divine madness" or intense states of spiritual inspiration</note>, all of which can be reduced to two main types. Some show nothing but blindness, stupidity, and an irrational impulse that tends toward senseless, bestial behavior. Others consist of a certain divine abstraction <note>a state of being "drawn away" from the material world or ordinary consciousness</note>, through which some individuals become, in fact, better than ordinary men. 

These latter types are divided into two kinds. First, there are those who, having become a dwelling place for gods or divine spirits, say and do marvelous things without either they themselves or others understanding the reason <note>original: *raggione*—logic or underlying cause</note> why. Such people are usually elevated to this state because they were originally unrefined and ignorant. In these individuals—being empty of their own spirit and sense, like a room that has been swept clean—the divine sense and spirit intrudes. This divine spirit is less able to find a place or reveal itself in those who are already full of [their own...]

<vocab>frenzies, divine abstraction, divine spirits, reason, Tansillo</vocab>

<summary>Tansillo categorizes "frenzies" into two types: the low, bestial variety and the high, divine variety. He further explains that divine inspiration often chooses those "empty" of their own ego, such as the simple or uneducated, to act as vessels for a higher spirit.</summary>
<keywords>divine madness, furori, spiritual inspiration, intellectual vs bestial, vessel of the divine, Giordano Bruno</keywords>

--- Page 72 ---
<meta>This page continues the Third Dialogue from the previous page. Tansillo is in the middle of explaining the two types of divinely inspired individuals: those who are passive vessels for the divine and those who actively engage their own intellect to reach a higher state.</meta>

### Third Dialogue.

[...their own] reason and sense, because sometimes divinity wishes the world to know for certain that those people do not speak through their own study and experience—as is manifest—it follows that they speak and act by a superior intelligence. Through this, the multitude of men worthily holds such people in greater admiration and faith. 

Others, by being accustomed or suited to contemplation and by having an innate, lucid, and intellectual spirit, are moved by an internal stimulus and natural fervor. This is aroused by the love of divinity, of justice, of truth, and of glory; by the fire of desire and the breath of intention, they sharpen their senses. In the seat <note>original: *soltro*—likely meaning the "throne" or "inner sanctum" of the mind</note> of the **cogitative faculty** <term>cogitative faculty: the part of the mind responsible for processing thoughts and reasoning</term>, they kindle the rational light with which they see more than is ordinary. These people do not ultimately come to speak and act like vessels and instruments, but as principal craftsmen and efficient causes.

**CICADA**: Of these two types, which do you judge to be better?

**TANSILLO**: The first have more dignity, power, and efficacy in themselves, because they possess the divinity. The second are themselves more worthy, more powerful and effective, and are of the divine <note>Bruno distinguishes between "having" divinity as a passive host versus "belonging" to the divine through active intellectual effort</note>. The first are worthy like the donkey that carries the sacraments; the second are like a sacred thing itself. In the first, one considers and sees in effect the divinity, and that divinity is admired, adored, and obeyed. In the second, one considers and sees the excellence of their own **humanity** <note>original: *humanitade*</note>.

**CICADA**: Now let us come to the point: these **frenzies** <note>original: *furori*—the "heroic" or divine inspirations being discussed throughout the work</note> of which we reason, and which we see put into execution in these sentences <note>referring to the poems or "propositions" being analyzed in the dialogue</note>: they are not oblivion, 

<meta>catchword: but a</meta>

<summary>Tansillo distinguishes between passive "vessels" of divine inspiration (who are like donkeys carrying holy relics) and active intellectual contemplators (who are themselves sacred). He argues that while the former show the power of God, the latter demonstrate the peak excellence of human nature.</summary>
<keywords>divine frenzy, intellect, contemplation, cogitative faculty, passive vs active inspiration, humanity, divinity</keywords>

### Third Dialogue.

but a divine impetus that imprints wings upon him, whereby approaching more and more to the **intellectual sun** <note>original: *sole intelligentiale*. In Neoplatonic philosophy, this refers to the ultimate source of truth and divinity, which illuminates the mind just as the physical sun illuminates the eyes.</note>, casting off the rust of human cares, he becomes a proven and pure gold, has a feeling of the divine and internal harmony, and accords his thoughts and gestures with the symmetry of the law innate in all things. 

Not like one intoxicated by the cups of **Circe** <note>The enchantress from Homer’s *Odyssey* who turned men into beasts; here Bruno uses her as a metaphor for being enslaved by base, worldly desires.</note> does he go stumbling and bumping now into this, now into that other ditch, now against this, now against that other rock; nor like a wandering **Proteus** <note>A sea god capable of changing his shape; Bruno uses him to represent the instability of those who lack a spiritual center.</note> changing himself now into this, now into that other face, never finding a place, manner, nor matter to stop and establish himself. 

But without untuning the harmony, he conquers and overcomes the horrendous monsters; and however much he may happen to decline, he easily returns to the <term>sesto: literally a "compass" or "order," referring to a state of balance and right direction</term> with those intimate instincts, which like nine muses leap and sing around the splendor of the universal **Apollo** <note>The god of light, music, and prophecy, representing the unified divine source around which the soul’s powers (the Muses) revolve.</note>: and under sensible images and material things, he goes about understanding divine orders and counsels. 

It is true that sometimes having as a faithful guide **love**, which is <term>gemino: twin</term> <note>original: *gemino*. Refers to the Neoplatonic concept of "Twin Loves"—one earthly and one heavenly. Here, even heavenly love can lead to a "frenzy" when the human mind reaches its limits.</note>; and because sometimes due to occurring impediments he sees himself defrauded of his effort, then like one insane and furious he plunges headlong the love of that which he cannot comprehend. Wherefore, confused by the abyss of divinity, he sometimes lets go his hands, and then returns again to force himself with the **will** toward that place where he cannot arrive with the **intellect**. 

It is true also that ordinarily he goes wandering, now more in one, now more

<summary>Bruno describes the "heroic" soul as a spiritual alchemist, turning the "rust" of human worry into "pure gold" through an intellectual ascent toward God. He contrasts this focused spiritual journey with the aimless, animalistic wandering of those distracted by worldly desires.</summary>

### Third Dialogue.

another form of the **twin Cupid** <note>original: *gemino Cupido*. In Neoplatonic philosophy, this refers to the two types of love: one that draws the soul toward physical beauty and another that elevates it toward divine contemplation.</note> is revealed: for the primary lesson that love grants him is that he must contemplate divine beauty in shadows (when he is unable to see it in a mirror); and just as **Penelope’s suitors** <note>The characters from Homer’s *Odyssey* who pursued Odysseus’s wife; Bruno uses them here to represent those who occupy themselves with lesser, worldly things (the "handmaidens") when they cannot attain the ultimate Truth (the "mistress").</note> dally with the handmaidens when they are not permitted to converse with the mistress. Now therefore, to conclude, you can understand from what has been said what kind of **frenzied one** <note>original: *furioso*. This refers to the "heroic enthusiast" who is possessed by a divine madness or inspiration that drives him toward the divine.</note> this is, whose image is placed before us. When it is said:

<meta>This page continues the Third Dialogue. The previous page established the metaphor of the "frenzied" lover—the soul seeking the divine—by contrasting him with animals like the butterfly, stag, and unicorn who follow mere physical instinct. This page explains why the "heroic" lover chooses to remain in the "fire" and "bonds" of love, seeing them not as suffering, but as a path to the ultimate Truth.</meta>

<header>Third Dialogue.</header>

### Where he demonstrates that his love is not like that
### of the butterfly, the stag, and the unicorn,
### who would flee if they had judgment of the
### fire, the arrow, and the snares, and who
### have no sense of anything but pleasure:

but is guided by a most sensible and all-too-clear-sighted **frenzy** <note>original: *furore*. For Bruno, this is not a mental illness, but a "divine madness" or intense intellectual passion that drives the soul toward God.</note>; which makes him love that fire more than any refreshment, that wound more than any health, those bonds more than any other liberty. Because this evil is not absolutely evil: but is so only in relation to the good according to common opinion, and is false; like that which the aged **Saturn** <note>The Roman god of Time (Cronus) who, in myth, devoured his own children. Bruno uses him here to represent how Time seems to "devour" or destroy things, which the human mind perceives as "evil" or loss, even though it is part of a divine order.</note> uses as seasoning in the devouring he performs of his own children. Because this evil, in the eye of eternity, is absolutely understood either as good, or as a guide that leads us to it; considering that this fire is the burning desire for divine things, this arrow is the impression of the ray of beauty of the **celestial light** <note>original: *superna luce*</note>, these snares are the species of the true which unite our mind to the first truth; and the species of the good which make us united and joined to the first and highest good. Toward that sense I approached when I said:

> With such a beautiful fire, and with such a noble bond
>    Beauty inflames me, and **integrity** <note>original: *honestá*. This refers to moral or spiritual beauty as much as physical appearance.</note> binds me,
>    So that in flame and servitude it is fitting that I rejoice,
>    Let me flee liberty and fear the ice;
> The blaze is such that I burn and am not undone,
>    The bond is such that the world praises it with me,
>    Neither does fear freeze me, nor does pain unbind me;
>    But the heat is tranquil, the entanglement is sweet.

<meta>catchword: Scor-</meta>

<summary>Bruno explains that the spiritual "frenzy" of the soul is a deliberate choice to seek divine truth through the metaphorical fires and bonds of love, interpreting apparent suffering as a necessary path to God. He distinguishes this intellectual passion from the blind, self-preserving instincts of animals.</summary>

<keywords>heroic frenzy, divine love, Saturn, allegory, Neoplatonism, Giordano Bruno, spiritual desire</keywords>

--- Page 77 ---
<meta>This page continues the Third Dialogue. The previous page concluded by explaining that the lover's "frenzy" is actually a divine desire for the first truth and goodness, viewing the "snares" and "fire" of love as intellectual bonds to the divine.</meta>

<header>Third Dialogue.</header>
<sig>C.4.</sig>

->**I behold so high the light that inflames me,**<-
->**And the snare woven from such rich thread,**<-
->**That as the thought is born, desire dies.**<-
->**Since so beautiful a flame shines in my heart,**<-
->**And so beautiful a bond binds my will,**<-
->**Let the shadow be a slave, and let my ashes burn.**<-
<note>In this poem, the speaker describes a transition from physical desire to intellectual contemplation. The "shadow" refers to the physical body or the lower part of the soul, which must become a servant to the higher "light" of the mind. The "ashes" represent the remnants of the self after being transformed by the fire of divine love.</note>

All loves (if they are heroic <note>original: *heroici*. From the Greek *eros*, but also implying the "heroic" struggle of the soul to transcend the physical world.</note> and are not purely animalistic—which are called "natural" and are base toward procreation, acting as instruments of nature in a certain way) have divinity as their object and tend toward divine beauty. This beauty first communicates itself to souls and shines within them; and from those souls, or (to put it better) *through* those souls, it then communicates itself to bodies. This is why a well-formed affection loves bodies, or bodily beauty, for that which is an index of the beauty of the spirit. Indeed, that which makes us fall in love with the body is a certain spirituality that we perceive in it, which is called beauty. This beauty does not consist of larger or smaller dimensions, nor in specific colors or forms, but in a certain harmony and consonance of limbs and colors. This shows a certain perceptible affinity <note>original: *sensibile affinità*. Bruno suggests that our physical senses can "detect" the spiritual harmony within a person, like a resonance between two musical instruments.</note> with the spirit to the most acute and penetrating senses. From this it follows that such senses more easily and intensely fall in love, and also more easily fall out of love, and more intensely feel indignation. They do so with the same ease and intensity that might occur during a change in an ugly spirit, which becomes revealed through some gesture or expressed intention—

<note>The list below clarifies the technical terms used by Bruno to describe the ascent from physical attraction to spiritual enlightenment.</note>
<term>heroici</term>: Heroic loves, distinguishing intellectual passion from mere animal instinct.
<term>diuinità</term>: Divinity, the ultimate source of all beauty.
<term>diuina bellezza</term>: Divine beauty, the "light" that reflects through the soul into the physical world.
<term>anima</term>: The soul, which acts as the mirror or bridge between the divine and the body.
<term>corporal bellezza</term>: Bodily beauty, seen here as a mere signpost or "index" of something higher.
<term>spirito</term>: Spirit, the inner essence or character of a person.
<term>armonia</term>: Harmony, the mathematical and spiritual proportion that defines true beauty.
<term>sensibile affinità</term>: Perceptible affinity, the resonance between the observer's soul and the beauty they see.

<summary>Bruno argues that "heroic" love is an intellectual pursuit of divine beauty, which is only reflected in physical forms. He defines beauty not as physical size or color, but as a spiritual harmony that the soul recognizes through the senses.</summary>
<keywords>heroic love, divine beauty, Neoplatonism, harmony, soul and body, spiritual index, Giordano Bruno</keywords>

<header>Third Dialogue.</header>

...so strongly that such ugliness passes from the soul to the body, making it no longer appear beautiful as it previously appeared. Therefore, the beauty of the body has the power to kindle a fire: but not truly to bind the lover or ensure they cannot flee, unless the grace required in the spirit supports it—such as integrity, gratitude, courtesy, and discernment. For this reason, I called that fire which kindled me "beautiful," because the bond that tied me was also noble.

**CI.** <note>Cicada, one of the two interlocutors in this dialogue.</note> Do not believe it is always so, Tansillo; for sometimes, although we discover the spirit to be vicious, we do not cease to remain kindled and ensnared. Thus, even though reason sees the evil and unworthiness of such a love, it still lacks the power to push away the disordered appetite. I believe the Nolan <note>The "Nolan" refers to the author, Giordano Bruno, who was born in Nola. He often uses this title to refer to himself in his dialogues.</note> was in such a state when he said:

> Alas, I am constrained by frenzy <note>original: *furore*. In Bruno’s philosophy, this is the "heroic furor," an intense, transformative state of the soul reaching for the infinite.</note>,
> To take hold of my own harm,
> Which makes love appear to me as a supreme good.
> Wearily, the soul does not care
> To ever try contrary counsels;
> And of the fierce tyrant,
> Who feeds me on hardships,
> And was able to exile me from myself,
> More than of liberty, I am content.
> I spread my sails to the wind,
> That it may withdraw me from the hateful good;
> And stormily lead me to the sweet harm.

<meta>catchword: TAN.</meta>

<summary>Tansillo argues that spiritual grace is necessary to truly bind a lover, while Cicada counters that a "frenzied" lover may remain enslaved to a "sweet harm" even when reason recognizes it as destructive.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the Third Dialogue from the *Heroic Furors*. Following Cicada's point that a lover can remain ensnared even by a vicious soul, Tansillo explains the mechanics of such an attraction, distinguishing between the irrational pull of beauty and the rational act of wishing someone well.</meta>

## Third Dialogue:

**TAN.** <note>Tansillo, the primary voice for the author's philosophical views in this dialogue.</note> This happens when both spirits are vicious and are tinted, as it were, with the same ink; for love is awakened, kindled, and confirmed by conformity <note>The idea that "like attracts like"—that a shared moral character, even a bad one, creates a bond.</note>. Thus, the vicious easily find common ground in acts of the same vice. And I do not wish to omit what I know from experience: that even though I discovered vices in a soul that are utterly abominable to me—such as a filthy avarice, a most vile greed for money, ingratitude for favors and courtesies received, and a love for people who are entirely base (of which vices this last one especially displeases me, because it robs the lover of the hope that by being or making himself more worthy, he might be more accepted by her)—nevertheless, I did not fail to burn for her bodily beauty. But what of it? I loved her without "good will" <note>original: *buona volontà*. In Renaissance philosophy, this refers to a rational desire for the other person’s ultimate good.</note>, since I would have been no more saddened than cheered by her misfortunes and accidents.

**CIC.** Therefore, that distinction they make between *loving* and *wishing well* is very appropriate and to the point.

**TAN.** It is true; for many we "wish well"—that is, we desire that they be wise and just—but we do not "love" them, because they are wicked and ignorant. Many we "love" because they are beautiful, but we do not wish them well, because they do not deserve it. And among the other things that the lover deems such a person unworthy of, the first is to be loved at all. And therefore, although he cannot abstain from loving, nonetheless to him...

<summary>Tansillo explains that while similarity in vice can bind two people, one can also experience a "vicious love" where they are physically attracted to beauty despite despising the person’s character. He and Cicada distinguish between "loving" (passionate attraction) and "wishing well" (benevolent affection).</summary>

<keywords>conformity of spirits, vice, avarice, physical beauty, loving vs. wishing well, moral worth, Giordano Bruno, Eroici Furori</keywords>

# Third Dialogue

...he should be said to be subject to so many torments <note>original: *martiri*. Bruno uses the language of religious martyrdom to describe the intense suffering of the soul seeking God.</note>: because every lover who is disunited and separated from the thing loved (to which, as he is joined by affection, he would wish to be joined in reality <note>original: *effetto*. Tansillo contrasts *affetto* (the internal state of loving) with *effetto* (the actualization or effect of that love in reaching its goal).</note>) finds himself in sorrow and pain. He tortures and torments himself: not because he loves—seeing as he feels his love is employed most worthily and nobly—but because he is deprived of that fruition <note>original: *fruitione*. This refers to the ultimate enjoyment or "possession" of the beloved object, a term often used in medieval theology to describe the soul's enjoyment of God.</note> which he would obtain if he reached the goal toward which he strives. 

He does not grieve because of the desire that enlivens him, but because of the difficulty of the pursuit <note>original: *studio*. In this context, it implies a focused, strenuous intellectual and spiritual effort.</note> that martyrs him. Let others, then, deem him unhappy at their pleasure for this appearance of a cruel fate, as if it had condemned him to such pains; for he will not, for all that, cease to recognize the debt he owes to Love, and to give him thanks, because Love has presented before the eyes of his mind an **intelligible form** <term>intelligible form: in the Platonic tradition, this is a non-physical essence or idea that can only be perceived by the intellect, not the physical eyes.</term>, in which—in this earthly life (shut up in this **prison of the flesh**, bound by these **nerves**, and supported by these **bones**) —it may be permitted to him to contemplate the divinity more highly than if any other form or likeness of it had been offered.

**CICADA.** So the divine and living object he speaks of is the highest intelligible form he could form of the divinity; and it is not some physical beauty that overshadowed his thought, as it appears on the surface of the senses?

**TANS.** <note>Tansillo begins his response to Cicada's question on the following page.</note>

<summary>Tansillo explains that the "heroic" lover suffers not from love itself, but from the separation between his desire and the divine object. He argues that this intellectual pursuit allows the soul to contemplate God even while imprisoned in the human body.</summary>

<keywords>heroic love, intelligible form, Platonic philosophy, fruition, body as prison, contemplation of divinity</keywords>

# Third Dialogue

**TAN.** True, because no **sensible thing** <note>original: *sensibile*. Referring to things that can be perceived by the five physical senses.</note>, nor any image of such a thing, can rise to such dignity. 

**CIC.** How then does he mention that **species** <term>species: in the philosophy of the time, a 'species' (Italian: *specie*) is not a biological category, but an "intentional species"—a mental representation or likeness of an object that allows the mind to perceive and understand it.</term> as the object, if (as it seems to me) the true object is **divinity** itself? 

**TAN.** Divinity is the final, ultimate, and most perfect object: but not in this state where we cannot see God except as in a shadow and a mirror <note>original: *in ombra et specchio*. This is a common Renaissance philosophical and theological motif, echoing St. Paul’s "through a glass, darkly." It suggests that in mortal life, we see only reflections of the divine truth, not the truth itself.</note>; and therefore He cannot be an object for us except through some **likeness**. This likeness is not the kind that can be abstracted and acquired from physical beauty and excellence by the power of the senses; rather, it is the kind that can be formed in the mind by the power of the **intellect**. 

When the soul finds itself in this state, it comes to lose the love and affection for every other thing, whether it be sensible or **intelligible** <note>original: *intelligibile*. This refers to things understood by the mind/intellect, rather than felt by the body.</note>; because the soul, joined to that divine light, becomes light itself, and consequently makes itself a god. For it draws the divinity into itself, being itself in God through the intention with which it penetrates the divinity (as far as is possible); and God is in the soul insofar as, after having penetrated, the soul comes to conceive divinity and (as far as is possible) to receive and comprehend it in its concept. 

Now, the human intellect feeds on these images and likenesses from this lower world, until it is permitted to behold with a purer eye the beauty of the divinity. It is like one who has arrived at a most excellent and highly decorated building; while he goes about considering it piece by piece, he is pleased, he con-

<meta>catchword: tenta (likely continuing as "contenta" or "contents himself")</meta>

<summary>Tansillo explains that while God is the ultimate object of human desire, in this life we can only perceive Him through intellectual "likenesses" or images rather than direct vision. He describes a mystical union where the soul, by contemplating divine light, becomes "a god" by drawing divinity into itself.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the "Third Dialogue" of Giordano Bruno's *The Heroic Furors* (De gli eroici furori). Tansillo is explaining to Cicognino how the soul's focus shifts from mental images (intelligible species) to the ultimate source of those images—the Divine. He is elaborating on the metaphysical hierarchy of the body, soul, and mind.</meta>

...contented, it feeds on a noble wonder. But if it should then happen that it sees the lord of those images, of incomparably greater beauty, having left behind all care and thought of them, it is entirely turned and intent on considering that one <note>original: *quell' uno*. Referring to "The One," the Neoplatonic concept of the ultimate, indivisible source of all existence.</note>. Here then is the difference between this state, where we see the divine beauty in **intelligible species** <term>species: as noted previously, mental representations or reflections of divine truth.</term> taken from effects, works, masteries, shadows, and likenesses of it, and that other state where it might be permitted to see it in its own presence.

He says further: **I FEED UPON A LOFTY ENTERPRISE**, because (as the **Pythagoreans** <note>Followers of Pythagoras, who believed in the transmigration of souls and a mathematical-harmonic structure of the universe.</note> note) just as the body moves about the soul, so the soul turns and moves about God.

**CI.** Then the body is not the place of the soul?

**TAN.** No, because the soul is not in the body locally, but as an **intrinsic form** and an **extrinsic former** <note>original: *forma intrinseca, et formatore estrinseco*. This means the soul is both the internal blueprint that gives the body life and the external force that shapes and organizes it.</note>; like that which makes the limbs and shapes the **composite** <note>The "composite" refers to the whole human being made of both matter and spirit.</note> from within and from without. The body, therefore, is in the soul, the soul in the mind, and the mind is either God or is in God, as **Plotinus** <note>A major Hellenistic philosopher and the founder of Neoplatonism, whose works deeply influenced Bruno’s view of the soul's ascent to the Divine.</note> said. Just as by essence it is in God, who is its life, similarly through intellectual operation—and the will that follows such operation—it refers itself to His light and beatific object. Worthily, then, does this affection of **heroic fury** <term>heroic fury: (Italian: *heroico furore*) a state of divine madness or intellectual passion where the soul is driven to transcend the physical world to unite with the infinite truth.</term> feed on such a lofty enterprise. Nor [is it deterred] because the object is infinite, in a most simple act, and our intellectual power cannot apprehend the infinite except through discourse, or in-

<summary>Tansillo explains that the soul is not "inside" the body, but rather the body exists within the soul, which in turn exists within the divine Mind. This Neoplatonic hierarchy justifies why the "heroic lover" seeks God beyond mere physical or mental images.</summary>

### Third Dialogue.

...a certain manner of discourse, as if to say in a certain potential or aptitudinal reason, and like one who leads himself to the pursuit of the infinite, so that he comes to establish an end where there is no end. <note>Bruno is describing a paradox: the soul seeks a "final goal," but because God is infinite, that goal can never be "finished" or exhausted.</note> CIC. Worthily so, because the ultimate end must not have an end, considering that it would not then be ultimate. It is therefore infinite in intention, in perfection, in essence, and in whatever other way it is an end. TAN. You speak the truth. Now, in this life, such a pasture <note>original: *pastura*. This refers to the spiritual and intellectual "food" upon which the soul grazes or finds nourishment.</note> is of such a nature that it inflames the desire more than it can satisfy it, as that divine Poet well shows who said:

> My soul is longing and weary 
> for the living God. <note>A translation of Psalm 42:2.</note>

And in another place:

> My eyes have grown weak from looking up to the heights. <note>original Latin: *Attenuati sunt oculi mei suspiciente in excelsu*. A reference to Isaiah 38:14 or Psalm 121, expressing the strain of spiritual contemplation.</note>

Therefore it says:

> **AND ALTHOUGH IT DOES NOT ATTAIN THE DESIRED END**, 
> and the soul melts away in such study, 
> **IT IS ENOUGH THAT IT BE SO NOBLY** 

kindled; he means that in the meantime the soul consoles itself and receives all the glory it can receive in such a state, and that it may participate in that ultimate furor <term>furor: in this context, a state of "divine madness" or inspired passion where the human soul is elevated beyond its normal limits by the love of truth.</term> of man as man in this condition in which he finds himself now, as we see. CICA. It seems to me that the **Peripatetics** <note>The Peripatetics were followers of Aristotle, who famously taught while walking about the "peripatos" or covered walkways of the Lyceum in Athens.</note> (as **Averroes** <note>Averroes (Ibn Rushd) was a 12th-century Islamic philosopher whose commentaries on Aristotle were central to medieval and Renaissance thought.</note> explained) mean this when they say that the supreme happiness of man consists in perfection through the **speculative sciences** <term>speculative sciences: branches of knowledge focused on theoretical contemplation and the understanding of universal truths, such as metaphysics and mathematics.</term>. <meta>This page continues the "Third Dialogue" of Giordano Bruno’s *The Heroic Furors*. Tansillo and Cicada are discussing the soul's ascent toward the infinite. ### Third Dialogue.

...by means of the <term>intelligible species</term><note>In Renaissance philosophy, an "intelligible species" is a mental representation or "image" that allows the human intellect to understand an object. It is the bridge between the physical world and the mind.</note> [the soul] unites either with the separate substances, as those people say; or with the divine mind, as is the manner of speaking of the Platonists. I will leave aside for now the discussion of the soul or man in any other state and way of being that might be found or believed in. **CIC.** But what perfection or satisfaction can man find in that knowledge which is not perfect? **TAN.** It will never be perfect in terms of how much the highest object can be understood; but it will be perfect in terms of how much our intellect can understand: it is enough that in this and any other state, the divine beauty is present to him as far as the horizon of his sight extends. **CIC.** But among men, not all can reach that point where one or two might arrive. **TAN.** It is enough that all should run; it is plenty that each does what is possible for them; because the <term>heroic genius</term><note>original: *heroico ingegno*. This refers to the exceptionally gifted or "frenzied" mind that seeks truth with passionate intensity, a central theme of Bruno's work.</note> is more content to fall or fail worthily and in lofty undertakings—where he might show the dignity of his spirit—than to succeed to perfection in things less noble and base. **CIC.** Certainly, a worthy and heroic death is better than an unworthy and vile triumph. **TAN.** Concerning this, I composed this sonnet.

> Since I have spread my wings toward the beautiful desire,
> The more I see the air beneath my feet,
> The more I offer my swift feathers to the wind,
> And I despise the world, and set out toward heaven.
> Nor does the cruel end of the son of Daedalus<note>A reference to Icarus, who flew too close to the sun with wings made of wax and fell to his death. While often used as a warning against pride, Bruno uses Icarus as a symbol of the "heroic" soul who accepts death as the price of seeking the ultimate light.</note>
> Make me turn downward; rather, I rise up all the more.
> For I well realize that I shall fall dead to the earth;
> But what life can equal my death?

<summary>Tansillo explains that human knowledge of the infinite is limited by our capacity, yet the "heroic genius" finds more fulfillment in a noble failure to reach the heights than in succeeding at trivial tasks. He uses the myth of Icarus to illustrate that a glorious death in pursuit of the divine is superior to a mundane life.</summary>

--- Page 86 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Third Dialogue" of Giordano Bruno’s *The Heroic Furors*. Following the discussion on the soul's striving for the infinite, the text transitions into a poetic reflection on the "heroic" flight of the mind, which Tansillo and Cicada then analyze through a Platonic lens.</meta>

### Third Dialogue.

<meta>catchword: matter</meta>

->I hear the voice of my heart through the air:<-
->“Where are you taking me, you reckless one? Stoop down,<-
->For rarely is great daring without grief.”<-
->“Fear not the high ruin,” I reply;<-
->“Cleave the clouds safely, and die content,<-
->If heaven destines us for so illustrious a death.”<-

**CICADA.** I understand what he means when he says, **“ENOUGH That UPWARDS I raised myself;”** but I do not understand when he says **“AND FROM THE IGNOBLE NUMBER I freed myself,”** unless he means having emerged from the <term>Platonic cave</term><note>A famous allegory from Plato’s *Republic* describing people who mistake shadows on a wall for reality. To "exit the cave" is to achieve true philosophical enlightenment.</note>; removed from the condition of the foolish and most ignoble multitude: seeing as those who profit from this contemplation cannot be many or numerous.

**TANSILLO.** You understand it very well. Furthermore, by "ignoble number," one can also understand the body and sensual cognition. From these, anyone who wishes to unite with a nature of a contrary kind <note>The "contrary nature" refers to the spiritual or divine realm, which is the opposite of the heavy, material body.</note> must rise and free themselves.

**CICADA.** The Platonists speak of two kinds of bonds by which the soul is tied to the body. One is a certain life-giving act which, like a ray, descends from the soul into the body. The other is a certain vital quality that results from that act within the body. Now, regarding this most noble "number" which moves—that is, the soul—how do you understand its being "dissolved" from the ignoble number which is the body?

**TANSILLO.** Certainly, it was not meant in either of those ways, but rather in the way that the powers <note>The "powers" (potenze) are the faculties of the soul, such as intellect and will.</note> that are not grasped or held captive in the womb of...

They strain toward the <term>intelligible world</term><note>In Platonic philosophy, the "intelligible world" is the realm of pure ideas and truth, far above the sensory world we experience with our bodies.</note> as if toward their native home, from which they were once cast down beneath the rule of fate and the limits of <term>generation</term><note>The cycle of physical birth, decay, and death.</note> due to their turning toward inferior things. <meta>This page continues the "Third Dialogue" of Giordano Bruno’s *The Heroic Furors*. The previous page discussed how souls fluctuate between the spiritual and material realms. This page elaborates on the "circular" nature of the universe and whether the soul's descent into the physical body is a matter of free will or "fatal" necessity.</meta>

### Third Dialogue.

In nature there is a revolution and a circle through which, for the perfection and help of others, superior things incline toward the inferior; and for their own excellence and happiness, inferior things rise toward the superior. Therefore, the <term>Pythagoreans</term><note>Followers of Pythagoras, who believed in the transmigration of souls and a mathematical structure to the universe.</note> and <term>Platonists</term><note>Followers of Plato, who emphasized the soul's journey from the world of ideas down into the material world and back again.</note> hold that it is granted to the soul that at certain times—not only through spontaneous will, which turns it toward the comprehension of different natures, but also by the necessity of an internal law written and recorded by the <term>fatal decree</term><note>The "decreto fatale" refers to the inescapable laws of the universe or "Fate," which govern the movements of all beings.</note>—it goes to find its own justly determined fate. And they say that souls do not decline from divinity so much by a certain determination and their own will as if they were rebels, as by a certain order through which they become affected toward matter; whence, not as if by free intention, but as if by a certain hidden consequence, they come to fall. This is the inclination they have toward <term>generation</term><note>The process of being born into a physical body; the transition from the spiritual to the material realm.</note>, as toward a certain lesser good. (I say a "lesser good" as far as it belongs to that particular nature, but not indeed as far as it belongs to the <term>universal nature</term><note>In Bruno’s philosophy, the "universal nature" is the whole of existence, where every event—even the "fall" of a soul—is part of a perfect, just, and necessary system.</note>, where nothing happens without an excellent end that disposes everything according to justice.) Finding themselves in this generation, they return once again to their superior states <note>original: "habiti superiori"</note> through the conversion that follows in the cycle of <term>vicissitude</term><note>A central theme for Bruno: the idea that all things in the universe are in a constant state of change and rotation, moving from one opposite to another.</note>.

**CIC.** So then, these men hold that souls are driven by the necessity of fate, and have no counsel of their own to guide them to...

<meta>The catchword "fatto?" at the bottom of the page suggests the sentence continues on the next page, likely discussing the "fate" or "deed" (fatto) that directs the soul.</meta>

<summary>This page describes the soul's cyclical journey between the divine and material realms, arguing that its descent into physical "generation" is governed by a universal law of justice and necessity rather than simple rebellion.</summary>
<keywords>Pythagoreans, Platonists, universal nature, fate, generation, soul's descent, justice, cycle of vicissitude</keywords>

--- Page 89 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Third Dialogue" of Giordano Bruno’s *The Heroic Furors*. It picks up mid-sentence following the discussion of the soul's inclination toward "generation" or physical birth. Tansillo is explaining how the soul relates to the different faculties of Mind, Reason, and Imagination.</meta>

### Third Dialogue.

...done? TANSILLO. Necessity, fate, nature, counsel, and will, in things that are justly and without error ordained, all concur as one. Furthermore (as <term>Plotinus</term><note>original: "Plotino." A major Neoplatonic philosopher whose works deeply influenced Bruno’s views on the soul’s journey.</note> reports), some hold that certain souls are able to escape their own misfortune; these, before the bodily habit is confirmed in them, recognizing the danger, flee back to the Mind. Because the Mind raises the soul toward sublime things, just as the imagination pulls it down toward inferior things: the Mind maintains it in a state of stability and identity, just as the imagination keeps it in motion and diversity; the Mind always understands the One, while the imagination is always busy fashioning various images. In the middle is the <term>rational faculty</term><note>The "facultá rationale" acts as a bridge in Renaissance psychology, connecting the higher spiritual intellect with the lower physical senses.</note>, which is composed of everything, as that in which the one concurs with the multitude, the same with the different, motion with stillness, and the inferior with the superior.

Now, this conversion and <term>vicissitude</term><note>The natural cycle of change and transformation that Bruno believed governed the entire universe.</note> is represented in the <term>wheel of metamorphoses</term><note>A symbolic image similar to the Wheel of Fortune, illustrating how a soul can rise toward the divine or sink toward the bestial.</note> where man sits at the highest point, a beast lies at the bottom, a half-man and half-beast descends from the left, and a half-beast and half-man ascends from the right. This conversion is shown where <term>Jove</term><note>Jupiter, the king of the Roman gods, who frequently transformed into animals in classical mythology.</note>, according to the diversity of his affections and manners toward inferior things, clothes himself in different figures, taking the form of beasts; and likewise, the other gods transmigrate into low and alien forms.
