### Third Dialogue.

...and on the contrary, through the feeling of their own nobility, they reclaim their own and divine form: just as the <term>heroic lover</term><note>original: "furioso heroico." In Bruno’s philosophy, the "heroic" individual is driven by a divinely inspired passion or "frenzy" to seek the truth and the infinite, rather than being trapped by earthly desires.</note> rising through the conceived image of divine beauty and goodness, with the wings of the intellect and the <term>intellectual will</term><note>The "voluntade intellettiua" is the soul's capacity to desire and strive for spiritual or universal truths rather than merely physical or sensory pleasures.</note> rises toward divinity, leaving behind the form of a lower subject. And therefore it was said: **FROM A viler subject I become a god. I CHANGE into a god from an inferior thing.**

# Fourth Dialogue.

->TANSILLO.<-

<image-desc>Ornamental woodcut initial letter "C" containing an image of a crescent moon and a face within a landscape.</image-desc>

Thus is described the discourse of <term>heroic love</term><note>original: "amor heroico." In Bruno’s philosophy, this is a divinely inspired passion or "frenzy" that drives the human soul to seek the infinite rather than being satisfied with worldly pleasures.</note> insofar as it strives toward its own object, which is the <term>highest good</term><note>original: "sommo bene." This refers to the ultimate source of all goodness and morality, often identified with God or the Unity of the Universe.</note>; and the <term>heroic intellect</term> which strives to reach its own goal, which is the first truth, or absolute truth. Now, in this first discourse, he presents the full summary and intention of this pursuit; the order of which is further detailed in the five following sections. He says, then:

<image-desc>Ornamental woodcut horizontal divider with scrolling vines and floral patterns.</image-desc>

To the woods the young <term>Actaeon</term><note>In Greek mythology, Actaeon was a hunter who stumbled upon the goddess Diana bathing. Bruno uses this myth as an allegory: Actaeon is the philosopher who, in searching for "the prey" (divine truth), is eventually transformed by what he sees.</note> unleashes his mastiffs and his greyhounds, when destiny directs his hesitant and incautious path along the trail of wild woodland beasts.

# Fourth Dialogue.

> Behold amid the waters the most beautiful form and face,
> That mortal and divine eyes could ever see,
> In purple, alabaster, and fine gold
> He saw; and the great hunter became the prey.
> ->The stag that toward the most dense<-
> ->Places directed its swiftest steps,<-
> ->Was quickly devoured by his many great dogs.<-
> ->I release my thoughts<-
> ->Toward a high prey; and they, turned back toward me,<-
> ->Bring me death with cruel and fierce bites.<-

---
<image-desc>An ornamental typographic headpiece featuring symmetrical scrollwork, acanthus leaves, and a central floral motif.</image-desc>

<term>Actaeon</term><note>In this allegory, Actaeon represents the philosopher or seeker who searches for divine truth, only to be transformed by the vision of it.</note> signifies the intellect intent upon the hunt for divine wisdom and the apprehension of divine beauty. He unleashes the mastiffs and the greyhounds, of which the latter are swifter and the former stronger. This is because the operation of the intellect precedes the operation of the <term>will</term><note>original: "voluntade." Bruno follows a philosophical tradition where the mind must first "see" or understand an object before the heart can "will" or love it.</note>; yet the will is more vigorous and effective than the intellect. This is due to the fact that for the human intellect, divine goodness and beauty are more lovable than they are comprehensible; furthermore, love is that which moves and pushes the intellect so that it may go before it like a lantern. 

**To the Woods**, uncultivated and solitary places, visited and explored by very few, and therefore where the footprints of many men are not imprinted. **The Young Man**, little experienced or practiced, as one whose life is brief and whose <term>frenzy</term><note>original: "furore." This refers to the "heroic furor" or divine madness—an intense, inspired state of the soul seeking the infinite.</note> is unstable, **On the Doubtful Path** of the uncertain and ambiguous...

<meta>catchword: te (the conclusion of the word "ancipite," meaning ambiguous or two-headed)</meta>

<summary>Bruno uses the myth of Actaeon to illustrate the soul's pursuit of divine wisdom, explaining that the intellect (greyhounds) leads the way while the more powerful will (mastiffs) follows, driven by love.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the commentary on the myth of Actaeon within Giordano Bruno’s *The Heroic Furors*. The previous page concluded with an explanation of why the "woods" represent uncultivated, solitary places where the hunter (the seeker of truth) pursues divine concepts. This page specifically interprets the symbolism of the "greyhounds and mastiffs" as intellectual and emotional faculties, and decodes the colors purple, gold, and alabaster as divine attributes.</meta>

# Fourth Dialogue.

...reason and affection designated in the character of Pythagoras <note>A reference to the "Pythagorean Y," a symbol of the fork in the road of life where one must choose the difficult path of virtue or the easy path of vice.</note>, where the right and arduous path is seen as more thorny, uncultivated, and deserted; and through which he unleashes the greyhounds and the mastiffs upon the trail of woodland beasts, which are the <term>intelligible species</term><note>In Renaissance philosophy, these are the mental "forms" or essences of things that the mind grasps, rather than the physical things themselves.</note> of ideal concepts. These are hidden, pursued by few, visited by the very rarest of men, and they do not offer themselves to all those who seek them. "Behold, among the waters"—that is, in the mirror of similarities, in those works where the efficacy of divine goodness and splendor shines; these works are signified by the subject of the "waters above and below," which are under and above the firmament. <note>A reference to Genesis 1:7. Bruno interprets the waters as the reflected glory of God visible in the physical and spiritual universe.</note> **HE SEES** the most beautiful form and face: that is, the power and external operation that can be seen through the habit and act of contemplation and the application of the mind, whether mortal or divine, of man or of any god.

CICERO: I believe he does not make a comparison, nor does he place divine and human apprehension in the same category regarding the *mode* of understanding—which is very different—but regarding the *subject*, which is the same.

TANSILLO: It is so. He says, "**IN PURPLE**, alabaster, and gold." Because that which, in the image of physical beauty, is crimson, white, and blond, signifies in divinity the **purple** <note>original: *ostro*, a precious dye derived from sea snails, signifying royal power.</note> of vigorous divine power, the **gold** of divine wisdom, and the **alabaster** of divine beauty. In the contemplation of these, the Pythagoreans, <term>Chaldeans</term><note>Followers of the "Chaldean Oracles," a mystical tradition that Bruno and other Neoplatonists believed held ancient, divine wisdom.</note>, Platonists, and others strive to raise themselves in the best way they can.

<meta>This page continues the philosophical interpretation of the myth of Actaeon. On the previous page, Tansillo explained how the seeker (the hunter) pursues divine truth through the "wilderness" of contemplation. This page explains the mystical transformation where the seeker becomes the sought—the hunter becomes the prey—as the intellect and will are unified with the divine object of their search.</meta>

# Fourth Dialogue.

**HE SAW**: the great hunter understood as much as is possible, and he became the prey. He went out to prey and remained the prey. This hunter acts through the operation of the intellect, by which he converts the things he has grasped into himself.

CICADA: I understand. For he forms the <term>intelligible species</term><note>In Renaissance philosophy, these are the mental "forms" or essences of things that the mind grasps, rather than the physical things themselves.</note> in his own way and proportions them to his own capacity, because things are received according to the mode of the receiver. <note>A fundamental Scholastic principle: *Quidquid recipitur ad modum recipientis recipitur* (Whatever is received is received according to the mode of the recipient).</note>

TANSILLO: And this hunt happens through the operation of the will, by an act of which he converts himself into the object.

CICADA: I understand. Because love transforms and converts the lover into the thing loved.

TANSILLO: You know well that the intellect apprehends things intelligibly—that is, according to its own nature. But the will pursues things naturally—that is, according to the essence through which they exist in themselves. <note>original: "intelligibilmente ideſt, ſecondo il ſuo modo... naturalmente, cioé ſecondo la raggione con la quale ſonò in ſe." Bruno distinguishes between the mind’s *internal* representation of a thing and the will's desire for the thing’s *actual* essence.</note> Thus Actaeon, with those thoughts—those dogs—that were seeking the Good, Wisdom, Beauty, and the "wild woodland beast" outside of himself, reached her presence. Ravished out of himself by such great beauty, he became the prey; he saw himself converted into that which he was seeking. And he realized that he himself had become the desired prey of his own dogs—his own thoughts—because, having now drawn the divinity into himself, it was no longer necessary to seek it outside of himself.

CICADA: Therefore it is well said that "the kingdom of God is within us," and that divinity dwells in us by the power of the reformed intellect and will. <note>A reference to Luke 17:21. Bruno suggests that through intellectual and spiritual purification, the human soul becomes a vessel for the divine.</note>

TANSILLO: That is so. Behold, then, how Actaeon, having been made the prey of his dogs,

<keywords>Actaeon, intellect, will, divine transformation, intelligible species, internal divinity, love, contemplation</keywords>

### Fourth Dialogue.

dogs, pursued by his own thoughts, he runs and directs his new steps; he is renewed to proceed divinely and more lightly—that is, with greater ease and with a more effective vigor—toward the thickest forests, the deserts, and the region of incomprehensible things. From what he once was, a common and ordinary man, he becomes rare and **heroic** <note>Bruno often puns on "heroic" (eroico) and "erotic" (Eros), suggesting a love-driven quest for the divine.</note>; he possesses rare habits and ideas, and leads an extraordinary life. **HERE HIS MANY GREAT DOGS BRING HIM DEATH.** Here ends his life according to the mad, sensual, blind, and illusory <note>original: "phantastico"</note> world; and he begins to live **intellectually**. He lives the life of the gods, feeds on **ambrosia**, and becomes drunk on **nectar**. <meta>The previous page concluded the metaphor of Actaeon, the hunter who is transformed into the prey of his own divine thoughts. ### Fourth Dialogue.

> Go. May every deity of this vast architect <note>original: "ampio architetto"; a reference to the Creator or the divine designer of the universe.</note>
> Be forever merciful to you,
> And do not return to me unless you are mine.

---
<image-desc>A horizontal ornamental woodcut divider with symmetrical floral and foliate scroll designs.</image-desc>

The progress previously symbolized by the hunter who urges on his dogs is represented here by a **winged heart**. This heart is sent forth from the cage where it remained idle and quiet, to build its nest on high and to raise its **fledglings**—which are its thoughts. This occurs now that the time has come when those impediments, provided by a thousand external occasions and internal natural **frailty** <note>original: "imbecillitá"; in Renaissance Italian, this refers to human weakness or the lack of spiritual strength rather than intellectual disability.</note>, have ceased. He therefore releases the heart to grant it a more magnificent condition, dedicating it to a higher purpose and intent, now that those powers of the soul—which the **Platonists** <note>original: "Platoni"; refers to the followers of Plato, specifically regarding the "two wings" of the soul: Intellect and Will.</note> also symbolize as the **two wings**—are more firmly feathered. 

And he entrusts it to the guidance of that god whom the blind masses consider insane and blind: **Love**. Through the grace and favor of heaven, Love is powerful enough to transform the heart into that other nature to which it aspires, or into that state from which it wanders as an exile. Thus he said: *And do not return to me, for you are not mine*; so that I might, not without merit, say with that other poet:

> You have left me, my heart,
> And the light of my eyes is no longer with me.

<meta>This page continues the "Fourth Dialogue" mentioned at the end of the previous section. The text transitions from the metaphor of the winged heart to the mystical concept of the "death of the soul" through divine union, drawing on Kabbalistic and biblical traditions to explain the soul's total surrender to the divine object of its love.</meta>

### Fourth Dialogue.

Next, he describes the death of the soul which is called the **death by a kiss** by the **Kabbalists** <term>Cabalisti: scholars of Jewish mysticism; their ideas about the soul's union with God were very influential for Renaissance philosophers like Giordano Bruno.</term>. This is symbolized in the **Song of Solomon** <note>original: "cantica di Salomone"; a book of the Bible often interpreted as an allegory for the love between the soul and God.</note> where the beloved says:

> Let him kiss me with the kiss of his mouth,
> For with its wounding
> An all-too-cruel love makes me languish.

By others it is called "sleep," where the **Psalmist** <note>Refers to the author of the biblical Psalms.</note> says:

> If it happens that I give sleep to my eyes,
> And my eyelids slumber,
> I shall find peaceful rest in Him.

Thus the soul speaks, as if faint from being dead within herself and alive in the object of her love.

---

<image-desc>A decorative horizontal woodcut band featuring symmetrical floral and foliate scrollwork.</image-desc>

Take care, oh you **frenzied ones** <note>original: "furiosi"; refers to those gripped by "heroic frenzy," an intense spiritual or intellectual passion that drives the soul toward the divine.</note>, of your heart:
   For mine has wandered too far from me,
Led into a harsh and pitiless hand,
A joyful dwelling where it yearns and dies.
With my thoughts I call it back at every hour,
And it rebels against me like a wild **gyrfalcon** <term>girfalco: a large, fierce bird of prey. In this context, it represents the soul's wild, untamed desire that no longer obeys its former "master" (the body or the ego).</term>,
No longer recognizing that friendly hand,
From which, so as not to return, it has flown away.

<summary>This page explores the mystical "death by a kiss," a state of spiritual ecstasy where the soul "dies" to its worldly self to live within the divine. It uses the metaphor of a rebellious gyrfalcon to describe the heart's refusal to return to its former, limited state once it has experienced higher love.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the "Fourth Dialogue" of Giordano Bruno’s "The Heroic Frenzies." ### Fourth Dialogue.

->Beautiful beast who, in the midst of so many pains,<-
->Satisfies, and binds the heart, spirit, and soul<-
->With your stings, your flames, and your chains,<-
->Through your looks, your words, and your ways;<-
->He who languishes, burns, and does not recover—<-
->Who is he who could heal, cool, and untie him?<-

<image-desc>ornamental scrollwork divider with floral and vine motifs</image-desc>

There the grieving soul—not out of true discontent, but with the feeling of a certain **amorous martyrdom** <note>original: "amoroso martire"; a common Renaissance concept where the suffering of the lover is viewed as a sacred or refining experience.</note>—speaks as if addressing those who are similarly impassioned; as if, though not by its own happy will, it has given leave to the heart, which runs where it cannot arrive, stretches where it cannot reach, and wishes to embrace what it cannot comprehend. And because the heart moves away from the soul in vain, the soul ever more and more goes on kindling itself toward the **infinite**.

**CIC.** <note>Cicada, one of the two interlocutors in this dialogue.</note> Whence does it proceed, oh Tansillo, that in such progress the soul is satisfied with its torment? Whence comes that spur which always goads it beyond what it already possesses?

**TAN.** <note>Luigi Tansillo, a real-life poet whom Bruno uses as his primary philosophical mouthpiece.</note> From what I shall now tell you. The **intellect** having reached the apprehension of a certain and defined **intelligible form** <term>intelligibile: in Renaissance philosophy, this refers to concepts or truths that can be grasped by the mind, as opposed to physical objects seen by the eyes.</term>, and the **will** <note>original: "uolontá"; the faculty of the soul that desires and chooses.</note> having reached an affection commensurate with such an apprehension; the intellect does not stop there. This is because, by its own light, it is moved to think of that which contains within itself every kind of thing that can be understood and desired, until it comes to 
<meta>catchword: understand</meta>

<summary>In this opening to the Fourth Dialogue, Bruno explores the "heroic" nature of the soul's desire, explaining that the human mind and will are naturally driven to seek the infinite even when it causes a form of spiritual suffering.</summary>
<keywords>infinite, intellect, will, amorous martyrdom, Tansillo, Cicada, intelligible form, desire</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the "Fourth Dialogue" of Giordano Bruno’s "The Heroic Frenzies." Following the previous page, Tansillo is explaining how the human intellect is never satisfied with finite knowledge because it is drawn toward the infinite source of all things.</meta>

### Fourth Dialogue.

...grasp with the **intellect** the eminence of the source of ideas, the ocean of all truth and goodness. From this it follows that whatever concept <note>original: "specie"; in Renaissance philosophy, this refers to the mental image or form by which we understand an object.</note> is presented to it and understood by it: from the very fact that it is presented and understood, the intellect judges that above it there is another greater, and another greater still, finding itself always in discourse and motion in a certain way. 

For the intellect always sees that everything it possesses is a measured thing, and therefore cannot be sufficient by itself, nor good by itself, nor beautiful by itself; because it is not the universe, it is not the **absolute being**: but is contracted <note>Bruno uses "contracted" to mean limited or individualized, as opposed to the "absolute" which is undifferentiated and infinite.</note> to being this specific nature, this concept, this form represented to the intellect and present to the soul. Always, then, from the beauty it has understood—which is consequently measured, and consequently beautiful only by **participation** <term>participation: the Platonic doctrine that material things are only "good" or "beautiful" because they share in a small part of the ultimate, divine Essence.</term>—the soul makes progress toward that which is truly beautiful, which has no margin and no boundary whatsoever.

**CIC.** This pursuit seems vain to me.

**TAN.** On the contrary, it is not; given that it is neither natural nor fitting that the infinite be fully grasped, nor can it give itself as a finite thing, for then it would not be infinite. But it is fitting and natural that the infinite, by virtue of being infinite, be infinitely pursued. This is a mode of pursuit which does not have the character of physical motion, but of a certain **metaphysical motion**; it does not move from the imperfect to the perfect, but rather goes circling through the various degrees of perfection to reach that certain infinite which is neither formed nor form...

<meta>catchword: CIC</meta>

<summary>Tansillo explains that the human mind constantly seeks higher truths because every individual concept it grasps is limited, driving a "metaphysical motion" toward the boundaryless, infinite beauty of God.</summary>
<keywords>intellect, infinite, absolute being, participation, metaphysical motion, perfection, Giordano Bruno</keywords>

--- Page 100 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Fourth Dialogue" of Giordano Bruno’s "The Heroic Frenzies." Following the previous page, Tansillo and Cicada continue their discussion on the soul's pursuit of the infinite. They are exploring the paradox of why the soul feels "tortured" or "insane" when it seeks the ultimate truth, which is symbolized by the "center" of a circle that cannot be reached by merely traveling along its circumference.</meta>

### Fourth Dialogue.

**CIC.** I would like to know: how can one arrive at the center by circling around? 

**TAN.** I cannot know that. 

**CIC.** Why do you say that? 

**TA.** Because I am able to say it, and then leave it for you to consider. 

**CIC.** If you do not mean to say that he who pursues the infinite is like one who, traveling along the circumference, seeks the center, then I do not know what you mean. 

**TAN.** It is something else. 

**CIC.** Now, if you do not wish to explain yourself, I do not wish to understand you. But tell me, if you please, what is meant by the phrase that says the heart is led into a **HARSH and MERCILESS HAND**? 

**TA.** It intends a simile or metaphor taken from the common saying that one is "cruel" who does not allow themselves to be enjoyed, or at least not fully enjoyed; and who is more an object of desire than of possession. Thus, because of what a person possesses, they do not remain entirely happy, because they yearn, they agonize, and they die <note>Bruno is describing the "divine cruelty" of the infinite: because the human mind is finite, it can never fully "possess" or "consume" the infinite God, leading to a state of perpetual, painful longing.</note>.

**CICA.** What are those thoughts that call him back, to withdraw him from such a noble undertaking? 

**TAN.** The sensory affects <term>sensory affects: the emotions and drives related to physical survival and bodily pleasures.</term> and other natural impulses that look to the governance of the body. 

**CICA.** What do those have to do with this man [the seeker] who cannot help or favor them in any way? 

**TAN.** They do not concern him personally, but rather the soul, which, being too intent on one work or study, becomes lax and little concerned with the other <note>Bruno refers to the idea that the soul has a limited amount of "energy"; if it is entirely focused on divine contemplation, it neglects the physical maintenance of the body.</note>.

**CIC.** Why does he call him **AS ONE INSANE**? 

**TAN.** Because he "over-knows" <note>original: "soprasape"; a term Bruno uses to describe a state of transcendental knowledge that exceeds the boundaries of ordinary human reason or common sense.</note>. 

**CICA.** Usually those who know less are called insane. 

**TAN.** On the contrary, those are called insane who...

<summary>Tansillo explains that the soul's pursuit of the infinite leads to a "heroic" state of suffering and perceived madness because the divine cannot be fully possessed by a finite being. This pursuit causes the soul to neglect the physical body, appearing "insane" to those who follow only common reason.</summary>
<keywords>infinite, center and circumference, divine cruelty, sensory affects, over-knowing, heroic frenzy, madness vs. wisdom</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the "Fourth Dialogue" of Giordano Bruno’s "The Heroic Frenzies." ### Fourth Dialogue.

...those who do not know in the ordinary way, or who aim lower because they have less sense, or who aim higher because they have more intellect. **CICADA**: I realize you speak the truth. Now tell me further, what are the stings, the flames, and the chains? **TANSILLO**: **Stings** <note>original: *punte*. These are the sharp provocations or stimuli that goad the soul into action.</note> are those new things that stimulate and awaken the affection so that it might pay attention; **flames** are the rays of present beauty that ignite whoever waits for them; **chains** are the parts and circumstances that keep the eyes of attention fixed, and join the objects and the powers <term>powers: the faculties of the soul, such as intellect and will.</term> together. **CICADA**: What are the **GAZES, VOICES, AND MODES**?

**TANSILLO**: **Gazes** are the reasons by which the object (as if it were looking at us) makes itself present to us; **Voices** <note>original: *accenti*. This refers to the "tones" or "expressions" through which the divine object communicates.</note> are the reasons by which it inspires and informs us; **Modes** are the circumstances by which it always pleases and delights us. Thus the heart, which sweetly languishes, suavely burns, and constantly perseveres in its work, fears that its wound might heal, that its fire might be quenched, or that its bond might be loosened. **CICADA**: Now recite what follows. **TANSILLO**:

### Fourth Dialogue.

...so that they look upon no other beauty or goodness than that which is present to them, as a friend and mother <note>The soul is described as a "mother" to the physical body and its generation, implying a protective and creative relationship.</note>. And he concludes at the end that if they do not wish to return for any other duty, they should at least come back to give it a sample of the reasons and the state of its heart.

**CICADA:** Before you go any further, I would like to understand from you what the soul means when it says to the thoughts: *DO YOU REPRESS SIGHT* so strongly?

**TANSILLO:** I will tell you. All love proceeds from seeing; intelligible love <term>intelligible love: love directed toward things that can only be understood by the mind or intellect, such as truth or divine concepts.</term> from seeing intelligibly; and sensible love from seeing sensibly. Now, this "seeing" has two meanings: it either signifies the visual power <term>visual power: the latent faculty of sight, whether it be the physical eye or the "eye" of the mind (the intellect).</term>—that is, the sight, which is the intellect or the senses—or it signifies the *act* of that power, which is that application which the eye or the intellect makes toward the material or intellectual object. When, therefore, the thoughts are advised to repress sight, it is not meant in the first way <note>i.e., it is not asking the soul to lose its capacity for sight or intellect entirely.</note>, but in the second; because this act is the father of the subsequent affection of the sensitive <term>sensitive appetite: the natural drive or desire toward physical, sensory pleasures.</term> or intellectual appetite.

**CICADA:** This is exactly what I wanted to hear from you. Now, if the act of the visual power is the cause of the evil or good that proceeds from seeing, how does it happen that we love and desire to see? And why is it that in divine things we have more love than knowledge?

**TANSILLO:** We desire to see because in some way we see the goodness of seeing <note>original: *bonta del vedere*. Bruno suggests we have a meta-awareness of the value of sight itself.</note>; because we are informed that through the act...

<meta>This page continues the conversation between Tansillo and Cicada regarding the soul's command to "repress sight." ### Fourth Dialogue.
#### Page 7

...of seeing, beautiful things offer themselves: therefore we desire that act <note>The "act" refers to the specific moment of looking or perceiving, as opposed to the general ability to see.</note>, because we desire beautiful things. 

**CICADA:** We desire the beautiful and the good, but the act of seeing is neither beautiful nor good; rather, it is more a touchstone <note>original: "parangone." A touchstone is a tool used to test the purity of gold; here, Cicada argues that sight is merely a neutral tool for judgment.</note> or light through which we see not only the beautiful and good, but also the wicked and the ugly. Therefore it seems to me that the act of seeing can be called "beautiful" or "good" only as much as sight itself can be called "white" or "black." If, then, the sight (which is an act) is neither beautiful nor good in itself, how can it become an object of desire? 

**TANSILLO:** If it is not desired for its own sake, it is certainly desired for the sake of something else, since the apprehension of that "other thing" cannot happen without it. 

**CICADA:** What will you say if that "other thing" is not yet known by the senses nor the intellect? How, I ask, can it be desired—or at least desired to be seen—if there is no knowledge of it at all? If neither the intellect nor the senses have performed any act toward it? Indeed, if it is in doubt whether the thing is intelligible <term>intelligible: things that can only be understood by the mind, such as mathematical truths or divine concepts.</term> or sensible <term>sensible: things that can be perceived by the physical senses, like color, sound, or heat.</term>, whether it is a corporeal or incorporeal thing, whether it is one, two, or many, or of one kind or another?

**TANSILLO:** I respond that in the senses and the intellect there is a certain appetite and <term>impulsion: a natural, internal drive or "push" toward an object of interest.</term> toward the sensible in general. This is because the intellect wants to understand all truth, so that it may then grasp everything that is a beautiful or good "intelligible" thing; likewise, the sensitive power wants to be informed of everything "sensible," so that it may then grasp whatever is a good or beautiful "sensible" thing.

<meta>This page continues the dialogue from the previous page where Cicada questioned how one can desire something not yet known. ### Fourth Dialogue.

Hence it happens that we desire to see unknown and never-before-seen things no less than things known and seen. And from this it does not follow that desire does not proceed from knowledge, or that we desire something that is unknown; rather, I say it remains certain and firm that we do not desire unknown things. For if they are hidden in terms of their particular being, they are not hidden in terms of their general being—just as in the entire power of sight all that is visible is found in potential <note>original: "attitudine." In this philosophical context, it refers to a latent capacity or potentiality that is ready to be activated.</note>, and in the intellect all that is intelligible <term>intelligible: concepts or truths that can be grasped by the mind, as opposed to physical objects.</term>. Therefore, just as in potentiality there is an inclination toward the act, it happens that both powers <note>The "powers" of sight and intellect.</note> are inclined toward the act in a universal sense, as toward something naturally grasped as good. Thus, the soul was not speaking to the deaf or blind when it consulted with its thoughts about repressing sight—which, although it is not the immediate cause of the will, is nonetheless its first and primary cause.

**CICADA:** What do you mean by this last statement? 

**TANSILLO:** I mean that it is not the figure or the "species" <term>species: in Renaissance philosophy, the "species" is the mental image or formal essence of an object that is transmitted to the senses or the mind.</term> represented sensibly or intelligibly that moves [the soul] by itself. For while someone is gazing at a figure manifest to the eyes, they do not yet come to love; rather, [love begins] from that instant when the soul conceives within itself that figured thing—no longer as something visible but as something thinkable <note>original: "cogitabile."</note>, no longer divisible <note>original: "diuidua," referring to the way physical objects can be broken down or perceived in parts.</note> but indivisible <note>original: "indiuidua."</note>, no longer...

<meta>catchword: under (Italian: sotto)</meta>

<summary>Tansillo explains that desire always stems from a form of universal knowledge already present in the soul's potential. He argues that love is triggered not by the external sight of an object, but by the internal, indivisible mental concept the soul forms of it.</summary>
<keywords>desire, potentiality and act, universal knowledge, internal species, nature of love, intellect, sight</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue where Tansillo explains that the soul is moved not by an object itself, but by the "species" or mental image of the good and beautiful. He is now clarifying how this internal vision leads to an infinite desire.</meta>

### Fourth Dialogue.

...not as a mere thing, but under the aspect of the good or the beautiful: then, immediately, love is born. Now, this is that seeing from which the soul would like to avert the eyes of its thoughts. Here, sight is accustomed to move the affection to love more than what it actually sees; because, as I said a little while ago, the soul always considers (through the universal knowledge it holds of the beautiful and the good) that beyond the degrees of the perceived species <term>species: here, the mental image or "formal essence" of an object captured by the mind.</term> of the good and beautiful, there are others and others unto infinity.

**CICADA:** Why is it that after we have been informed of the species of beauty which is conceived in the soul, we still desire to feed our external sight?

**TANSILLO:** It comes from the fact that because the soul would always love that which it loves, it always wants to see that which it sees. Therefore, it wants that species which was born from its seeing not to become attenuated, weakened, or lost. It wishes, then, to see further and further, so that what might grow dark in the internal affection might be frequently illuminated by the external appearance; which, as it is the principle of its being, must also be the principle of its preservation. A similar thing happens in the act of understanding and considering; for just as sight relates to visible things, so the intellect relates to intelligible things. I believe, then, that you understand to what end and in what manner the soul intends when it says: **HE SHALL REPRESS HIS SIGHT** <note>original: "REPRIMET IL VEDERE". A hybrid phrase where "reprimet" is Latin for "he will repress."</note>.

**CICADA:** I understand very well. Now continue to relate what happened...

<summary>Tansillo explains that the soul seeks to refresh its internal love through external sight to prevent its mental images from fading, and notes that the intellect seeks the infinite good beyond what is immediately perceived.</summary>

->happiness<-

happiness, and misery. And why am I not left behind by this? Why does death not come to my aid, now that I am deprived of life? Up, up, O my fleeting thoughts, O my rebellious heart: let the sense of sensible things live, and the intellect of intelligible things. Let the body be succored with matter and its corporeal subject, and let the intellect be satisfied with its own objects; so that this composition may hold together, and this machine <note>machine: Bruno uses "machina" to describe the complex, functional structure of the human body-soul union</note>—where by means of the spirit the soul is joined to the body—may not dissolve. How wretchedly, through an internal struggle rather than external violence, must I witness this horrible divorce within my own parts and limbs? Why does the intellect trouble itself to give laws to the sense and deprive it of its food? And why does the sense, on the contrary, resist the intellect, wishing to live according to its own statutes and not according to those of another? For it is these [the senses], and not those [the laws of the intellect], that can maintain and bless it, because...

<meta>This page continues the Fourth Dialogue of Giordano Bruno’s "The Heroic Furors." must be attentive to its own comfort and life, not to that of others. There is no harmony or concord where there is unity <note>Bruno argues that true "harmony" requires different parts working together, rather than one part (the intellect) absorbing or destroying the others (the senses).</note>, where one being wants to absorb the whole being; but rather where there is order and analogy among diverse things; where every thing preserves its own nature. Let the sense, then, feed upon sensible things according to its own law; let the flesh serve the law of the flesh, the spirit the law of the spirit, and reason the law of reason: let them not be confounded, let them not be disturbed. It is enough that one does not ruin or prejudice the law of the other; just as it is not right for the sense to outrage the law of reason, it is also a shameful thing for the latter to tyrannize over the law of the former—especially where the intellect is more of a pilgrim and a stranger, and the sense is more at home, as if in its own country.

Behold then, O my thoughts, how some of you are obliged to remain in the care of the house, while others can go to seek provisions elsewhere. This is the law of nature; this consequently is the law of the author and principle of nature. You sin, then, now that—all seduced by the beauty of the intellect—you leave the other part of me to the peril of death. ### Fourth Dialogue.

...is it evil and human, or rather divine, if they are not gods but men and animals?

It is the law of fate and nature that everything should operate according to the condition of its own being. Why then, while you pursue the stingy nectar of the gods <note>A reference to divine knowledge or immortality, which Bruno suggests is "stingy" because it is so difficult for mortals to attain.</note>, do you lose your own present and proper state, perhaps afflicting yourselves with the empty hope of someone else's? Do you believe that nature would not feel indignant at giving you the next blessing, if you so foolishly despise that which she offers you right now?

->Heaven will disdain to grant the second grace<-
->To him who does not hold the first gift dear.<-

With these and similar arguments, the soul, taking up the cause of the weaker part <note>The "weaker part" refers to the physical body and the sensory life, which the Soul feels are being unfairly abandoned by the mind's higher aspirations.</note>, seeks to recall her thoughts to the care of the body. But those thoughts (though late) return to show themselves to her—not in the form they had when they departed, but only to declare their rebellion and to force her entirely to follow them. Whereupon the grieving Soul laments in this fashion:

> Ah, dogs of Actaeon, oh ungrateful beasts,
> Which I directed toward my goddess’s retreat,
> And you return to me void of hope,
> Or rather, coming back to the maternal shore, <note>In this allegory, Actaeon represents the human intellect. His "dogs" are his thoughts. After seeing the divine (the goddess Diana), the thoughts turn back upon the person, "devouring" their old human self to transform them into something divine.</note>

->And swiftly lift myself to the high object;<-

<image-desc>Decorative typographic ornament frieze with symmetrical floral scrolls and stylized leaf patterns.</image-desc>

The Platonists <note>Followers of Plato and later Neoplatonic thinkers, like Plotinus, who believed the soul occupied a middle ground between the material and the divine.</note> maintain that the soul, as far as its higher part is concerned, always consists in the **intellect**, where it has the quality of intelligence rather than soul; for it is named "**soul**" [anima] only insofar as it vivifies and sustains the body. Thus, in this case, the same essence which nourishes and maintains the thoughts on high along with the *exalted heart*, is induced by the lower part to grieve and to recall those thoughts as rebels. 

**CICADA.** So they are not two contrary essences, but one essence subject to two points of contrariety? 

**TANSILLO.** Exactly so; just like a ray of the sun which, on one end, touches the earth and is joined to lower and dark things which it illuminates, vivifies, and kindles; and on the other end, is joined to the element of fire—that is, to the star <note>In Bruno’s cosmology, the Sun is the "star" from which the ray proceeds.</note> from which it proceeds, has its beginning, is diffused, and in which it has its own and original subsistence. So too is the soul, which is situated on the horizon of nature <term>horizon of nature: a philosophical concept describing the human soul as the boundary line between the eternal/spiritual world and the temporal/material world</term>...

<meta>catchword: ra cor- [likely "natura corporea" or "corporeal nature"]</meta>

<summary>In this passage, the Soul laments its division between earthly life and divine aspiration, while the character Tansillo explains the Neoplatonic view that the soul acts as a bridge (or horizon) between the material body and the higher intellect.</summary>

### Fourth Dialogue.

...[between] corporeal and incorporeal nature, she <note>The soul is traditionally referred to with feminine pronouns in this context.</note> has the means to rise to higher things and incline toward lower things. And you can see that this does not happen by reason or order of local motion <note>Physical movement through space.</note>; but only through the impulse of one or another power or faculty. Just as when sense rises to imagination, imagination to reason, reason to intellect, and intellect to the mind; then the whole soul turns toward God and inhabits the **intelligible world** <note>The realm of pure ideas and divine truth, as opposed to the physical world.</note>. Conversely, she descends by turning toward the **sensible world** <note>The physical world perceived by the senses.</note> by way of the intellect, reason, imagination, sense, and vegetation <term>vegetatione: the lowest level of soul-power, responsible for basic life functions like growth and nutrition</term>.

**CICADA.** It is true that I have heard that since the soul finds itself at the lowest rank of divine things, it deservedly descends into the mortal body, and from this it rises again to the divine ranks. And that there are three ranks of intelligences: for there are some in which the intellectual part surpasses the animal part—which they say are the celestial intelligences; others in which the animal part surpasses the intellectual—which are human intelligences; and still others in which the one and the other carry themselves equally, like those of the **demons** or **heroes** <term>heroi: semi-divine beings or souls striving for the divine; from the Greek "heros"</term>.

**TANSILLO.** In the learning that the mind does, therefore, it cannot desire anything except what is near, close, known, and familiar to it. Thus a pig cannot desire to be a man, nor those things that are suitable to human appetite. It loves more to wallow in the mud than in a bed of fine linen <term>original: "biſſino" (byssus), an ancient and exceptionally fine silk-like fabric</term>; it loves to unite with a sow, not with the most beautiful woman that nature produces...

<summary>Tansillo explains the soul's ability to move between divine and material realms through its various faculties, while Cicada categorizes different types of intelligences (celestial, human, and heroic).</summary>

### Fourth Dialogue.

...which, turning to her [the soul] late, come to pull her upward rather than to be received by her. And here, because of the distraction <term>distraction: in this philosophical sense, the state of being pulled in opposite directions (Latin: *distractio*)</term> she suffers from the common love of matter and of intelligible things, she feels herself torn and ripped apart in such a way that she must ultimately yield to the more vigorous and strong impulse. Here, if through the power of contemplation she ascends or is snatched above the horizon of natural affections—where with a purer eye she might learn the difference between the one life and the other—then, conquered by high thoughts, as if dead to the body, she aspires to the heights. And although she lives in the body, she vegetates there as if dead; she is present there in the act of giving life <gloss>animatione: the basic biological function of the soul in "animating" the body</gloss> but absent in the act of her operations <note>The higher functions of the intellect and will that operate independently of the body.</note>. This is not because she does not operate while the body is alive, but because the operations of the composite <note>The "composite" refers to the human being as a union of both body and soul.</note> are weakened, sluggish, and as if disregarded.

**CIC.** Just so, a certain theologian (who said he was snatched up even to the third heaven <note>A reference to the Apostle Paul, who in 2 Corinthians 12:2-4 describes being "caught up to the third heaven."</note>), enamored by the sight of it, said that he desired the dissolution of his body. 

**TAN.** In this way, where before he lamented for his heart and complained of his thoughts, now he desires to rise with them on high. He shows his regret for the communication and familiarity contracted with corporeal matter, and says: 

**L E A V E   M E**, corporeal life, and do not hinder me from re-ascending to my more native home, to my sun! Leave me now, so that I no longer pour tears from my eyes—either because I can hardly help them, or because I remain

### Fourth Dialogue.

...separated from my good; leave me, for it is neither fitting nor possible that these two streams <note>The "two streams" or "rivers of tears" refer to the eyes of the lover.</note> should flow without their source—that is, without the heart. I should not (I say) create two rivers of tears down here if my heart, which is the source of such rivers, has flown up on high with its nymphs, which are my thoughts. Thus, little by little, from that lack of love <note>original: "disamore"; here meaning a detachment or withdrawal of affection from worldly things.</note> and that regret, one proceeds to the hatred of inferior things. This is almost shown by the saying: *WHEN SHALL IT BE that nature dissolves my heavy burden?* <note>original: "QVAND' IL MIO PONDO greue". The "heavy burden" is a poetic and philosophical reference to the physical body which weighs down the soul.</note> and that which follows thereafter.

**CIC.** I understand this very well, and also what you wish to infer by it regarding your main intention: namely, that there are levels of loves, affections, and furies <term>furies: in Bruno's philosophy, "heroic furies" are not fits of anger, but states of intense, divine inspiration or intellectual passion that drive the soul toward truth.</term>, according to the degrees of greater or lesser light of knowledge and intelligence.

**TAN.** You understand correctly. From this, you must learn that doctrine which—commonly taken from the Pythagoreans <term>Pythagoreans: followers of the ancient philosopher Pythagoras, known for their beliefs in the mathematical harmony of the universe and the transmigration of souls.</term> and Platonists—holds that the soul makes two progresses, of ascent and descent <gloss>ascenso et descenso: the spiritual journey of rising toward the divine or falling toward material existence.</gloss>, through the care it has for itself and for matter; both because it is moved by its own appetite for the good, and because it is driven by the providence of fate.

**CIC.** But please tell me briefly what you understand regarding the soul of the world <term>soul of the world: *anima mundi*, the concept that the entire universe is a single living organism with a unifying spirit.</term>: can it not also ascend or descend?

**TAN.** If you ask about the "world" according to the common meaning—that is, insofar as it signifies the universe—I say that because it is infinite and without dimension or measure,

### Fourth Dialogue.

...comes to be immobile, inanimate, and formless, even though it serves as the place for infinite mobile worlds within it, and possesses infinite space where there are so many great animals that are called stars <term>stars: Bruno famously proposed that stars were not points of light on a fixed sphere, but suns like our own, which he considered "great animals" because they possessed their own souls and internal principles of motion.</term>. If you ask according to the meaning used by true philosophers—that is, insofar as "soul" signifies every globe and every star, such as this Earth, the body of the Sun, the Moon, and others—I say that such a soul neither ascends nor descends, but turns in a circle. Thus, being composed of superior and inferior powers: with the superior powers it turns toward divinity, and with the inferior ones it turns toward the physical mass <note>original: "mole"; refers to the bulk or material body of the planet/star.</note> which is vivified and maintained by it within the tropics of generation and corruption <term>generation and corruption: a traditional philosophical term for the cycle of birth and death, or the constant changing of matter in the physical world.</term> of the living things in those worlds. It preserves its own life eternally, because the act of divine providence always, with the same measure and order, and with divine heat and light, preserves them in their ordinary and identical being.

**CIC.** It is enough for me to have heard this regarding that matter.

**TAN.** Just as it happens, then, that these particular souls are affected in various ways regarding their habits and inclinations according to different degrees of ascent and descent, so they come to show different manners and orders of furies, loves, and senses. This occurs not only in the scale of nature according to the orders of different lives that the soul takes in different bodies—as the Pythagoreans <term>Pythagoreans: followers of Pythagoras who believed in the transmigration of souls (metempsychosis), where a soul might inhabit different animal or human bodies over time.</term>, Sadducees <note>original: "Saduchimi"; here Bruno refers to them in the context of ancient sects discussing the soul's journey, though historically their views on the afterlife were contested.</note>, and others expressly maintain—

<meta>catchword: et (and)</meta>

<summary>Tansillo explains that while the infinite universe is immobile, the individual worlds (stars and planets) within it are living beings that rotate in place, balancing their attention between divine intelligence and the physical matter they animate.</summary>

<keywords>infinite space, stars as animals, divine providence, generation and corruption, circular motion, Pythagoreans, transmigration of souls</keywords>

### Dialogue Four.

...and implicitly Plato, and some who delve even deeper into his thought; but also in the scale of human affects <term>affects: the various emotions, passions, and psychological states that move the soul.</term>, which is as numerous in its degrees as the scale of nature, seeing as man, in all his powers, exhibits every species of being.

**CIC.** Therefore, by their affects, one can know whether souls are moving upward or downward, or if they come from above or below—whether they are advancing toward becoming beasts or toward being divine. This might be according to their specific essence <note>original: "essere specifico"; refers to the actual transformation of the soul's nature.</note>, as the Pythagoreans <term>Pythagoreans: followers of Pythagoras who believed in metempsychosis, the transmigration of souls into different animal or human bodies based on their conduct.</term> understood it; or merely according to the similarity of their affects, as is commonly believed. For the human soul ought not to be able to become the soul of a brute <note>original: "bruto"; referring to non-human animals lacking reason.</note>, as Plotinus and other Platonists well said, following the judgment of their master <note>original: "suo principe"; referring to Plato.</note>.

**TAN.** Quite right. Now, to come to our point: from animal fury, this soul we have described is promoted to fury; it is called "heroic" <term>heroic fury: a state of divine madness or intense intellectual passion where the soul, driven by love, seeks to transcend the material world to contemplate the infinite.</term>. **WHEN WILL IT BE** that I raise myself to that high object, and dwell there in the company of my heart and my chicks <note>The "chicks" (pulcini) are a metaphor for the soul's young thoughts or nascent intellectual desires.</note>—mine and his alike? This same theme continues when he says:

> **F**ate, when will you grant that I climb the mount,
> Which, to bless me, to the high gates carries me,
> Which make those famous beauties known;
> And let the tenacious sorrow strongly comfort
> He who made my disjointed limbs joined,
> Nor leave my pale powers dead?
<note>In the original Italian, Bruno uses "equivocal rhymes" (rhyming a word with itself but with a different meaning): *monte* (mountain) / *monte* (climb); *porte* (gates) / *porte* (carries); *conte* (famous) / *conte* (recounted/known); *conforte* (comfort) / *forte* (strongly); *gionte* (disjointed/joined) / *gionte* (arrived/reached); *morte* (pale/dimmed) / *morte* (dead).</note>

O destiny, O fate, O divine immutable providence, when will it be that I climb that mountain—that is, when will I reach such height of mind—that allows me to touch, by transporting me, those high entrances and inner shrines <term>inner shrines: original "penetrali," referring to the most secret or sacred parts of a temple; here used to describe the deepest mysteries of the divine.</term>? These places make evident and, as if understood and numbered, those "known"—that is, rare beauties. When will it be that he who joined and united my members that were disunited and disjointed <note>Bruno is referring to the concept of the soul as the principle that binds the "contrary" physical elements (like heat and cold) into a living body.</note>—namely, that Love which has joined together these bodily parts which were as divided as one contrary is divided from another—strongly and effectively comforts my pain (loosening me from the tightest bonds of cares in which I find myself)? And when will he ensure that these intellectual powers <term>intellectual powers: the faculties of the soul, such as reason and intuition, which allow for the contemplation of higher truths.</term>, which in their current actions are faint, are not left entirely dead, but are made to aspire upward by breathing a little? When, I say, will he comfort me fully by granting these powers a free and unimpeded flight, through which I might—

### Dialogue Four.

...my whole substance to nestle itself there where, by exerting myself, it is necessary for me to mend all my faults; where, once my spirit arrives, it is worth more than its rival <note>The "rival" refers to the lower, material nature or the physical body that competes with the spirit's higher aspirations.</note>; because there is no outrage that resists it, no contrariety that conquers it, no error that assaults it. Oh, if it reaches and arrives there where, by straining, it waits; and ascends, and reaches that height where its object <note>In Bruno’s philosophy, the "object" of the intellect is truth or the divine essence itself.</note> wishes to remain mounted, high, and elevated; if it should happen to take hold of that good which cannot be understood by any other than one—that is, by itself (since every other possesses it only in the measure of their own capacity, and only that One possesses it in all fullness); then it will happen that I am happy in that way described by him who foretells all things—that is, he speaks from that height in which saying all and doing all are the same thing; in that way spoken or done by him who foretells all, that is, who is the <term>efficient cause: the primary source of change or rest; in this context, God as the creator and sustainer of the universe.</term> and principle of all things, whose saying or preordaining is the true doing and beginning. Behold how the affection of love proceeds through the ladder of superior and inferior things, just as the intellect or feeling proceeds from these intelligible or knowable objects to those, or from those to these. 

<term>CICADA</term>: Thus most of the wise believe that nature takes pleasure in this <term>alternating circulation: original "vicissitudinale circolatione," referring to the constant change and movement of the universe where things rise and fall in a cycle.</term> seen in the whirling of her wheel.

# Fifth Dialogue.

->I. CICADA.<-

<image-desc>An ornate historiated initial "F" depicting a standing male figure in classical or noble attire, looking toward a landscape background. The scene is framed within a square border.</image-desc>

**F**orce yourself to let me see, so that I might consider for myself the conditions of these "furors" <note>The "heroic furors" are intense, passionate states of the soul—driven by love and intellect—that seek to transcend the material world to reach divine truth.</note> through what appears explained in the order (in this struggle <note>original: "militia." Bruno uses military terminology to describe the soul's disciplined effort and "warfare" against lower impulses.</note>) described here. 

TAN. See how they carry the emblems of their passions or fortunes. Let us leave aside the consideration of their names and attire; it is enough that we focus on the meaning of the **emblems** <term>imprese: a Renaissance symbolic device consisting of a pictorial image (the "body") and a short motto (the "soul"), used to express a personal philosophy or state of mind.</term> and the understanding of the writing—both that which is placed as the form of the "body" of the image, and the other which is placed, for the most part, to clarify the emblem. 

CIC. So we shall do. Now, here is the first one, who carries a shield divided into four colors, where on the crest is depicted a flame beneath a bronze head, from the apertures of which a smoky wind exits with great force; and there is written in

<meta>This page continues the Fifth Dialogue of "The Heroic Furors." Following Cicada's request to see the emblems of these spiritual passions, Tansillo begins to interpret the first device. The previous page established that the "imprese" (emblems) consist of both an image (the body) and a motto (the soul) to illustrate the internal state of the lover-philosopher.</meta>

# Fifth Dialogue.

...concerning, **BUT THE THREE KINGDOMS FELT IT.** <note>original Latin: "AT REGNA SENSERUNT TRIA." The "three kingdoms" refer to the three elements—fire, water, and air—that are affected by the lover's intense emotion.</note> **TAN.** To explain this, I would say that since the fire is there—which, as can be seen, heats the globe containing the water—it happens that this moist element, being rarefied and thinned by the power of the heat and consequently transformed into vapor, requires much greater space to be contained. Hence, if it does not find an easy exit, it breaks the vessel with the greatest force, noise, and destruction. But if there is a place or an easy exit from which it can evaporate, it issues forth with less violence, little by little; and according to the measure by which the water turns into vapor, it exhales into the air with a whistling sound.

Here is signified the heart of the "furioso" <note>In Bruno’s philosophy, the "furioso" is the "heroic enthusiast"—a person whose soul is driven by a divinely-inspired passion to transcend earthly limits.</note>, where, as if onto well-prepared tinder <note>original: "esca," meaning bait or tinder.</note>, the amorous fire has caught. It follows that of his vital substance <note>The "vital substance" refers to the "pneuma" or spirit-body that mediates between the physical soul and the body in Renaissance physiology.</note>, some sparks into fire, some is seen boiling in the breast in the form of tearful weeping, and some ignites the air through the exit of windy sighs.

And therefore it says: **BUT THE THREE KINGDOMS FELT IT.**
Where that word, **AT** <note>original: "AT," a Latin conjunction used here to emphasize contrast or a turning point.</note>, has the power to imply difference, or diversity, or contrariety; as if to say that there is something else that might have had a sense of the same thing, but does not. This is very well explained in the following rhymes beneath the figure.

## II. TANSILLO.

Next is depicted one who has on his shield, likewise divided into four colors, a crest where there is a sun spreading its rays onto the back of the earth. And there is a motto that says: **THE SAME ALWAYS, EVERYWHERE, THE WHOLE** <note>original Latin: "IDEM SEMPER VBIQVE TOTVM"</note>. **CIC.** I see that the interpretation cannot be easy. **TAN.** The meaning is all the more excellent as it is less common; you will see that it is singular, unique, and not far-fetched. You must consider that although the sun, in relation to different regions of the earth, appears different to each—from time to time, from place to place, and from part to part—yet with respect to the entire globe as a single entity, it always and in every single place performs its whole work. This is because at whatever point of the ecliptic <term>ecliptic: the apparent path of the sun through the heavens over the course of a year</term> it may be found, it is simultaneously creating winter, summer, autumn, and spring; and the universal globe of the earth is receiving within itself these four seasons <note>original: "tempeste," here used to mean the four divisions of the year or climatic states</note>. For it is never hot in one part without being cold in another; just as when it is hottest for us in the Tropic of Cancer, it is coldest at the Tropic of Capricorn, so that by the same logic it is winter in that part while it is summer in this one, and for those who are in between, the climate is temperate, according to the vernal or autumnal disposition. Thus the earth al-

...present are the rains, the winds, the heat, the cold; indeed, it would not be humid here if it did not dry out somewhere else, and the sun would not heat this side if it had not ceased to heat the other. **CIC.** Before you finish concluding, I understand what you mean to say. # Fifth Dialogue.

### IIII. CICADA.

**CICADA.** What is the meaning of that fly <note>The Italian "mosca" here refers to a moth, a common Renaissance symbol for the soul drawn to the light of God or Truth.</note> flying around the flame, appearing almost on the point of burning itself? And what does that motto mean: **ENEMY, NOT ENEMY** <note>original: "HOSTIS NON HOSTIS"</note>? **TANSILLO.** The meaning of the butterfly <note>original: "farfalla"</note> is not very difficult; seduced by the beauty of the brilliance, innocent and friendly, it goes to run into the death-dealing flames. Therefore, **ENEMY** is written because of the effect of the fire, but **NOT ENEMY** because of the affection of the fly. The fire is an enemy to the fly passively <note>meaning the fly is the victim of the fire's heat</note>, but not an enemy actively <note>meaning the fly does not approach the fire with hostile intent</note>. The flame is an enemy for its heat, but not an enemy for its splendor. **CICADA.** Now, what is written on the tablet? **TANSILLO.**

---

<image-desc>Decorative horizontal woodcut divider with symmetrical floral scrollwork.</image-desc>

**M**ay it never be that I complain of love,
*Without which I do not wish to be happy;*
*Even if it be true that through it I endure painful hardship,*
*I do not want to not-want what is so permitted to me;*
*Whether the sky be clear or dark, cold or burning,*
*I will always be one and the same toward the unique phoenix;* <note>The "Phoenix" represents the singular, incorruptible object of the lover's intellectual pursuit—the Truth or the Divine.</note>
*Hardly can any other destiny or fate undo*
*That knot which death itself cannot loosen.*
->To the heart, to the spirit, to the soul<-
*There is no pleasure, or liberty, or life,*

Here in the figure is shown the similarity that the enthusiast <note>The Italian *furioso* refers to the "heroic lover," a person driven by a divine frenzy or intellectual passion for Truth.</note> has with the moth <note>original: *farfalla*</note> attracted toward its light. In the verses, however, he shows more difference and dissimilarity than anything else; for it is commonly believed that if that insect <note>original: *mosca*, literally "fly," but used here for the moth.</note> foresaw its ruin, it would not follow the light so much as it would then flee from it, thinking it a great evil to lose its own being by dissolving in that enemy fire. But to this man, it is no less pleasing to vanish in the flames of amorous ardor than to be caught up <note>original: *abstratto*, meaning "abstracted" or "drawn away" in a contemplative trance.</note> in contemplating the beauty of that rare splendor. Under this splendor—by the inclination of nature, by the choice <note>original: *elettion*</note> of the will, and by the disposition of fate—he toils, serves, and dies. He does so more joyfully, more resolutely, and more vigorously than under any other pleasure that might be offered to the heart, any liberty granted to the spirit, or any life found in the soul.

**CICADA.** Tell me, why does he say: **ALWAYS** I shall be one? <note>Referring to the lover’s constancy and unwavering focus on the one divine object.</note>

**TANSILLO.** Because it seems worthy to him to provide a reason for his constancy; considering that while the wise man changes with the moon, the fool changes like— <note>Tansillo is cut off here, but he references the biblical and philosophical proverb *Stultus mutatur ut luna* (The fool changes like the moon), contrasting the vacillation of the worldly man with the constancy of the heroic lover.</note>

<summary>Tansillo explains that while a moth dies accidentally by the flame, the heroic lover consciously chooses to "die" (surrender his ego) in the light of divine beauty, finding this sacrifice more joyful than ordinary life. The dialogue transitions to the theme of the lover's absolute constancy.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the "Fifth Dialogue" of Giordano Bruno’s *The Heroic Furors*. The previous page introduced an analogy where the lower powers of the soul (the senses and passions) are compared to a local army defending its home territory against the "foreign" intervention of the higher intellect.</meta>

...finds itself in its own country—practiced, expert, and well-equipped—it rises up against the foreign adversary who descends from the mountain of intelligence to restrain the peoples of the valleys and marshy plains. There, due to the harsh presence of enemies and the difficulty of steep ditches, these higher thoughts would go astray and be lost entirely, were there not a certain turning toward the splendor of the *intelligible species* <term>intelligible species: the pure mental form of an object as understood by the mind, free from physical matter; original: "specie intelligibile"</term> through the act of contemplation. In this way, the soul turns from the lower levels to the higher levels.

**CICADA.** What levels are these?

**TANSILLO.** The levels of contemplation are like the degrees of light. Light is not present at all in darkness; it is present somewhat in shadow; it is seen better in colors, according to their order, from one opposite, which is black, to the other, which is white. It is present more effectively in the splendor diffused over polished and transparent bodies, such as in a mirror or the moon; more vividly in the rays scattered from the sun; and highest and most principally in the sun itself. 

Now, since the apprehensive <note>the faculty of perceiving or knowing; original: "potenze apprensiue"</note> and affective <note>the faculty of feeling or desiring; original: "potenze affettiue"</note> powers are ordered so that each subsequent power always has an affinity with the one immediately preceding it, by turning toward the one that elevates it, the soul is reinforced against the lower power that weighs it down. (Just as reason, by turning toward the intellect <note>In Bruno's philosophy, "reason" is the logical, step-by-step mind, while "intellect" is the higher faculty that grasps universal truths directly.</note>, is not seduced or defeated by sensory knowledge or physical desire, but rather, according to the law of the intellect, it comes to tame and correct the senses.) It happens that when the appe-

<meta>This page continues the "Fifth Dialogue" of Giordano Bruno’s *The Heroic Furors*. Following the previous page's discussion on the soul's ascent through various degrees of light, Tansillo is now explaining how the rational part of the soul overcomes the lower, sensory desires by turning toward divine light.</meta>

# Fifth Dialogue.

the reasoning spirit <note>original: "tito rationale," likely a transcription error for "lo spirito rationale"</note> clashes with sensual concupiscence <term>sensual concupiscence: the strong desire for physical or worldly pleasures; original: "concupiscenza sensuale"</term>. If the light of the intellect presents itself to the mind's eye through an act of conversion <note>a "turning" or redirection of the soul's focus toward higher things; original: "atto di conversione"</note>, reason recovers its lost virtue and strengthens its resolve <note>literally "strengthens the nerves"</note>, terrifying and routing its enemies.

**CICADA.** In what manner do you mean this conversion takes place? 

**TANSILLO.** Through three preparations noted by the contemplative Plotinus <note>a major Neoplatonist philosopher (c. 204–270 AD)</note> in his book *On Intelligible Beauty* <note>referring to *Ennead* V.8</note>. The first is to resolve to conform oneself to a divine likeness by turning one’s gaze away from things that are below one's own perfection, and common to equal and inferior species. Second is to apply oneself with all intention and attention to the higher species <note>the pure, spiritual forms of reality</note>. Third is to captivate the entire will and affection toward God. For from this it follows that, without doubt, the divinity—who is present everywhere and ready to instill Himself in those who turn to Him through the act of the intellect and expose themselves openly to Him through the affection of the will—shall flow into them. 

**CICADA.** Is it not, then, corporeal beauty that charms this man? 

**TANSILLO.** Certainly not, because that is not true nor constant beauty, and therefore it cannot cause true or constant love. The beauty seen in bodies is an accidental <term>accidental: a quality that is not essential to the nature of a thing and can change or disappear; original: "accidentale"</term> and shadowy thing; like other things that are absorbed, altered, and spoiled by the changing of the subject <note>the physical body</note>, which often turns from beautiful to ugly without any change occurring in the soul. The reason

<summary>Tansillo explains the three Neoplatonic steps for the soul to transcend physical desire and reach divine light, arguing that physical beauty is merely a "shadowy" accident compared to true intellectual beauty.</summary>
<keywords>Plotinus, Enneads, intelligible beauty, spiritual conversion, intellect vs. will, corporeal beauty, Neoplatonism</keywords>

--- Page 133 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Fifth Dialogue" of Giordano Bruno’s *The Heroic Furors*. Tansillo is explaining the process of Neoplatonic ascent, where the soul moves from the beauty of the body to the beauty of the soul, and finally to the divine intellect.</meta>

# Fifth Dialogue.

thus it learns the truest beauty by turning toward that which creates beauty in the body and comes to shape it as beautiful; and this is the soul <term>soul: in Bruno's philosophy, the soul is the "internal artist" that shapes the physical body from within; original: "anima"</term> which has thus fashioned and formed it. Next, the intellect rises higher and understands well that the soul is incomparably beautiful above any beauty that can exist in bodies. However, the intellect is not convinced that the soul is beautiful in itself and by its own nature <note>original: "da per se, et primitiuamente"</note>, considering that the difference seen in the nature of souls would not then occur—where some are wise, lovable, and beautiful, while others are foolish, hateful, and ugly. 

It is necessary, therefore, to rise to that superior intellect which is beautiful in itself and good in itself. This is that unique and supreme captain, who, placed alone before the eyes of militant thoughts <note>the "militant thoughts" are the soul's internal struggles and reasoning processes as it strives for divine truth</note>, illuminates, encourages, and strengthens them, rendering them victorious through the contempt of every other beauty and the rejection of any other good. This, then, is the presence that allows one to overcome every difficulty and conquer every violence. 

**CICADA.** I understand it all. But what does it mean: ***She governs me in peace; nor does she make that snare or that torch cease?*** <note>Cicada is quoting a verse, likely from a poem previously discussed in the text; "snare" and "torch" are traditional metaphors for the binding and burning power of love</note>

**TANSILLO.** He means and proves that whatever kind of love it may be, the greater its power <note>original: "imperio"</note> and the more certain its dominion, the tighter it makes the snares felt, the firmer the yoke, and the more burning the flames. This is unlike ordinary princes and tyrants, who use greater constraint and force where they see they have less power. 

**CICADA.** Pass on.

-> VI. <-
<meta>catchword: TAN</meta>

<summary>Tansillo explains how the intellect ascends from the beauty of the body to the soul, and finally to the supreme Intellect (God), which acts as a "captain" for the mind's thoughts. He also describes how divine love, unlike earthly tyranny, exerts its greatest power through a peaceful yet total dominion over the soul.</summary>
<keywords>Neoplatonism, soul, intellect, divine beauty, militant thoughts, love, Giordano Bruno, Tansillo</keywords>
