<meta>This page continues the "Fifth Dialogue" of Giordano Bruno’s *The Heroic Furors*. Following the poem describing the Phoenix and the Lover, Tansillo begins to interpret the symbolic differences between the two, focusing on the themes of reincarnation, the nature of the soul, and the levels of the human intellect.</meta>

# Fifth Dialogue.

**THEY OPPOSE** <note>original Latin: "OBSTANT"</note> is not meant to signify that the fates are contrary either to the youth, or to the phoenix, or to both; but that the fatal decrees of the one and the fatal decrees of the other are not the same, but diverse and opposite. For the phoenix is what it was, since the same matter is renewed through fire to be the body of a phoenix, and the same spirit and soul come to inform it; the frenzied lover <term>frenzied lover: "il furioso," the individual driven by "heroic" passion for divine knowledge</term> is that which he was not, because the subject that is now a man was previously of some other species according to countless differences. Thus, one knows what the phoenix was and knows what it will be; but this subject <note>referring to the human soul or individual</note> cannot return to clothe itself in the same or similar natural form except through many and uncertain means. 

Furthermore, the phoenix in the presence of the sun exchanges death for life; and this man, in the presence of love, exchanges life for death. Moreover, she <note>the phoenix</note> lights the fire upon the aromatic altar, while he finds it and carries it with him wherever he goes. She also has fixed terms for a long life; but he, through infinite differences of time and innumerable causes of circumstances, has uncertain terms for a brief life. She burns with certainty, he with doubt of seeing the sun again.

CICERO. What do you believe this could symbolize?

TANSILLO. The difference that exists between the lower intellect—which they call the intellect of power <term>intellect of power: "intelletto di potenza," also known as the potential or "possible" intellect; the capacity of the human mind to receive information and become all things</term>, or the possible or passible intellect, which is uncertain, multivariable, and multiform—and the superior intellect, which perhaps is...

<image-desc>Circular red ink library stamp in the bottom left corner from the "BIBLIOTHÈQUE IMPÉRIALE" (now the Bibliothèque nationale de France), featuring an imperial eagle emblem in the center.</image-desc>

G.
<meta>catchword: which</meta>

<summary>Tansillo explains the symbolic contrast between the Phoenix, which undergoes a predictable and identical rebirth, and the "heroic lover," whose soul undergoes uncertain transformations. This serves as an allegory for the difference between the "potential" human intellect and a higher, more stable divine intellect.</summary>
<keywords>Phoenix, reincarnation, heroic frenzy, potential intellect, fatal decrees, transformation of the soul, Giordano Bruno</keywords>

--- Page 136 ---
<meta>This page continues Tansillo’s explanation of the symbolic difference between the phoenix and the human lover. He is currently distinguishing between the various levels of the "intellect," comparing the universal human intellect to the moon and the individual human mind to the eyes.</meta>

...what is called by the **Peripatetics** <term>Peripatetics: Followers of Aristotle’s philosophy, named for his habit of walking while teaching</term> the lowest of the intelligences; and which immediately influences all individuals of the human species, and is called the **active and actuating intellect** <term>active intellect: "intelletto agente," the faculty that transforms potential knowledge into actual understanding</term>. This unique, specific human intellect, which has influence over all individuals, is like the **moon**, which takes no other form than that single one, which is always renewed by its turning toward the **sun**, which is the first and universal intelligence.

But the individual and plural human intellect comes, like the eyes, to turn toward innumerable and most diverse objects; thus, it is informed according to infinite degrees that correspond to all natural forms. Wherefore it happens that this particular intellect is frenzied, wandering, and uncertain; just as that universal one is quiet, stable, and certain, both in its **appetite** <term>appetite: "appetito," the driving desire or will toward an object</term> and in its **perception** <term>perception: "apprensione," the mental grasping of a concept</term>. 

Or rather, from this (as you yourself can easily decipher) is signified the nature of the varied, wandering, inconstant, and uncertain perception and desire of the **senses**; and of the defined, firm, and stable concept and desire of the **intelligence**. This is the difference between **sensual love**—which has neither certainty nor discrimination of objects—and **intellectual love**—which aims at a certain and single object, toward which it turns, by which it is illuminated in its concept, and from which it is kindled in its affection, becoming inflamed, enlightened, and maintained in unity, identity, and state.

<meta>catchword: CIC.</meta>

<summary>Tansillo explains the distinction between the universal human intellect (represented by the moon) and the individual, wandering human mind. He contrasts the inconstancy of sensual love with the stability and unity of intellectual love directed toward a single divine object.</summary>

G. 2.

### VII. CICADA.

But what is the meaning of that image of the sun with one circle inside and another outside, bearing the motto **IT ENCIRCLES** <note>original: "CIRCUIT"</note>?

**TANSILLO.** I am certain I would never have grasped the meaning of this if I had not heard it from the creator of the image himself. Now, you must know that the word "encircles" refers to the motion the sun makes through that circle, which is described as being both inside and outside. This signifies that the motion is simultaneously being made and is already completed <note>Tansillo is describing a state of "eternal present" where there is no distinction between the process and the result</note>. Consequently, the sun is always found at every point of that circle at once. Because if it moves in an instant, it follows that it simultaneously moves and has been moved, and that it exists equally throughout the entire circumference of the circle. Thus, in this sun, motion and rest coincide as one.

**CICADA.** I understood this concept in the dialogues on the **Infinite Universe and Innumerable Worlds** <note>A reference to Bruno's own work, *De l'infinito, universo e mondi* (1584), where he argues for an infinite cosmos</note>, where it is explained how **Divine Wisdom** is "most mobile" (as **Solomon** said) <note>Reference to the Book of Wisdom 7:24: "For wisdom is more mobile than any motion."</note> and yet the same Wisdom is "most stable," as is said and understood by all those who possess true understanding. Now, proceed to make me understand the main point.

**TANSILLO.** It means that his sun <note>Referring to the sun of the lover or the higher intellect</note> is not like this physical sun, which (as is commonly believed) circles the earth with its **diurnal motion** <term>diurnal motion: the apparent daily rotation of the heavens from east to west</term> in twenty-four hours, and with its **planetary motion** <term>planetary motion: the slower annual movement of the sun through the constellations of the zodiac</term> in twelve months—thereby creating the four distinct seasons of the year, according to its position at the four **cardinal points** <note>The solstices and equinoxes</note> of the **Zodiac** <term>Zodiac: the "circle of animals," the belt of constellations through which the sun, moon, and planets appear to move</term>. Rather, this higher sun is of such a nature that, because it is the...

<summary>Tansillo explains a solar emblem where motion and rest are identical, representing the divine intellect. Cicada relates this to Bruno's earlier writings on the infinite universe and the paradoxical nature of God as both perfectly still and perfectly mobile.</summary>
<keywords>solar emblem, divine wisdom, motion and rest, Solomon, infinite universe, diurnal motion, planetary motion, Zodiac, coincidence of opposites</keywords>

-># Fifth Dialogue<-

...eternity itself (and consequently a possession that is entirely whole and complete) simultaneously, simultaneously embraces winter, spring, summer, and autumn; simultaneously, simultaneously day and night: because it is everything for everyone and in all points and places.

**CICADA.** Now apply what you are saying to the figure.

**TANSILLO.** Here, because it is not possible to depict the sun in its entirety at every point of the circle, two circles have been drawn: one that encompasses it to signify that it moves through it; the other which is encompassed by it to show that it is moved through it.

**CICADA.** But this demonstration is not very clear or precise.

**TANSILLO.** It is enough that it is the clearest and most precise that he could make: if you can make a better one, you are given the authority to remove that one and put the other in its place, for this was put there only so that the soul should not be without a body <note>Tansillo uses a common Renaissance metaphor: the "soul" is the meaning of the emblem, while the "body" is the physical drawing itself.</note>.

**CICADA.** What do you say about that **IT ENCIRCLES** <note>original: "CIRCVIT"</note>?

**TANSILLO.** That motto, according to its full meaning, signifies the matter as much as it can be signified; given that it signifies that it turns, and that it is turned: that is, the present and the perfect <note>In the grammatical sense, "perfect" refers to a completed action. Tansillo is describing a divine state where the act of moving and the state of having arrived are the same thing.</note> motion.

**CICADA.** Excellently. And therefore those circles which poorly signify the condition of such motion and rest, we can say are put there to signify circulation alone. And so I remain satisfied with the subject and the form of the **heroic emblem** <term>heroic emblem: known as an "impresa," these were symbolic devices used by Renaissance thinkers to represent a philosophical or moral aim through the combination of a picture and a short motto.</term>. Now, let the rhymes be read.

**TANSILLO.**

# Fifth Dialogue

...flames; or rather: warms, ignites, burns, inflames.

You must further consider that these are not four synonyms, but four distinct terms signifying as many stages of the effects of fire. For fire first warms, second ignites, third burns, and fourth inflames or sets ablaze that which it has warmed, ignited, and burned. And so, in the "furious" <note>The "furious" lover refers to the "heroic enthusiast," a central figure in Bruno’s philosophy who is driven by an intense, intellectual, and spiritual passion for the divine.</note>, these signify desire, attention, devotion, and affection, which he feels do not vary for a single moment.

**CICADA.** Why does he place them under the title of "troubles"?

**TANSILLO.** Because the object, which is the divine light, is in this life more a matter of laborious longing than of quiet enjoyment; for our mind, in relation to that light, is like the eyes of nocturnal birds <note>original: "ucelli notturni." A reference to a classic philosophical metaphor, most famously used by Aristotle, comparing the human intellect's difficulty in grasping the most obvious divine truths to an owl’s eyes being blinded by the sun.</note> before the sun.

**CICADA.** Proceed, for now I can understand everything from what has been said.

### VIII. TANSILLO

On the next crest <note>original: "cimiero." A heraldic device or helmet ornament used here as a visual emblem for meditation.</note> there is painted a full moon with the motto: **SUCH SHE IS TO ME ALWAYS, AND TO THE STAR.** <note>original: "TALIS MIHI SEMPER ET ASTRO."</note>

This means that to the star—that is, the sun—and to him, she is always such as she appears here: full and bright in the entire circumference of her circle. To help you understand this better, I want you to hear what is written on the tablet <note>original: "tauoletta." This refers to the poem accompanying the visual emblem.</note>.

affection toward the human intelligence <note>In Renaissance philosophy, "intelligence" often refers to a spiritual or cosmic mind that illuminates the individual human soul.</note> signified by the moon; because just as the moon is said to be the lowest of all the stars and is nearest to us, so too is the illuminating intelligence of all of us (in this current state) the last in the order of the other intelligences, as noted by Averroes <note>A 12th-century philosopher from Islamic Spain whose commentaries on Aristotle were foundational to Western thought.</note> and other more subtle Peripatetics <note>The Peripatetics were followers of Aristotle’s philosophy; the name refers to their habit of walking while discussing.</note>. To the potential intellect <note>The "potential intellect" is the human capacity for thought before it has actually grasped a concept.</note>, that intelligence sometimes sets, insofar as it is not active at all, and sometimes—as if emerging or rising from the depths of the hidden hemisphere—it shows itself now empty, now full, according to how much light of intelligence it bestows. Sometimes its orb is dark, sometimes white, because at times it shows itself through shadows, similitudes, and vestiges <note>original: "uestigio." A "vestige" is a trace or footprint of the divine found in the physical world.</note>, and at other times more and more openly. Now it declines toward the South <note>original: "Austro."</note>, now it mounts toward the North <note>original: "Borea."</note>; that is, it is sometimes moving further and further away, and sometimes drawing closer and closer. But the intellect in act <note>The "intellect in act" refers to the mind when it is actively engaged in understanding or contemplating truth.</note>, through its continuous struggle (for this is not the natural state of the human condition, where it finds itself so troubled, combated, urged, solicited, distracted, and as if torn apart by the lower powers <note>The "lower powers" are the physical senses and animal instincts that distract the mind from higher contemplation.</note>), always sees its object as steady, fixed, and constant; and always full and in the same splendor of beauty. Thus, it is always taken from him insofar as it is not granted, and always restored to him insofar as it is granted. It always burns as much in his affection <note>In this context, "affection" refers to the will's yearning or love for the divine.</note> as it always shines in his thought; it is always as cruel in withdrawing itself by what is withheld, as it is always as beautiful in communicating itself by what is pre-

CICADA: Now apply your understanding to the motto. TANSILLO: It says, therefore, *TALIS MIHI SEMPER* <note>original: "Such she is to me always."</note>; that is, through my continuous application of intellect, memory, and will <note>These three—intellect, memory, and will—were considered the three primary faculties of the human soul in Renaissance psychology.</note> (because I wish to remember, understand, and desire nothing else), she is always thus to me. And so far as I am able to grasp her, she is entirely present; she is not divided from me by any distraction of thought, nor does she become darker to me through a lack of attention, because there is no thought that might divert me from that light, and there is no natural necessity that might force me to attend to her less. She is *TALIS MIHI SEMPER* <note>Such she is to me always</note> on her own part, because she is invariable in substance, in virtue, in beauty, and in effect toward those things that are constant and invariable toward her. It says next, *UT ASTRO* <note>original: "As to a star."</note>, because with respect to the sun—which is her illuminator—she is always equally luminous, given that she is always equally turned toward it, and the sun always diffuses its rays to her in the same way. Just as, physically, this moon which we see with our eyes—although toward the earth it may now appear dark, now bright, now more or less illuminated and illuminating—it is always equally illuminated by the sun; because it always receives the sun's rays, at least across the back of its entire hemisphere. Just as this earth also is always illuminated in that hemisphere

CICADA: How is this intelligence signified by the moon that shines through the hemisphere? TANSILLO: All intelligences are signified by the moon, insofar as they participate in both **act** and **potency** <note>In Renaissance philosophy, "act" refers to something being fully realized or "doing," while "potency" refers to the potential or capacity to receive a form. Only God was considered "pure act" without any potentiality.</note>, for which I say they have light materially and according to participation, receiving it from another. I say this because they are not lights by themselves and by their own nature, but in regard to the sun, which is the first intelligence, which is pure and absolute light, just as it is also pure and absolute act. CICADA: Therefore, are all things that have a dependency, and which are not the first act and cause, composed as if of light and darkness, of matter and form, of potency and act? TANSILLO: It is so. Furthermore, our soul in its entire substance is signified by the moon, which shines through the hemisphere of the superior powers; hence, it is turned toward the light of the intelligible world <note>The "intelligible world" is the realm of ideas and divine truth that can be understood by the mind, as opposed to the "sensible world" perceived by the eyes.</note>, and it is dark in the inferior powers, where it is occupied with the government of matter.

### IX. CICADA.

And it seems to me that what has just been said has a certain consequence and symbol in the emblem <note>original: *impresa*. An emblem or "impresa" was a popular Renaissance device consisting of a symbolic picture and a motto used to express a personal philosophy or state of mind.</note> that I see on the following shield, where there is a—

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Tansillo and Cicada regarding the soul's constancy. Tansillo compares the soul's focus on the divine to an "antictona" (counter-earth) or a deep-rooted tree. The conversation shifts to a defense of Epicurean philosophy, specifically the idea that true virtue and "heroic love" involve reaching a state where external hardships are no longer felt because the mind is entirely absorbed in a higher pleasure.</meta>

# Fifth Dialogue.

...this counter-earth <note>original: "antictona," a term from Pythagorean astronomy referring to a hypothetical celestial body. Here, Bruno uses it to describe a state of the soul that remains fixed relative to the "sun" of divine intelligence.</note> between our earth and the sun, insofar as it appears varied to our eyes; but insofar as it always receives an equal portion of solar splendor within itself. And for this reason, it remains constant and firm against the North Winds <note>original: "Aquiloni," the Roman personification of the cold, stormy winds from the North.</note> and tempestuous winters, through the firmness it possesses in its star <note>In Bruno’s cosmology, "star" often refers to the celestial sphere or the specific "seat" where the soul is spiritually "planted."</note> in which it is planted by design and intention, just as the aforementioned deep-rooted plant keeps its roots interwoven with the veins of the earth. 

**CICADA:** I value being in tranquility and free from trouble more than being in such a state of strong endurance. 

**TANSILLO:** This is a maxim of the Epicureans <note>Followers of Epicurus. While often stereotyped as seekers of base pleasure, Bruno interprets them here as seeking "ataraxia"—a state of mental calm and freedom from suffering.</note>, which, if well understood, will not be judged as profane as the ignorant believe it to be; seeing as it does not deny that what I have said is a virtue, nor does it prejudice the perfection of constancy; but rather it adds to that perfection which the common people understand. This is because he [Epicurus] does not consider true and complete virtue of fortitude and constancy to be that which feels and endures hardships, but rather that which bears them without feeling them. He does not consider a divine and heroic love to be complete if it feels the spur, the bit, or remorse, or pain for any other love; but rather that which has effectively lost all sense of other affections. Thus, it has reached such a pleasure that no displeasure is powerful enough to distract it or make it stumble <note>original: "cespitare," to trip or falter.</note> for a moment. And this is to touch the highest bliss <note>original: "somma beatitudine."</note> in this state: to have pleasure <note>original: "voluptá," used here in the philosophical sense of *voluptas*, the refined pleasure of a mind at peace.</note> and have no sense of pain. 

**CICADA:** Common opinion does not believe this sense...

<meta>catchword: of Epi-</meta>

<summary>Tansillo explains that the heroic lover's constancy is like a tree rooted in a star, eventually reaching an Epicurean state of "ataraxia" where divine pleasure makes them indifferent to physical or emotional pain. He defends Epicurean thought as a higher form of virtue rather than a profane pursuit of pleasure.</summary>

<keywords>Epicureanism, constancy, heroic love, ataraxia, virtue, counter-earth, Giordano Bruno, Tansillo, Cicada, divine pleasure</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue from the previous page, where Tansillo was defending Epicurean philosophy against the common misconception that it is merely hedonistic. He transitions here into a specific example of Epicurus’s own death to illustrate "heroic" constancy.</meta>

# Fifth Dialogue.

...of Epicurus. **TAN.** Because they do not read his books, nor the works of those who report his teachings without envy; they are the opposite of those who study the course of his life and the manner of his death. At that moment, with these words, he dictated the beginning of his will: "Being in the final and likewise happiest day of our life, we have ordained this with a quiet, healthy, and tranquil mind; for although the greatest pain of the stone <note>original: "dolor de pietra." Epicurus died of kidney stones or a similar blockage; here, his ability to find happiness despite physical agony is used as an example of philosophical "constancy."</note> tormented us on one side, all that torment was absorbed by the pleasure of our discoveries <note>original: "inuentioni," referring to his philosophical findings and rational contemplations.</note> and the consideration of our final purpose." And it is obvious that he did not place happiness in eating, drinking, resting, and procreating any more than he placed pain there; rather, he placed it in not feeling hunger, nor thirst, nor fatigue, nor lust. From this, consider what we believe to be the perfection of constancy: it is not merely that the tree does not shatter, break, or bend, but that it does not even move. In the likeness of such a tree, the person of constancy keeps their spirit, sense, and intellect fixed where they have no feeling of tempestuous assaults.

**CIC.** Do you wish, then, that the endurance of torments should be a desirable thing because it is the act of a strong man?

**TAN.** What you call "enduring" is only a part of constancy and is not the entire virtue; what I am speaking of is "enduring strongly," or what Epicurus called "not feeling." This deprivation of feeling <note>The state of *apatheia* or *ataraxia*, where the mind is so focused on higher truths that physical suffering is no longer registered by the consciousness.</note> is caused by the soul being entirely absorbed in the pursuit of virtue, the true good, and happiness. Just as Regulus <note>Marcus Atilius Regulus, a Roman general and model of civic virtue who, according to legend, returned to Carthage to face certain torture and death rather than break his word or see Rome suffer. He is used here as a secular martyr for virtue.</note>...

<meta>catchword: non</meta>

<summary>Tansillo defends Epicurus by citing his final moments, arguing that true constancy is a state of being so absorbed in spiritual or intellectual truth that physical pain is no longer felt.</summary>
<keywords>Epicurus, constancy, virtue, pleasure and pain, kidney stone, Marcus Atilius Regulus, stoicism, ataraxia</keywords>

<image-desc>A rectangular woodcut headpiece consisting of a symmetrical arrangement of scrolling floral and leaf patterns.</image-desc>

Here are shown the pains and discomforts that are found in love, especially in vulgar love <note>original: "amor volgare." In this context, it refers to common or earthly lust as opposed to the "heroic" or intellectual love discussed elsewhere in the manuscript.</note>, which is nothing other than the workshop of Vulcan—that blacksmith who forges the thunderbolts of Jove <note>The Roman king of the gods, also known as Jupiter.</note> that torment delinquent souls. This is because disordered love carries the principle of its own punishment within itself; for God is near, is with us, and is inside of us. There is found within us a certain sacred mind and intelligence served by its own affection, which possesses its own avenger. This avenger, through the remorse of a certain synderesis <term>synderesis: a term in scholastic theology referring to the innate "spark" of conscience that remains even in a fallen soul</term> at the very least—acting like a rigid hammer—lashes the transgressing spirit. 

That mind observes our actions and affections, and according to how it is treated by us, it ensures that we are treated by it. In all lovers, I say, this blacksmith Vulcan is present: just as there is no man who does not have God within him, there is no lover who does not have this god. God is most certainly in everyone, but which god resides in each person is not so easily known. And yet, if it can be examined and distinguished, I believe nothing else can clarify it but love—acting as the one who pulls the oars, swells the sail, and directs this composite being <note>original: "questo composto." A philosophical term for the human entity composed of both body and soul.</note>, leading to either a good or a bad disposition.

<meta>This page continues the "Fifth Dialogue" started on the previous page. # Fifth Dialogue.

I speak of being well or poorly disposed regarding that which is put into execution through moral actions and contemplation; for as for the rest, all lovers commonly feel some discomfort. This is because, just as in all composite things <note>original: "cose miste." In Renaissance philosophy, this refers to the physical world where elements are mixed, meaning nothing is ever found in its pure, perfect state.</note>, there is no good conceived or felt to which evil is not joined or opposed, just as there is no truth to which the false is not attached and joined. Thus, there is no love without fear, zeal, jealousy, resentment, and other passions that proceed from the contrary that perturbs us, even as the other contrary satisfies us. 

In this way, when the soul begins to think of recovering its natural beauty, it strives to purge, heal, and reform itself. And so it employs fire, because being like gold mixed with earth and formless, it seeks to free itself from impurity with a certain rigor. This takes effect when the intellect—the true blacksmith of Jove <note>Jupiter, the king of the gods.</note>—lays its hands upon it, exercising the acts of the intellectual powers. 

**CICADA:** To this, it seems to me, refers that which is found in Plato’s *Symposium* <note>original: "Conuito." This refers to Plato's famous dialogue on the nature of love.</note> where he says that Love has inherited from his mother Penia <note>The personification of Poverty. In the myth, Love is the child of Poverty (Penia) and Resource (Poros), explaining why love is always in need but always searching.</note> the state of being parched, thin, pale, barefoot, humble, without a bed, and without a roof. Through these circumstances is signified the torment of the soul troubled by contrary affections. 

**TANSILLO:** So it is, because the spirit affected by such a frenzy <term>frenzy: or *furore*, referring to "heroic frenzy," a state of divine inspiration where the soul is driven beyond ordinary reason toward the divine.</term> is distracted by profound thoughts, hammered by urgent cares, heated by fervent desires, and fanned by frequent occasions. In conclusion, and to the point: whatever kind of love it may be, it is always so labored and tormented that it cannot fail to be the raw material in the forges of Vulcan <note>The Roman god of fire and blacksmithing; the husband of Venus.</note>. This is because the soul—being a divine thing and by nature not a servant but the mistress of bodily matter—becomes troubled even in that which voluntarily serves the body, where it finds nothing that satisfies it. And however much it remains fixed upon the beloved thing, it always happens that it is equally agitated, tossing amidst the gusts of hopes, fears, doubts, zeal, consciences, remorses, obstinacies, repentances, and other tormentors <note>original: "manigoldi," literally executioners or henchmen.</note> who are the bellows, the coals, the anvils, the hammers, the tongs, and other instruments found in the workshop of this sordid and filthy husband of Venus.

# Fifth Dialogue.

-> But this one possesses all that is pleasing <-
-> Of beauty, intelligence, and majesty. <-

---

<image-desc>An ornamental woodcut header featuring symmetrical scrolling foliate and floral motifs.</image-desc>

Here, he makes a comparison between his object <note>The "object" refers to the lady or the divine truth the enthusiast pursues.</note>, which contains all the circumstances, conditions, and types of beauty within a single subject, and others who display no more than one quality each; and even then, these qualities are scattered across different individuals. This is like what happened with the single category of physical beauty, where all the ideal conditions could not be found by Apelles <note>The text attributes this to Apelles, though the famous anecdote of selecting the best features from various models to create a perfect whole is often associated with the painter Zeuxis.</note> in a single person, but rather across several maidens.

Now, in this case, where there are three kinds of beauty—even though it happens that all three are found in each of the three goddesses (for Venus does not lack wisdom and majesty; in Juno there is no lack of loveliness and wisdom; and in Pallas, majesty is noted along with loveliness)—it nevertheless happens that one quality surpasses the others. Wherefore that specific quality comes to be esteemed as a property <term>property: a characteristic that belongs essentially and uniquely to a subject</term>, and the others as common accidents <term>accidents: in philosophy, qualities that can change or be shared without altering the core essence of the subject</term>. This is because, among these three gifts, one predominates in each goddess, serving to define her and entitle her as sovereign over the others.

And the cause of such a difference is that they possess these qualities not by essence <term>essence: the fundamental nature of a thing</term> and primarily, but by participation <term>participation: the philosophical concept that things in the world are not perfection itself, but merely "participate" in or reflect divine ideals</term> and derivatively. Just as in all dependent things, perfections exist according to degrees of greater and lesser, more and less.

<summary>The text concludes a poem comparing the beloved to Venus, Juno, and Pallas, then explains through Neoplatonic philosophy how the beloved uniquely unites the three divine qualities of beauty, majesty, and wisdom.</summary>
<keywords>Judgment of Paris, Venus, Juno, Pallas Minerva, Apelles, essence vs. participation, Neoplatonism, beauty, wisdom, majesty</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the "Fifth Dialogue," specifically the philosophical commentary on the nature of the divine object of the enthusiast's love. # Fifth Dialogue.

But in the simplicity <term>simplicity: the theological concept that God is not composed of different parts or qualities, but is a single, unified essence</term> of the divine essence, everything is present in its totality, and not according to measure; and therefore, there is no more wisdom than there is beauty and majesty, nor is there more goodness than strength. Instead, all attributes are not only equal, but also identical and one and the same thing. 

Just as in a sphere all dimensions are not only equal (the length being as much as the depth and width), but also identical; considering that what you call "deep" in a sphere, you can also call "long" and "wide." So it is with the height of divine wisdom, which is the same as the depth of power and the breadth of goodness. All these perfections are equal because they are infinite. This is because each is necessarily according to the greatness of the others; whereas in finite things, it happens that one may be wiser than they are beautiful and good, or better and more beautiful than they are wise, or wiser and better than they are powerful, and more powerful than they are good and wise. 

But where there is infinite wisdom, there can be nothing but infinite power; for otherwise one could not know infinitely. Where there is infinite goodness, infinite wisdom is required; for otherwise one would not know how to be infinitely good. Where there is infinite power, there must be infinite goodness and wisdom, so that so much good may be known and that the power to do good is understood. Now, therefore, see how the object of this enthusiast <note>Italian: *furioso*. In this context, it refers to a "heroic enthusiast"—one who is driven by a divine frenzy or intellectual passion for truth.</note>, almost intoxicated by...

<summary>This page explains the concept of divine simplicity, arguing that in the infinite divine essence, attributes like wisdom, power, and goodness are not distinct or unequal but are one and the same, much like the dimensions of a sphere.</summary>
<keywords>divine simplicity, attributes of God, infinite, wisdom, power, goodness, enthusiast, Neoplatonism</keywords>

drink of the gods, will be incomparably higher than those others that differ from it. I mean to say that the intelligible species <term>intelligible species: the mental representation or "form" through which the mind understands a thing</term> of the divine essence contains the perfection of all other species in the highest degree. This is so that, depending on the degree to which one can participate in that form, one will be able to understand all, do all, and be so enamored of this one beauty as to hold all others in contempt and weariness. Therefore, the spherical apple <note>A reference to the Golden Apple of Discord from the Judgment of Paris. In this philosophical context, the "apple" represents the prize of ultimate beauty/truth, which should be awarded to the unified Divine Essence rather than a single goddess.</note> must be consecrated to that one, as it is all in all. It should not be given to fair Venus, who is surpassed by Minerva in wisdom and by Juno in majesty. **CIC.** Certainly, just as there are degrees of natures and essences, so proportionally are there degrees of the intelligible species, and the magnificences of the amorous affects and furies <term>furies: "furori," the inspired states of spiritual or intellectual ecstasy that drive the soul toward the divine</term>.

# Dialogo Quinto.

### H.4.

...capable of the divine beauty and goodness of that infinite splendor; which so influence the intellectual and rational desire that they cause it to aspire infinitely, according to the way in which that excellent light perceives the infinitely great, beautiful, and good. Because so long as love is finite, satisfied, and fixed at a certain measure, it will not be concerning the species of divine beauty but some other formed thing; but while it goes ever further and further in aspiration, it can be said to be engaged with the infinite. 

**CICADA.** How fittingly is "aspiring" signified by "breathing" <note>original: "spirare." Bruno plays on the etymological connection between *aspirare* (to long for/aspire) and *spirare* (to breathe or for a wind to blow).</note>? What symbol do the winds have in common with desire? 

**TANSILLO.** Whoever among us aspires in this state, that person sighs, and that same person breathes. And therefore, the vehemence of aspiring is noted by that Hieroglyph of strong breathing. 

**CICADA.** But there is a difference between sighing and breathing. 

**TANSILLO.** However, the one is not signified by the other as the selfsame thing for the selfsame thing, but as like for like. 

**CICADA.** Continue, then, with your point. 

**TANSILLO.** The infinite aspiration, therefore—shown through sighs and signified by the winds—is under the governance not of Aeolus in the Aeolian Islands <note>In mythology, Aeolus kept the winds imprisoned in caves on these islands.</note>, but of those two aforementioned lights <note>A poetic reference to the eyes of the beloved, representing divine intelligence or beauty.</note>. These lights kill the frenzied lover <term>furioso</term> <note>In Bruno’s philosophy, the "furioso" is the "heroic enthusiast" who is driven by a divine madness or "frenzy" to seek union with the infinite.</note> not only innocently but most benignly, making him die through studious affection in regard to all other things; for those eyes, which make him tempestuous when they are closed and hidden, will make him tranquil when open. <meta>This page continues the "Fifth Dialogue" from the previous page, where Tansillo and Cicada are discussing the nature of divine love and infinite desire. The previous page concluded with the metaphor of the winds and breathing as symbols for the soul's aspiration toward the divine. This section explores the metaphysical relationship between the finite human intellect and its infinite object.</meta>

# Dialogo Quinto.

...which, like a cloudy veil, shadows the eyes of the human mind within this body: it happens that the soul, through such effort, becomes rather troubled and tormented; whereas, if that veil were torn away and cast aside, the soul would find such high repose as would suffice to satisfy the condition of its nature. 

**CICADA.** How can our finite intellect pursue an infinite object? 

**TANSILLO.** Through the infinite power that it possesses. 

**CICADA.** This power is in vain if it is never realized in actual effect. 

**TANSILLO.** It would be in vain if it were directed toward a finite act, where infinite power would be privative <note>meaning it would be "empty" or "lacking" because a finite result cannot contain an infinite potential</note>; but it is not so concerning the infinite act, where infinite power is a positive perfection. 

**CICADA.** If the human intellect is, by its nature, a finite act, how and why does it possess infinite power? 

**TANSILLO.** Because it is eternal; and so that it may always find delight, and its happiness may have neither end nor measure; and because, just as it is finite in itself, so it may be infinite in its object. 

**CICADA.** What is the difference between the infinity of the object and the infinity of the power? 

**TANSILLO.** The latter is finitely infinite, while the former is infinitely infinite. <note>Bruno suggests that the human mind is "finitely infinite" because it is a finite container with an infinite capacity to receive, whereas God/The Universe is "infinitely infinite" because it is infinite in both essence and capacity.</note> But let us return to our subject. The motto there says, **"The new Aeolian offspring"** <note>original: "NOVÆ partæ Æoliæ." A reference to the winds of Aeolus being reborn as the lover's sighs.</note>, because it seems one can believe that all the winds (which are in the cavernous abysses of Aeolus <note>In Greek mythology, Aeolus was the keeper of the winds, which he held trapped in a deep cave.</note>) have been converted into sighs, if we wish to count those which proceed from the affection that aspires without end to the supreme good and infinite beauty.

# Dialogo Quinto.

### XIII. CICADA.

Let us now see the meaning of that burning torch, around which is written **FOR LIFE; NOT FOR AN HOUR** <note>original: "AD VITAM; NON AD HORAM"</note>. **TANSILLO.** It signifies perseverance in such love, and the burning desire for the true good, in which the enthusiast <note>The 'furioso' or heroic enthusiast refers to a person possessed by a "divine frenzy," a Platonic concept where the soul is driven by an intense, intellectual passion to reach the divine truth.</note> burns while in this temporal state. This is what I believe the following table shows.

The peasant departs from his dwelling,
When day clears the breast of the East,
And when the sun strikes most closely,
Tired and parched by heat, he seeks the shade;
He labors then, and toils until
Black darkness shrouds the hemisphere;
Then he finds rest. But I face constant blows
Morning, midday, evening, and night.
->These fiery rays<-
Which emerge from those two arches of my sun <note>A poetic reference to the eyes of the beloved, or in this philosophical context, the dual light of divine intelligence and beauty.</note>,
Of my soul (as my destiny wills)
From the horizon never depart;
->Burning at all hours<-
From its meridian my afflicted heart.

<meta>This page continues the "Fifth Dialogue." # Dialogo Quinto.

**CICADA.** This explanation more truly and properly unfolds the meaning of the image.

**TANSILLO.** I do not need to struggle to make you see these properties, for seeing them requires nothing more than a more attentive consideration. The rays of the sun are the reasons <note>Here, "reasons" refers to the "rationes" or the logical and spiritual modes through which the divine is understood.</note> by which the divine beauty and goodness manifest themselves to us. And they are fiery, because they cannot be grasped by the intellect without subsequently warming the affection <note>In Renaissance philosophy, "intelletto" (intellect) is the power of knowing, while "affetto" (affection or will) is the power of loving or desiring. They are always linked.</note>. The two arches of the sun are the two types of revelation that the scholastic theologians call morning and evening <note>original: "matutina et uespertina." These terms, popularized by St. Thomas Aquinas, refer to "morning knowledge" (knowing things through God's essence) and "evening knowledge" (knowing things through their own created nature).</note>; whereby the intelligence that illuminates us, acting as a mediating air, brings us that "species" <note>The term "species" refers to the mental image or form of a thing that enters the mind.</note> either in its power—admiring it in itself—or in its efficacy—contemplating it in its effects. 

The "horizon of the soul" in this context is the realm of the higher powers, where the vigorous impulse of the affection assists the strong apprehension of the intellect. This is signified by the heart, which, burning at all hours, is afflicted; for all the fruits of love that we can gather in this state <note>Meaning our current earthly, mortal life.</note> are not so sweet that they are not also joined to a certain affliction—at least that which proceeds from the awareness of not having full fruition <note>The state of complete possession or perfect enjoyment of the beloved object.</note>. This occurs especially with the fruits of natural love, the condition of which I could not better express than did the Epicurean poet <note>A reference to Lucretius, the Roman poet and author of *De Rerum Natura* (On the Nature of Things).</note>.

<meta>catchword: From hu- [original: Ex ho-]</meta>

He draws on Scholastic theology and identifies the soul's "horizon" as the meeting point between thought and desire.</summary>

# Fifth Dialogue.

> Truly, from the human face and beautiful complexion,
> Nothing is given to the body to be enjoyed except thin semblances,
> Which a wretched hope often catches like the wind.
> As when a thirsty man seeks to drink in dreams, and no water
> Is given that can quench the fire in his limbs;
> But he chases phantoms of liquid, and labors in vain;
> And he thirsts while drinking in the middle of a rushing river.
> So in love, Venus mocks the lover with phantoms,
> Nor can they satisfy themselves by gazing at bodies face to face,
> Nor can they scrape anything away from the tender limbs
> With their hands, wandering uncertainly over the whole body.
> Finally, when with limbs joined they enjoy the flower
> Of youth; while now the body presages joys,
> And Venus is about to sow the womanly fields:
> They greedily press body to body, and join the saliva
> Of the mouth; and they breathe into each other, pressing lips with teeth,
> In vain, because they can scrape nothing from there,
> Nor penetrate, and pass into the body with the whole body.
> <note>original: "Ex hominis veró facie..." This is a passage from Lucretius’s *De rerum natura*, Book IV. Bruno uses these famous Latin verses on the futility of physical lust to illustrate the even greater frustration of the soul seeking the infinite.</note>
> ->with the whole body.<-

In the same way, one judges regarding the kind of taste that we can have here for divine things; while we force ourselves to penetrate and unite with them, we find we have more affliction in the desire than pleasure in the concept <note>The "concept" here refers to the mental grasp or intellectual understanding of the divine.</note>. And for this reason, that wise Hebrew <note>A reference to King Solomon; specifically Ecclesiastes 1:18: "For in much wisdom is much grief: and he that increaseth knowledge increaseth sorrow."</note> may have said that he who increases knowledge increases sorrow, because from a greater apprehension <term>Apprehension: in this philosophical context, it refers to the act of the mind grasping or becoming aware of an object.</term> arises a greater and higher desire, and from this follows greater resentment and pain for the deprivation of the thing desired. Wherefore the Epicurean <note>referring again to the poet Lucretius, an adherent of Epicurean philosophy.</note> who follows the most tranquil life, spoke thus regarding vulgar love <note>original: "amor volgare." This refers to earthly, physical, or carnal love, which Bruno contrasts with the "heroic" love for the divine.</note>.

<meta>catchword: Sed (But)</meta>

<summary>Tansillo quotes Lucretius to illustrate how physical union is a futile attempt to merge two bodies, then applies this logic to the soul's pursuit of God. He concludes that increasing one's knowledge of the divine only increases the pain of being separated from it.</summary>

# Fifth Dialogue.

> But it is fitting to flee those phantoms, and to brush away 
> The fodder of love from oneself, and turn the mind elsewhere, 
> And not keep this care and certain pain for oneself; 
> For the ulcer gains strength and grows old by being fed, 
> And from day to day the madness spreads, and the misery grows heavy. 
> Nor does he lack the fruit of Venus who avoids love, 
> But rather he takes the benefits which are without penalty.
> <note>original: "Sed fugitare decet..." This continues the passage from Lucretius’s *De rerum natura* (Book IV) started on the previous page. Lucretius argues that to find peace, one must avoid the "simulacra" (phantoms or images) of the beloved that fuel the fire of passion.</note>

**CIC.** What does he mean by the **MERIDIAN** of the heart? **TAN.** The highest or most eminent part or region of the will; where it is most brightly, strongly, effectively, and rightly heated. He means that such an affection is not like it is at the beginning, when it is just moving, nor like it is at the end, when it is at rest; but like it is at the middle, where it burns with fervor. <note>In Renaissance thought, the "Meridian" represents the sun at its highest point (noon), symbolizing the peak of intensity or the most direct "heat" of the soul's desire for the divine.</note>

### XIIII, CICADA.

But what signifies that fiery arrow which has flames in place of an iron tip, around which a cord is wound, and carries the motto: **LOVE PRESSES LIKE AN INSTANT** <note>original: "AMOR INSTAT VT INSTANS." This is a sophisticated pun. In Latin, *instans* can mean "the present moment" (the instant) or "insistent/pressing." The lover’s pain is both eternal and focused on an ever-present, pressing intensity.</note>? Tell me, what do you understand by it? **TAN.** It seems to me he wants to say that love never leaves him, and that it afflicts him eternally in the same way. **CIC.** I see the cord, the arrow, and the fire clearly enough; I understand what is written, **LOVE PRESSES**; but what follows I cannot grasp: that is, that love, as an "instant" or as something "insistent," insists. This seems to have the same poverty of meaning as if someone were to say: "This man has feigned this enterprise as feigned,"

<meta>This page continues the "Fifth Dialogue" of Giordano Bruno's *The Heroic Frenzies*. the door as the door, I understand it as I understand it, its value as it is valued, I esteem it as one who esteems it. **TAN.** He determines and condemns more easily, who considers less. 
That word **INSTANT** <note>original: "instans"</note> does not function as an adjective derived from the verb **to press** <note>original: "instare," meaning to urge, persist, or stand over</note>, but is a substantive noun taken to mean the "instant" of time.

**CICA.** Now what does it mean that love presses like the instant? 

**TAD.** <note>The text uses "TAD." here, likely a variant or error for Tansillo.</note> What does Aristotle mean in his book on Time, when he says that eternity is an instant, and that in all of time there is but one instant? 

**CI.** How can this be, if there is no amount of time so minimal that it does not contain more instants? Does he perhaps mean that the Deluge, the Trojan War, and we who exist now are all in one single instant? I would like to know how this instant is divided into so many centuries and years? And if, by that same proportion, we cannot say that a line is but a point? 

**TAN.** Just as time is one, but exists in diverse temporal subjects, so the instant is one in all the diverse parts of time. As I am the same person who I was, who I am, and who I will be; I am the same person here at home, in the temple, in the field, and everywhere I am. 

**CIC.** Why do you claim that the instant is the whole of time? 

**TAN.** Because if the instant did not exist, time would not be made; therefore, time in its essence and substance is nothing other than the instant. And this should suffice if you understand it (as I have no desire to play the pedant over the fourth book of the *Physics* <note>Bruno refers to Aristotle’s *Physics*, Book IV, where the philosopher discusses the nature of time and the "now" (the instant) as the link between the past and the future.</note>), from which you may comprehend what he wishes to

<meta>This page continues the "Fifth Dialogue" of Giordano Bruno's *The Heroic Frenzies*. The previous page concluded with Tansillo explaining that time is essentially composed of "the instant," and he was about to clarify why this means love is ever-present rather than fleeting.</meta>

# Dialogue Five.

...it would seem that love assists him no less than all of time: because this word, **INSTANT** <note>original: "I N S T A N S"</note>, does not mean a point of time at all. **CIC.** This meaning must be specified in some manner, if we do not wish the motto to be flawed by ambiguity <note>original: "equiuocatione"</note>; for otherwise, we might freely understand it to mean that his love lasts but an instant—that is, an **atom of time** <term>atomo di tempo: in Renaissance physics, the smallest indivisible unit of duration</term> and a mere nothing—or that he means it is (as you interpret) forever. **TAN.** Certainly, if these two contrary senses were implied, the motto would be a joke. But it is not so, if you consider it well; since it is impossible for love to "press" or "persist" <note>original: "inste ó insista," playing on the Latin roots of *instans*</note> in an instant that is a mere atom or point. One must necessarily understand "instant" in another sense. And to leave the classroom behind <note>original: "uscir di schuola," a suggestion to move from academic hair-splitting to poetic truth</note>, let us read the stanza.

One time scatters, and one time gathers;
One builds, one destroys; one weeps, one laughs;
One time has sad, one time has happy desires;
One labors, one rests; one stands still, one flees;
One time offers, one time takes back;
One moves, one stops; one brings to life, one kills:
In all the years, months, days, and hours
Love waits for me, strikes me, inflames me, and binds me.

->Continuously it disperses me,<-
Always it consumes me, and keeps me in tears,
And my sad languishing is always just as great,
In every time it troubles and elevates me,

**CIC.** I have understood the meaning quite well and I confess that everything accords very well. However, it seems to me time to proceed to the next one.

### XV. TANSILLO.

Here you see a serpent languishing in the snow where a laborer <note>original: "zappatore," a digger or peasant</note> had thrown it; and a naked boy burning in the midst of a fire, with certain other small details and circumstances, with the motto that says, **THE SAME, IN THE SAME WAY, NOT THE SAME** <note>original: "IDEM, ITIDEM, NON IDEM"</note>. This seems to me more like an enigma than anything else, therefore I do not trust myself to explain it fully: yet I would believe it is meant to signify the same troublesome fate, which in the same way torments both—that is, most intensely, without mercy, unto death—with different instruments or contrary principles, showing itself to be both cold and hot. But this seems to me to require a longer and more distinct consideration. **CIC.** Another time. Read the poem.

# Dialogue Five.

I believe you would have a powerful plea,
To make her feel some mercy for your torment.
Within the eternal fire
I writhe, I waste away, I burn, I burst into flame;
Yet in the ice of my goddess, seeking my escape,
Neither love for me, nor pity finds a place;
Alas, for she does not feel
How great is the rigor of my burning flame.

Serpent, you seek to flee, but you are powerless;
Retreat to your hole—it has dissolved;
You summon your own strength—it is extinguished;
You wait for the sun—thick mist hides it;
You beg the peasant <note>original: "uillan," referring to the laborer mentioned on the previous page</note> for mercy—he hates your fangs;
You call on Fortune—the fool does not hear you.
Flight, shelter, strength, star, man, or fate
Cannot offer you escape from death.
You freeze <note>original: "addensi," literally to become dense or thicken, as water does when turning to ice</note>, I melt;
I look at your cold rigor, you at my burning heat;
You long for this ill, I for that desire;
Neither can I free you, nor you free me from this snare.
Now that we are sufficiently enlightened
Regarding this cruel fate, let us abandon all hope. <note>A likely allusion to the famous inscription on the gates of Hell in Dante's *Inferno*: "Lasciate ogne speranza, voi ch'intrate."</note>

# Dialogo Primo,
I.ii.

...turned in the middle, and the third of a dog looking forward; to signify that past things afflict with thought, but not as much as present things which in effect torment us; but always for the future we promise ourselves better. Therefore, there is the wolf that howls, here the lion that roars, and next the dog that fawns <note>Original: "applaude," here meaning a dog showing joy or wagging its tail to please its master.</note>.

**CES.** What is contained in that motto written above?
**MA.** You see that above the wolf is **ALREADY** <note>original: "IAM," signifying the past</note>, above the lion **NOW** <note>original: "MODO," signifying the present</note>, and above the dog **HEREAFTER** <note>original: "PRAETEREA," signifying the future</note>, which are words that signify the three parts of time.
**CES.** Now read what is on the tablet.
**MAR.** I shall do so.

# Dialogo Primo.

**CESARE.** This is exactly the head of a **frenzied lover** <note>Original: "furioso amante." In Giordano Bruno’s philosophy, the "heroic frenzy" is a state of divine madness where the lover seeks the infinite.</note>; although it might apply to almost all mortals in whatever way and manner they are afflicted. For we neither should nor can say that this description applies to all states in general, but to those who were and are troubled. Considering that for one who has sought a kingdom and now possesses it, the fear of losing it is fitting; for one who has labored to acquire the fruits of love—such as the particular favor of the beloved—the sting of jealousy and suspicion is fitting.

And as for the states of the world, when we find ourselves in darkness and evil, we can surely prophesy light and prosperity; when we are in happiness and discipline <note>In this context, "discipline" refers to a state of social order, learning, and organized religious practice.</note>, without a doubt we can expect the arrival of ignorance and hardships. This happened to **Hermes Trismegistus** <note>original: "Mercurio Trimigisto," the legendary Hellenistic figure associated with the Hermetic writings which Bruno highly valued.</note> who, seeing **Egypt** in such splendor of sciences and divinations—through which he considered men to be companions of demons and gods, and consequently most religious—made that prophetic lament to **Asclepius** <note>referring to a famous passage in the "Asclepius," part of the Hermetic corpus, foretelling the decline of Egyptian religion and the arrival of a "dark" age.</note>. He said that there must follow the darkness of new religions and cults, and that of present things nothing would remain but fables and matter for condemnation.

Similarly, the **Hebrews**, when they were slaves in Egypt and exiled in the deserts, were comforted by their prophets with the hope of liberty and the gaining of a homeland. When they were in a state of dominion and tranquility, they were threatened with...

<summary>Cesare explains that the "frenzied lover" represents the human condition of constant change, where prosperity breeds fear and suffering breeds hope. He uses the "Lament of Hermes" and the history of the Hebrews to illustrate this law of historical cycles (vicissitude).</summary>
<keywords>Hermes Trismegistus, Asclepius, Egypt, Hebrews, vicissitude, historical cycles, frenzied lover, prophecy</keywords>

# First Dialogue.

...dispersion and captivity. Today, there is no evil or disgrace to which they <note>Referring to the Jewish people, contrasted with the Egyptians mentioned previously.</note> are not subject; yet there is no good or honor that they do not promise themselves. Similarly, it happens to all other generations and states: which, if they endure and are not completely annihilated, must, by the force of the **vicissitude** <term>vicissitude: a central concept in Bruno's philosophy referring to the constant, cyclical change of all things in the universe.</term> of things, move from evil to good, from good to evil, from lowliness to height, from height to lowliness, from darkness to splendor, and from splendor to darkness. For this is what the natural order entails. Beyond this order, if another be found that spoils or corrects it, I believe it and have no reason to dispute it, because I do not reason with any spirit other than a natural one.

**MARICONDO.** We know that you are acting not as a theologian, but as a philosopher, and that you are treating philosophy, not theology.

**CESARE.** So it is. But let us see what follows.

## II. CESARE.

# First Dialogue

If, adorned in various tablets, I come
Offered by my vows in the temple of Fame?
Because another heroic undertaking calls me back,
Who will ever think it less fitting
That she should hold me back in her captive cult—
She whom heaven so honors and loves?
Leave me, leave other desires,
Troublesome thoughts, give me peace,
Why do you want me to withdraw
From the sight of the sun <note>In Bruno’s symbolism, the "sun" represents the source of divine truth and intellectual light.</note> that so pleases me?
Say of me, merciful ones: "Why do you gaze at
That which, by gazing, so undoes you?"
Why for that torch
Are you so longing? Because it makes me more content
Than any other pleasure, this torment.

---

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut divider featuring symmetrical scrolling floral and vine motifs with a central stylized face or mask.</image-desc>

**MARICONDO.** Regarding this, I was saying to you that although one remains fixed upon a physical beauty and external devotion, he can honorably and worthily occupy himself; provided that from material beauty—which is a ray and splendor of the form <note>The "form" refers to the spiritual or essential blueprint of a thing, as opposed to its physical matter.</note>, and a spiritual act of which it is a vestige <term>vestige: a footprint or trace of the divine left in the physical world.</term> and shadow—he comes to rise to the contemplation and worship of divine beauty, light, and majesty. In this way, through these visible things, he comes to enlarge his heart toward those which are so much more excellent in themselves, and pleasing to the purified soul <term>purified soul: an "animo ripurgato" is a soul cleansed of material passions through philosophical contemplation.</term>, as they are more removed from matter and sense. "Alas!" (he will say), "if a shadow-like, dark, and fleeting..."

<meta>catchword: fleeting,</meta>

Maricondo then explains the Neoplatonic idea that physical beauty is a mere shadow of divine form, serving as a stepping stone for the soul to reach higher spiritual truths.</summary>

--- Page 173 ---
<meta>This page continues the "First Dialogue" of Giordano Bruno’s "The Heroic Frenzies." The previous page ended with Maricondo describing how physical beauty serves as a "vestige" (a footprint or trace) of divine beauty, which pleases the "purified soul." This page begins mid-sentence, completing the thought that the soul is drawn upward through the contemplation of physical beauty toward its divine source.</meta>

...appearing, painted upon the surface of physical matter <term>physical matter: the tangible, bodily substance of the world.</term>, so pleases me and so moves my affection <term>affection: the inner movement of the soul or emotions toward an object.</term>, impressing upon my spirit I know not what reverence of majesty; it captivates me, and so sweetly binds and attracts me, that I find nothing presented to me by the senses that satisfies me so much. What then will the effect be of that which is substantially, originally, and primitively <note>In this context, "primitively" refers to the "first" or most fundamental version of beauty, rather than something crude.</note> beautiful? What will it do to my soul, to the divine intellect, to the rule of nature?

It is fitting, therefore, that the contemplation of this vestige <term>vestige: a trace or "footprint" of a higher reality left in the physical world.</term> of light should lead me—through the purification of my mind—to the imitation, conformity, and participation of that more worthy and high beauty, into which I may transform myself and to which I may be united. For I am certain that Nature, which has placed this beauty before my eyes and has endowed me with an internal sense <note>The "internal sense" refers to the faculty of the mind that processes sensory data into higher intellectual or spiritual understanding.</note> through which I can infer a deeper and incomparably greater beauty, wills that I be promoted from here below to the height and eminence of more excellent forms.

Nor do I believe that my true deity <note>original: "nume." A poetic term for a god or divine power.</note>, just as he shows himself to me in vestige and image, would deign to be indignant that I come to honor him and sacrifice to him in image and vestige—provided that my heart and affection are always ordered to look higher. For who could possibly honor him in his own essence and proper substance, if he cannot comprehend him in that manner?

**CESARE.** You demonstrate very well how, to men of heroic spirit...

<summary>Maricondo explains that the beauty found in physical matter is a "vestige" or divine footprint intended to lead the soul toward the contemplation of original, divine beauty. He argues that honoring the divine through these physical images is appropriate, as humans cannot yet comprehend the divine essence directly.</summary>
<keywords>divine beauty, physical matter, vestige, purification of the soul, heroic spirit, Neoplatonism, internal sense, contemplation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the First Dialogue of Giordano Bruno’s "The Heroic Frenzies." ### First Dialogue

[They] are converted into good, and they make captivity serve as the fruit of greater liberty; and <margin>*</margin>having been once defeated, they turn it into an opportunity for an even greater victory. You know well that the love of physical beauty, for those who are well-disposed, not only brings no delay to greater undertakings, but rather provides them with wings to reach them. This happens when the necessity of love is transformed into a virtuous pursuit, through which the lover strives to reach a state where he is worthy of the beloved object—and perhaps even of something greater, better, and more beautiful still. Thus, he may either rest content having gained what he desires, or become satisfied by his own [inner] beauty, by which he may worthily look down upon the beauty of others, which he has conquered and surpassed. From there, he either finds peace or turns to aspire to even more excellent and magnificent objects. And so the heroic spirit <term>heroic spirit: the soul of the philosopher-lover who is driven by an intense, "heroic" passion to transcend the material world.</term> will continually strive until it sees itself raised to the desire of divine beauty in itself—without likeness, figure, image, or appearance, if such a thing be possible, and if there is more to help you arrive at such a height.

MARICONDO: See then, Cesarino, how this "frenzied one" <term>frenzied one: "il furioso," the central figure of the dialogue, whose "madness" is actually a high intellectual passion.</term> is justified in feeling resentment toward those who criticize him as a captive of "low beauty" to which he offers vows and hangs votive tablets <note>original: "voti, et appenda tabelle." This refers to the ancient practice of placing tablets or offerings in a temple to honor a deity or mark the fulfillment of a vow.</note>. In this way, he does not become a rebel against the voices that call him back to higher undertakings; for, since these lower things derive from those higher ones and have...

CESARINO: God, the divine beauty and splendor, shines and is present in all things; therefore, it does not seem to me an error to admire Him in all things, according to the manner in which He communicates Himself to them. It would certainly be an error if we were to give to others the honor that belongs to Him alone. But what does it mean when he says **LEAVE ME**—leave other desires?

MARICONDO: He banishes from himself those thoughts that present other objects to him, which do not have the power to move him as much, and which want to steal away from him the sight of the sun, which can present itself to him through this window <note>the "window" refers to the specific object of the philosopher's contemplation or the senses themselves</note> more than through others.

CESARINO: How is it that, though importuned by these thoughts, he remains constant in gazing at that splendor which undoes him? And why does it not make him so content that it does not also come to torment him forcefully?

MARICONDO: Because all our comforts in this <term>state of controversy: the human condition of being caught between the material world and the divine, where the soul is in a constant state of struggle or "warfare."</term> are not without their discomforts—which are as great as the comforts are magnificent. Just as the fear of a King is greater, consisting in the loss of a kingdom, than that of a beggar, which consists

<meta>This page continues the First Dialogue of Giordano Bruno’s "The Heroic Frenzies." Following the previous discussion on how divine beauty can be glimpsed through earthly windows, Maricondo now explains why the "heroic" lover experiences such intense emotional extremes compared to those with more mundane concerns.</meta>

->First Dialogue<-

...over the risk of losing ten coins <note>original: "diece danaij." A small sum used here to contrast with the high stakes of a ruler or a philosopher.</note>; the concern of a prince over a republic is more urgent than that of a rustic over a herd of pigs, just as the pleasures and delights of the former are perhaps greater than the delights and pleasures of the latter. Therefore, loving and aspiring higher brings with it greater glory and majesty, along with greater care, thought, and pain. I mean this to be so in this state where one contrary is always joined to another, finding the maximum opposition always within the same kind, and consequently concerning the same subject—even though contraries cannot exist together at the same moment. It is exactly this way, proportionally, in the love of the "Superior Cupid" <note>Intellectual or divine love, as opposed to "Vulgar Cupid," which is physical desire.</note>, just as the Epicurean poet <note>Lucretius (c. 99–55 BC), a Roman poet and philosopher.</note> declared regarding the common and animal Cupid when he said:

> The passion of lovers wavers in uncertain wanderings,
> Nor is it certain what they should first enjoy with eyes or hands;
> What they sought they press tightly, and cause pain 
> To the body, and often sink teeth into the lips 
> And plant kisses, because the pleasure is not pure, 
> And there are stings beneath that incite them to hurt that very thing, 
> Whatever it is—that madness from which these seeds of passion rise. 
> But Venus lightly breaks the pains during love, 
> And a coaxing pleasure, mixed in, restrains the bites; 
> For in it there is hope, from which the origin of passion comes, 
> That the flame can also be extinguished by that same body.
> <note>original Latin: "Fluctuat incertis erroribus ardor amantum... Reſtingui quoq; poſſe ab eodem corpore flammam." These lines are from Lucretius’s *De Rerum Natura*, Book IV. Bruno uses them to illustrate how even physical love is a mix of pain and pleasure, a concept he will apply to the philosopher's "heroic" love for the divine.</note>

<meta>This page continues the First Dialogue of Giordano Bruno’s "The Heroic Frenzies." Following the previous discussion—including a quote from Lucretius—about how pleasure and pain are inextricably linked in human experience, the speaker (Maricondo) now concludes his point on the nature of opposites before Cesare introduces a new symbolic emblem.</meta>

->First Dialogue<-

Here then are the seasonings <note>original: "condimenti." Bruno uses this culinary metaphor to describe how nature makes the "bitterness" of life palatable.</note> with which the mastery and art of nature ensure that one consumes oneself in the pleasure of that which destroys him; and that he becomes content in the midst of torment, and tormented in the midst of all contentments. This is because nothing is produced absolutely from a single peaceful principle, but everything arises from contrary principles through the victory and dominion of one side of the opposition. There is no pleasure of generation <note>The Aristotelian concept of "coming-to-be" or birth.</note> on one hand, without the displeasure of corruption <note>The Aristotelian concept of "passing-away" or decay.</note> on the other. Where these things that are generated and corrupted are joined—as if within the same composite subject—one finds the sensation of delight and sadness existing together. It follows that the state is named delight rather than sadness only if the former happens to be predominant and can stimulate the senses with greater force.

### III. CESARE.

Now let us consider this following image, which is of a Phoenix burning in the sun, and with its smoke it almost seems to obscure the splendor of that very sun by whose heat it is inflamed; and there is a note that says: **NEITHER SIMILAR, NOR EQUAL.** <note>original Latin: "NEQVE SIMILE, NEC PAR." This motto suggests that while the lover (the Phoenix) is transformed by the divine (the Sun), the two remain fundamentally different in scale and nature.</note> **MAR.** <note>Likely an abbreviation for Maricondo, the other speaker in the dialogue.</note> Let the verse be read first.

**MAR.** <note>Maricondo, one of the interlocutors in the dialogue.</note> Without weighing or comparing this man’s studies, I return to what I was saying to you the day before yesterday: that praise is one of the greatest sacrifices that a human affection can make to an object. And leaving aside divine matters, tell me: who would know Achilles, Ulysses, and so many other Greek and Trojan captains? Who would have knowledge of so many great soldiers, sages, and heroes of the earth, if they had not been placed among the stars and deified by the sacrifice of praise, which has kindled the fire on the altar of the hearts of illustrious poets and other reciters? Thus, the sacrificer, the victim, and the canonized deity commonly ascend to heaven together, by the hand and vow of a legitimate and worthy priest.

**CES.** <note>Cesare, another interlocutor.</note> You speak well of "worthy and legitimate priests," for today the world is full of imposters <note>original: "appostici." A Latin proverb describing how mediocre people or fools flatter one another.</note>. Thus the glory of both the one who celebrates and the one who is celebrated is in vain; for the one has woven a statue of straw, or carved a block of wood, or put in...

->## First Dialogue<-

Wherefore a theologian <note>Likely a reference to Pseudo-Dionysius the Areopagite, who emphasized "apophatic" or negative theology—the idea that God is best described by what He is not, and celebrated through silence.</note> rightly said that, since the source of light surpasses by far not only our intellects but also the divine ones, it is fitting that it should be celebrated not with discourses and words, but with silence. **CES.** Not indeed with the silence of brute animals <note>original: "animali bruti." Refers to creatures lacking reason, or humans who live only by their senses.</note> and others who are in the image and likeness of men, but of those whose silence is more illustrious than all the cries, noises, and shouts of those who can be heard.

### IIII. MARICONDO

# First Dialogue.

The heart, which those thoughts wish to refresh,
Cannot withdraw from woes more than a little;
The spirit, which ought to rest for a while,
Does not master its pleasure for even a moment;
The eyes, which should be closed in sleep,
Are open to weeping all night long.
Alas, my lights <note>original: "lumi." A poetic term for the eyes, suggesting they are the windows or lamps of the soul.</note>, with what study and art
Can I tranquilize my troubled senses?
My spirit, in what time and in what places
Will I mitigate your intense pains?
And you, my heart, how can I satisfy you
With that which compensates for your heavy suffering?
->When the due tributes<-
Will the soul give to you, O troubled mind,
Together with the heart, the spirit, and the grieving eyes?

<image-desc>An ornamental woodcut divider featuring symmetrical scrolling foliage and floral patterns.</image-desc>
---

Because the mind aspires to divine splendor, it flees the fellowship of the crowd; it withdraws from common opinion. I say it distances itself not only from a multitude of subjects, but also from the community of studies, opinions, and judgments. This is because the danger of contracting vices and ignorance is greater as the number of people to whom one joins oneself is larger. In public spectacles (said the moral philosopher <note>A reference to the Roman Stoic philosopher Seneca, who famously warned that "the more people we associate with, the greater the danger" of moral corruption.</note>), vices more easily creep in by means of pleasure. If the mind aspires to the high splendor, let it retire as much as it can into unity; let it contract itself as much as possible into itself, in such a way that

<meta>This page continues the "First Dialogue" of Giordano Bruno’s "The Heroic Frenzies."
