# First Dialogue.

[He] will not be like the many, because they are many; and he will not be an enemy of the many because they are different—if it is possible to preserve both goods; otherwise, let him cling to that which seems better to him.

Converse with those whom either he can make better, or by whom he may be made better: for the splendor that he can give to them, or receive from them. Be content more with one suitable person than with the inept multitude; nor will he think he has acquired little when he has become such that he is wise for himself, remembering what Democritus says: "One for me is worth a whole people, and a whole people is as one." <note>original: "VNVS MIHI PRO POPVLO EST. ET POPVLVS PRO VNO." A fragment attributed to the Greek philosopher Democritus, emphasizing the quality of a single wise mind over the quantity of the masses.</note> And what Epicurus wrote to a companion of his studies: "These things I say to you, not to many; for we are a sufficiently large theater for one another." <note>original: "HAEC TIBI, NON MVLTIS. SATIS ENIM MAGNVM ALTER ALTERI THEATRVM SVMVS." A famous quote regarding the self-sufficiency of friendship and intellectual pursuit, requiring no public audience.</note>

The mind, then, that aspires high, first leaves aside any care for the multitude, considering that that light scorns common toil and is found nowhere except where intelligence resides—and not just any intelligence, but that which is among the few, the principal ones, and the first: the first, the principal, and the one. **CES.** How do you understand the mind’s "aspiring high"? For example, by looking at the stars? Toward the empyrean heaven? <note>In traditional cosmology, the Empyrean was the highest, most spiritual part of the universe, composed of pure light or fire.</note> Above the crystalline sphere? <note>The "crystalline heaven" was a sphere in the Ptolemaic system thought to exist between the fixed stars and the Empyrean.</note> **MAR.** Certainly not; but by proceeding into the depths of the mind, for which it will not be

<meta>catchword: necessary</meta>

Maricondo clarifies that the "ascent" of the mind is an internal journey into the depths of the soul, not a physical observation of the stars or heavens.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the "First Dialogue" from Giordano Bruno's "The Heroic Frenzies." Following the previous discussion on why a seeker of wisdom must withdraw from the crowd, the dialogue shifts to the internal nature of divinity. Bruno argues that God is found within the soul rather than through external rituals or physical travel.</meta>

## First Dialogue.

It is not primarily necessary to open one's eyes to the heavens, to raise one's hands on high, to direct one's steps toward the temple, or to ring the ears of idols so that one might be better heard; rather, one must enter into the most intimate part of oneself, considering that God is near, with oneself, and within oneself more than one can even be to oneself—as He who is the soul of souls, the life of lives, and the essence of essences. For consider that what you see above, or below, or around (however you choose to describe it) regarding the stars, are physical bodies—they are creations similar to this globe upon which we reside—and the divinity is no more nor less present in them than it is in our own world, or in ourselves. This, then, is how one must first act: by withdrawing from the multitude into oneself. 

Next, one must become such that he does not merely value but scorns every hardship; so that the more his passions and vices struggle from within, and his corrupt enemies oppose him from without, the more he must draw breath and rise up, and with a singular spirit (if it be possible) conquer this steep mountain. <note>Bruno uses the "steep mountain" as a classic metaphor for the difficult intellectual and spiritual ascent toward truth.</note> Here, no weapons or shields are required other than the greatness of an unconquered mind <note>original: *animo invitto*</note> and a steadfast spirit that maintains the balance and tenor of life. This balance proceeds from knowledge and is guided by the art of contemplating things both high and low, divine and human—for it is in this that the highest good <term>summum bonum</term> <note>The "highest good" or *sommo bene* refers to the ultimate goal of human existence, which for Bruno is the intellectual union with the divine.</note> consists. 

Regarding this, a moral philosopher <note>Referring to the Roman Stoic Seneca the Younger (c. 4 BC – AD 65).</note> who wrote to Lucilius <note>Referring to Seneca’s *Moral Letters to Lucilius*, a series of 124 letters on how to live a virtuous life.</note> said that it is not necessary to navigate the Scyllas or the Cha- <note>The text refers to Scylla and Charybdis, the legendary sea monsters from Greek mythology located in the Strait of Messina, representing great external dangers.</note>

<sig>K.2.</sig>
<meta>catchword: riddi (referring to the completion of "Cariddi" or Charybdis)</meta>

<summary>Bruno argues that the divine is found within the human soul rather than through external religious rituals or physical movement. He emphasizes that an unconquered mind and internal contemplation are the only tools necessary to reach the "highest good."</summary>
<keywords>immanence, internal divinity, Seneca, Lucilius, summum bonum, unconquered mind, intellectual ascent, Scylla and Charybdis</keywords>

Next, let us see what follows: Here is the wheel of time displayed, which moves around its own center; and there is the motto **STAYING, I MOVE** <note>original: *MANENS MOVEOR*</note>. What do you understand by that?

### VI. CESARINO.

All goes well; let us come to what follows. I see a ship tilted upon the waves: its shrouds <note>The ropes used to support the masts of a ship</note> are attached to the shore, and it has the motto: **IT FLUCTUATES IN PORT** <note>original: *FLUCTUAT IN PORTU*</note>. Reason out what this might signify: and if you have determined its meaning, explain. 

MARICONDA. Both the figure and the motto have a certain kinship with the preceding motto and figure, as can easily be understood if one considers them for a moment. But let us read the article <note>original: *articolo* — here referring to the poetic stanza or the specific point of the emblem</note>.

> If by the heroes, by the gods, by the nations
> I am assured that I should not despair;
> Nor let fear, nor sorrow, nor impediments
> Of death, of the body, or of pleasures
> Be things I learn of, suffer, or feel any longer;
> And so that I may see my paths clearly,
> Let doubt, pain, and sadness be extinguished
> By hope, joy, and delights made whole.

From what we have considered and said in the preceding discourses, one can understand the meaning of this, especially where it has been demonstrated that the perception of lower things is weakened and annulled when the superior powers are intensely focused on a more magnificent and <term>heroic object</term> <note>In Bruno’s philosophy, the "heroic object" is the infinite truth or the divine, which the "heroic lover" seeks to contemplate.</note>. So great is the virtue of contemplation (as **Iamblichus** <note>Iamblichus (c. 245–325 AD) was a Neoplatonist philosopher who wrote extensively on the soul's ascent to the divine.</note> notes) that it sometimes happens not only that the soul rests from its lower functions, but that it leaves the body entirely. This I do not wish to be understood in any way other than those explained in the book *On the Thirty Seals* <note>original: *trenta sigilli*. This refers to Bruno's own work, *De triginta sigillis* (1583), which deals with the art of memory and psychological "contractions."</note>, where many modes of <term>contraction</term> <note>The "contraction" (*contrattione*) is a technical term for the soul's ability to withdraw its energy from the physical senses and concentrate it on an internal or spiritual point.</note> are produced. Of these, some are shameful, but others **heroically** ensure that one does not learn the fear of death, does not suffer the pain of the body, and does not feel the hindrances of pleasures. Thus, the hope, the joy, and the delights of the superior spirit are so intensely focused that they extinguish all the passions that could have their origin in **dou-**

...doubt, pain, or any sadness. CESARE:
But what is that thing from which he <note>The "he" refers to the lover/soul mentioned in the preceding poem.</note> requires that it fulfill his desires—which he makes so burning—and asks that it listen to his reasons—which he makes so hollow? MARZIO:
He means the object which gazes upon him the moment he makes himself present to it; for seeing the divinity is the same as being seen by it. Just as seeing the sun coincides with being seen by the sun, so likewise being heard by the divinity is exactly the same as hearing it, and being favored by it is the same as laying oneself open to it. From that single and unchanging source proceed thoughts both uncertain and certain; desires both burning and satisfied; and reasons both heard and hollow; according to whether a person presents themselves worthily or unworthily through their <term>intellect</term> <note>In Bruno’s work, the "intellect" is the power that perceives universal truths.</note>, <term>affection</term> <note>The "affetto" or will, which drives the soul toward the object of its love.</note>, and actions. In the same way, the same pilot is said to be the cause of the sinking or the safety of the ship, depending on whether he is present on it or absent from it; except that the pilot, through his own failure or skill, ruins or saves the ship. But the divine power, which is entirely in everything, does not offer or withdraw itself except through the <term>conversion</term> <note>From the Latin *conversio*, a "turning toward" the light of truth.</note> or <term>aversion</term> <note>From the Latin *aversio*, a "turning away" from the divine.</note> of the other.

---

Therefore, it seems to me that the following figure has a great connection and sequence with this, where there are two stars in the form of

<summary>In this passage, Marzio explains that the soul's experience of God is reciprocal: seeing the divine is the same as being seen by it. He uses the analogy of a ship's pilot to show that while the divine is always present, its effect on us depends on whether we turn toward or away from it.</summary>

By love's hand written you might see

Which is an unworthy reward for my great love;
Let there not be such rigor alongside such splendor.

Open the gates of your gracious gaze;
Look at me, O beautiful one, even if you wish to give me death.

# First Dialogue.

The ascent proceeds in the soul from the faculty and impulse found in its wings, which are the intellect and the intellectual will; through these, the soul naturally refers itself and directs its gaze toward God as the highest good and first truth, and as absolute goodness and beauty.

Just as every thing naturally has a regressive impulse toward its origin and a progressive one toward its end and perfection—as was well said by Empedocles <note>Empedocles (c. 490–430 BCE) was a Greek philosopher who taught that the universe is governed by the forces of Love (attraction) and Strife (repulsion).</note>, from whose judgment it seems to me one can infer what the Nolan <note>Giordano Bruno (1548–100) often referred to himself as "the Nolan" because he was born in Nola, Italy.</note> said in this octave:

> It is fitting that the sun should circle back to where it departs,
> And turn its wandering lights toward its source;
> That which is of earth, to earth should retire,
> And to the sea should run the rivers parted from the sea;
> And from whence they have spirit, and desires are born,
> They should aspire as if to venerable deities:
> Thus, from my goddess every thought
> Being born, it is necessary that it return to my goddess.

The intellectual power never rests, never finds satisfaction in truth already grasped, but always proceeds further and further toward the incomprehensible truth. Likewise, we see that the will, which follows apprehension <note>In Renaissance philosophy, "apprehension" refers to the mind's act of grasping or perceiving an idea.</note>, is never satisfied by any finite thing. Consequently, the essence of the soul refers to no other end than the source of its substance and entity. Through the natural powers, by which it is con-

<meta>This page continues the "First Dialogue" of Giordano Bruno’s *The Heroic Frenzies*. # First Dialogue.

...[the soul] draws toward the care and governance of matter, it turns to relate itself and has an impulse to assist and communicate its perfection to lower things, because of the resemblance it has with divinity. For divinity, out of its own goodness, communicates itself either infinitely by producing—that is, communicating being <note>original: "communicando l'essere"</note> to—the infinite universe and the innumerable worlds within it, or finitely, by producing only this universe which is subject to our eyes and common reason. 

Since, therefore, within the single essence of the soul these two kinds of powers are found—according to whether it is ordered toward its own good or the good of others—it happens that the soul is depicted with a pair of wings, by means of which it is powerful toward the object of the first and immaterial powers; and with a heavy stone, by which it is made capable and effective toward the objects of the second and material powers. 

From this it follows that the entire affection of the "frenzied one" <note>The "furioso" or "frenzied one" refers to the heroic seeker described in the book’s title, whose "frenzy" is actually a divine passion for truth and beauty.</note> is two-fold <note>original: "ancipite," meaning two-headed or pulled in two directions.</note>, divided, and troubled. It is placed in a state of ease for leaning lower rather than forcing itself upward, considering that the soul finds itself in a low and hostile land, and occupies a region far from its more natural home, where its strengths are more diminished. 

**CES.** Do you believe that this difficulty can be remedied?

# First Dialogue.

...it comes to have more air beneath it to sustain it, and consequently is less burdened by gravity. Indeed, it can fly so high that, without the effort of cutting through the air, it cannot return to the depths below—even though it is commonly judged that it would be easier to cut through the air downward toward the earth than upward toward the other stars. 

**CES.** So, through progress in this pursuit, does one always acquire greater and greater ease in mounting upward? 

**MAR.** It is so; as Tansillo <note>Luigi Tansillo (1510–1568) was a celebrated Neapolitan poet. Bruno uses him as a character and a source of wisdom in this dialogue.</note> well said:

> The more air I perceive beneath my feet,
> The more I offer my swift wings to the wind;
> And scorning the world, toward heaven I make my way.

Just as every part of physical bodies and the elements—the closer it approaches its natural place <note>Bruno refers to the Aristotelian idea that elements like fire or earth have a "natural place" they strive to return to; here, the soul's natural place is the divine.</note>—moves with greater impetus and force, until at last (whether it wishes or not) it must arrive there. In the same way that we observe physical parts seeking their own whole bodies, so we must judge intellectual things as they move toward their proper objects, which serve as their natural places, homelands, and goals. From this, you can easily understand the full meaning signified by the emblem, the motto, and the verses <note>original: "carmi"</note>. 

**CES.** To such an extent that anything added to it would seem superfluous to me.

### IX. CESARE.

Now let us see what is represented by those two radiating arrows upon a shield, around which is written THE PRESENT MOMENT HAS CONQUERED? <note>original: "VICIT INSTANS"</note>

MARICONDO. The war continues within the soul of the frenzied lover <note>The "furioso" or frenzied lover is Bruno's term for the seeker of divine wisdom, whose passion drives them beyond ordinary human limits.</note>, which for a long time—due to the great familiarity it had with matter—was harder and unfit to be penetrated by the rays of the splendor of divine intelligence and the beauty of divine goodness. During this time, it is said that the heart, enameled in diamond—that is, the affection that is hard and unfit to be warmed or penetrated—has served as a shield against the blows of love that brought assaults from countless directions. This means it has not felt itself wounded by those wounds of eternal life of which the Song of Songs <note>The "Cantita" refers to the Song of Solomon in the Bible, often interpreted by Renaissance philosophers as an allegory for the soul's love for God.</note> speaks when it says: **YOU HAVE WOUNDED MY HEART, O MY BELOVED, YOU HAVE WOUNDED MY HEART.** <note>original Latin: "VULNERASTI COR MEUM, O DILECTA, VULNERASTI COR MEUM."</note> These wounds are not made of iron or any other material moved by the vigor and strength of muscles; rather, they are the arrows of Diana or of Phoebus. They belong either to the goddess of the wildernesses of the contemplation of Truth <note>original: "Veritade"</note>—that is, Diana, who represents the order of fruitful intelligences that reflect the splendor received from the First Principle to communicate it to those deprived of a clearer vision—or perhaps they come from the more principal deity, Apollo, who with his own innate and unborrowed splendor sends out his arrows, which are his rays, from

<meta>This page continues the First Dialogue of Giordano Bruno’s "The Heroic Frenzies." # First Dialogue.

...innumerable parts, so many and of such kind that they are all the species of things <note>In Renaissance philosophy, "species" are the mental forms or images of objects that the mind perceives and processes.</note>, which indicate divine goodness, intelligence, beauty, and wisdom. According to various orders of apprehension, [the soul’s faculties] become frenzied lovers, so that the diamond-like subject <note>The "diamond-like subject" refers to the stubborn or "hard" human heart/soul mentioned on the previous page that initially resists divine influence.</note> no longer reflects the light impressed upon its surface; instead, softened and tamed by the heat and light, it becomes entirely luminous in its substance—entirely light—because it has been penetrated within its affection and conception. 

This does not happen immediately at the beginning of generation <note>That is, at the moment of birth or the soul’s entry into the body.</note>, when the soul has newly emerged to be intoxicated by Lethe <note>In Greek mythology, the river of forgetfulness; Bruno uses it here to describe the soul losing its divine knowledge upon entering a physical body.</note> and soaked in the waves of oblivion and confusion. From this, the spirit becomes more captive to the body and is put to work in the functions of "vegetation" <note>The "vegetative" soul refers to basic biological life: growth, nutrition, and reproduction.</note>; little by little, it is "digested" <note>Bruno uses the metaphor of digestion to describe the soul's process of refining raw sensory experience into higher thought.</note> so as to be fit for the acts of the sensing faculty, until, through the rational and discursive faculty, it reaches a purer intellectual state. From there, it can be introduced to the mind and no longer feel clouded by the vapors of that "humor" which, through the exercise of contemplation, has not rotted in the stomach but is maturely digested.

In this disposition, the present frenzied lover shows himself to have lasted for six lustra <note>A "lustrum" is a five-year period; the lover has thus been in this state for thirty years.</note>, during which time he had not yet reached that purity of concept that could make him a capable dwelling for those foreign species <note>Higher, divine ideas that are "foreign" to the material world.</note> which, offering themselves equally to all, are always knocking at the door of intelligence. Finally, the love that had assaulted him from various sides and at various times...

<meta>This page continues the First Dialogue of Giordano Bruno’s "The Heroic Frenzies." # First Dialogue.

...how in vain (just as the sun is said to shine and warm in vain for those who are in the bowels of the earth and the opaque deep) [is light], were it not for its having encamped in those holy lights—that is, by having shown through two intelligible species <note>The "intelligible species" are the mental forms or images that allow the intellect to grasp non-physical realities like beauty or truth.</note> that divine beauty which, by the reasoning of truth, bound his intellect, and by the reasoning of goodness, warmed his affection. 

Material and sensory pursuits, which previously used to triumph, as it were, were overcome, remaining (to the detriment of the soul’s excellence) intact; because those lights, which the illuminating agent intellect <note>The "agent intellect" (intelletto agente) is a concept in Aristotelian and Neoplatonic philosophy referring to the part of the mind that acts upon sensory data to create universal understanding.</note> and sun of intelligence made present, found easy entry through his own lights: that of truth [entered] through the gate of the intellectual power, and that of goodness through the gate of the appetitive power <note>The "appetitive power" refers to the faculty of the soul that desires or moves toward what it perceives as good; essentially the will.</note>, into the heart—that is, into the substance of the general affection. 

This was that double arrow that came as if from the hand of an angry warrior—that is, more ready, more effective, and more daring than it had for so long before shown itself when it was weak or negligent. Then, when he was first so warmed and illuminated in his conception, that was the victorious point and moment of which it is said: 

**THE INSTANT CONQUERED.** <note>original: "**VICIT INSTANS.**" This refers to the decisive moment of spiritual transformation where the soul is suddenly overcome by divine light.</note>

From this you may understand the meaning of the proposed figure, motto, and article which says:

<meta>This page continues the "First Dialogue" of Giordano Bruno’s *The Heroic Frenzies*. In the previous section, the author established that the soul is moved from a state of sensory confusion toward intellectual clarity. We now see the poetic description of this "conquest" of the soul by divine beauty, followed by a philosophical breakdown of how this beauty enters the heart.</meta>

# First Dialogue.

<image-desc>Decorative horizontal typographic ornament with scrolling floral and vine motifs centered around a circular element.</image-desc>

Against the blows of Love I built a strong defense,
When my heart, glazed with diamond, endured
Assaults from various quarters, and so many;
Wherefore my studies triumphed over his.
At last (as the heavens destined)
One day he encamped in those holy lights,
Which, through mine own, alone among all others,
Found easy entry to my heart.
From there, he hurled that double arrow at me,
Which came from the hand of an angry warrior,
And which for thirty years <note>Original: "sei lustri." A "lustrum" is a five-year period; Bruno refers to his age or the duration of his intellectual resistance.</note> had failed to wound me:
He marked that spot and held fast there,
Planting his trophy over me, from where he is able
To keep my fleeting wings restrained.
Thenceforth, with more solemn
Preparation, the rages of my sweet enemy
Never cease to strike my heart.

<image-desc>Decorative horizontal typographic ornament with scrolling floral motifs, matching the one above.</image-desc>

A singular instant was the end of the beginning and the perfection of victory. Unique twin species <note>The "twin species" refer to the two ways the divine manifests to the mind: as Truth (intellect) and as Goodness (will/affection).</note> were those which alone among all others found easy entry; seeing as they contain within themselves the efficacy and virtue of all the others; for what better and more excellent form can present itself than

<meta>catchword: of that</meta>

<vocab>love, diamond, triumphed, arrow, warrior, trophy, victory, virtue, excellent</vocab>

<summary>In this poem and its following commentary, Bruno describes the moment the soul's intellectual defenses are overcome by divine beauty, which enters through the "twin gates" of truth and goodness. This marked a permanent shift in his life, ending thirty years of resistance to these spiritual "assaults."</summary>

...that beauty, goodness, and truth, which is the source of every other truth, goodness, and beauty? **HE MARKED THAT SPOT**, took possession of the affection, re-marked it, and imprinted his character upon it; **AND HE HELD FAST THERE**, and has confirmed, established, and sanctioned it so strongly that he can no longer lose it; for it is impossible for one to turn to love anything else once they have understood divine beauty in their conception. And it is impossible for him not to love it, just as it is impossible for the appetite <note>In Renaissance philosophy, "appetite" refers to the soul's natural drive or desire toward an object, whether sensory or intellectual.</note> to fall upon anything other than the good or a semblance of the good. And therefore, the desire for the highest good must especially prevail. 

Thus the wings are restrained—those wings which used to be fleeting, rushing downward with the weight of matter. Thus from that place, they never cease to strike, stirring the affection and awakening the thought: those sweet rages <note>Original: "dolci ire." Bruno uses oxymorons—contradictory terms—to describe the "heroic frenzy," where the spiritual pain of longing for God is experienced as a profound joy.</note> which are the effective assaults of the gracious enemy, who for so long was kept excluded, a stranger, and a wanderer. He is now the sole and entire possessor and disposer of the soul; because she neither wishes, nor wants to wish for, anything else; nor does she find pleasure in, nor does she want to find pleasure in, anything else, whence she often says:

> Sweet rages, sweet war, sweet darts,
> My sweet wounds, my sweet sorrows.

# First Dialogue

...assigned and defined by nature: In the air, the eagle; on the earth, the lion; in the water, the whale <note>Original: "ceto."</note>. Each of these, as it demonstrates more strength and dominion than the others, also comes to make an open act of magnanimity <term>Magnanimity: From the Latin *magna anima* (great soul), it refers to a noble-mindedness that disdains pettiness and treachery.</term>, or something similar to magnanimity. > But the savage goddess, having found from her watchtower a time for harm,
> Seeks the high rooftops, and from the stable’s highest peak
> She sounds the pastoral signal, and through the curved horn
> She strains her Tartarean <term>Tartarean: Relating to Tartarus, the deepest abyss of the underworld; hence, infernal or terrifying.</term> voice, at which immediately every
> Grove trembled, and the deep woods thundered.
> <note>Original Latin: "At sæua é speculis tempus dea nacta nocēdi, Ardua tecta petit, stabuli et de culmine summo Pastorale canit signū, cornuq; recuruo Tartareā intendit vocē, qua protinus omne Contremuit nemus, et siluæ intonuere profundæ." From Virgil’s *Aeneid*, Book VII.</note>

Regarding the Eagle, it is also known that when she wishes to proceed with her hunting, she first rises straight up from the nest in a perpendicular line, and usually on the third time she pounces from on high with greater impetus and speed than if she were flying in a flat line. Thus, in the time during which she seeks the advantage of flight velocity, she also takes the opportunity to scout her prey from afar, which she either despairs of catching or resolves to take after making three gazes. 

**CE.** Could we conjecture for what reason, if the prey presented itself to her eyes at the first gaze, she does not immediately launch herself upon it? 

**MA.** Certainly not [out of weakness]. But perhaps she waits to distinguish if a better or more convenient prey might present itself to her.

# First Dialogue

Furthermore, I do not believe this is always the case, but it is so for the most part. Now let us come to our point. Regarding the **whale** <note>Original: "ceto ó Balena."</note>, it is a clear thing that, being such a massive animal, it cannot divide the waters without its presence being sensed by the rebuff of the waves; added to this is the fact that many species of this fish are found which, by their movement and breathing, spout out a windy storm of watery spray. From all three species of these principal animals, therefore, inferior animals have the ability to take time for escape; so that they do not proceed in a subtle or treacherous manner.

But **Love**, which is stronger and greater, and which has supreme dominion in heaven, on earth, and in the sea—and which, by comparison to these others, should perhaps demonstrate a magnanimity <term>Magnanimity: Greatness of soul; the quality of being noble and generous, especially toward an adversary.</term> all the more excellent as it has more power—nevertheless assaults and wounds unexpectedly and suddenly.

> Nor does the wound inflicted show a broad front;
> But it consumes the innermost hidden marrows,

...sea, on land, in heaven, this one comes to (as it were) tyrannize the universe. 

**CESARINO**: There is no greater empire, no worse tyranny, no better lordship, no more necessary power, no thing more sweet and suave; one finds no food more austere and bitter, no deity more violent, no god more pleasant, no agent more treacherous and feigning, no author more royal and faithful. # First Dialogue

> Do not waste your efforts here,
> Because (O fair god <note>Refers to Cupid, the god of Love.</note>), unless it be in vain, then wrongly
> Do you seek further to slay one who is already dead.

<image-desc>Decorative horizontal woodcut ornament featuring symmetrical scrollwork, acanthus leaves, and a central stylized mask or floral motif.</image-desc>

---

This whole meaning is metaphorical, like the others, and it can be understood through the feelings of those souls. Here, the multitude of arrows that have wounded and continue to wound the heart signify the innumerable individuals and species of things in which the splendor of divine beauty shines forth, according to their various degrees. From these things, the affection for the proposed and perceived Good is set aflame. Both of these—the individuals and the species—through the logic of <term>potentiality and act</term> <note>A classic Aristotelian concept: "potentiality" is the capacity to become or do something, while "act" is the actual realization of that capacity.</note>, and <term>possibility and effect</term>, both torment and console; they provide a sense of sweetness while also making one feel bitterness.

But where the entire affection is completely converted to God—that is, to the <term>Idea of Ideas</term> <note>In Platonic philosophy, the "Idea of Ideas" is the supreme, ultimate form from which all other concepts and things derive their existence.</note>—by the light of intelligible things, the mind becomes exalted to a <term>super-essential unity</term> <note>A state of spiritual transcendence where the soul rises above its own individual nature to become one with the divine source.</note>. It becomes all love, entirely one; it no longer feels itself provoked by diverse objects that might distract it. Instead, it is a single wound in which the entire affection converges, and which becomes that very affection itself. 

In such a state, there is no love or appetite for any particular thing that can provoke or even present itself before the <term>will</term> <note>Italian: *voluntade*; Bruno views the will as the driving force that follows the intellect toward the divine.</note>, because there is nothing more "right" than the Right, nothing more beautiful than Beauty, and nothing better than Good... <note>Original: "bontà". This sentence concludes on the following page.</note>

<summary>In this passage, Maricondo explains that the "arrows" striking the heart represent the many ways divine beauty appears in the world. He describes how the soul eventually transcends these individual distractions to reach a state of total, unified love for the divine "Idea of Ideas."</summary>

...nothing is found greater than Greatness, nor anything more radiant than that Light which, by its presence, obscures and cancels out all other lights. CESARI: To the perfect, if it truly is perfect, nothing can be added; therefore, the will is no longer capable of any other desire when that which belongs to the perfect, the supreme, and the greatest is present to it. I can, then, understand the conclusion where it says to Love: **DO NOT WASTE YOUR EFFORTS HERE.**

Because if not in vain, then wrongly (as one speaks through a certain likeness and metaphor) do you try to kill one who is already dead. That is, one who has no more life or sensation regarding other objects, through which he might be stung or pierced: why should he be further exposed to other <term>species</term> <note>In Renaissance philosophy, "species" are the sensory or intellectual images through which we perceive the world. To be "exposed to other species" means to be distracted by new sensory information.</note>? And this lament belongs to one who, having tasted that highest unity, wishes to be entirely exempt and withdrawn from the multitude. MARICONDO: You understand very well.

Here I do not trust myself to understand or determine everything that the frenzied one <note>original: "il furioso". <meta>This page continues the prose commentary from the previous page, detailing the psychological state of the "heroic enthusiast" who feels overwhelmed by his spiritual or intellectual quest. The text picks up from the word "apprehension" (apprehens...) on the previous page, finishing with the phrase "better suited to the pursuit" (meglio atto al negocio).</meta>

...better suited to the pursuit, he does not know where or whither he should turn; no place of flight or refuge is shown, for from every side threaten the waves of fearful and mortal fury. **TO ONE WHO DOES NOT KNOW THE PORT, NO WIND IS HIS OWN.** <note>original Latin: "Ignoranti portum, nullus suus ventus est." A famous maxim from Seneca’s *Letters to Lucilius*, meaning that if a person does not know which port they are sailing toward, no wind is favorable.</note> He sees that he who has committed himself much—and indeed too much—to fortuitous things, has built for himself disturbance, imprisonment, ruin, and submersion. He sees how fortune toys with us; that which she places kindly into our hands, she either causes to break by making it spill from those very hands, or causes it to be taken by the violence of others, or causes it to suffocate and poison us, or pesters us with suspicion, fear, and jealousy, to the great harm and ruin of the possessor. **DO YOU THINK ANY GIFTS OF FORTUNE ARE FREE FROM DECEITS?** <note>original Latin: "Fortunae an ulla putatis dona carere dolis?" Adapted from Virgil’s *Aeneid*, where it refers to the treachery of the Trojan Horse. Bruno applies it here to the unreliability of worldly luck.</note>

Now, because the strength that cannot test itself is void; the magnanimity <note>original: "magnanimitá". In Renaissance philosophy, this refers to a "greatness of soul" that seeks high honors and truth, but here it is rendered powerless by circumstances.</note> that cannot prevail is nothing, and study without fruit is in vain; he sees the effects of the fear of evil, which is worse than the evil itself. **FEAR OF DEATH IS WORSE than death itself.** <note>original Latin: "Peior est morte timor ipse mortis." A common Stoic sentiment suggesting that anticipation of suffering is more painful than the suffering itself.</note> Already through fear he suffers everything he dreads to suffer: horror in the limbs, weakness in the nerves, trembling of the body, anxiety of the spirit; and that which has not yet overtaken him becomes present, and is certainly worse than what might actually arrive. What thing is more foolish than to grieve for a future thing, which is absent, and which when present is not felt?

<meta>catchword: CES.</meta>

<summary>Bruno analyzes the psychological paralysis of the seeker who lacks a clear goal, illustrating how the fear of failure and the unpredictability of Fortune are more damaging than any actual misfortune.</summary>

# First Dialogue

**Cica**, These are considerations regarding the surface and the narrative of the figure <note>This likely refers to an emblem or symbolic image the characters have been analyzing throughout the dialogue.</note>. But the true aim of the heroic enthusiast <term>heroic enthusiast: *furioſo heroico*, a central concept in Bruno's philosophy describing a person driven by a "divine madness" or intellectual passion to transcend the physical world</term>, I think, concerns the frailty of the human intellect <term>human intellect: *ingegno humano*, the natural capacity of the mind</term>. When fixed upon the divine quest, it suddenly finds itself engulfed in the abyss of incomprehensible excellence; whence sense and imagination become confused and absorbed. Not knowing how to advance, nor turn back, nor where to turn, the intellect vanishes and loses its own being, no differently than a drop of water that vanishes in the sea, or a small breath <note>original: "picciol ſpirito". In this context, it refers to a small puff of air or vapor.</note> that thins out, losing its own substance in the spacious and immense air. **Tans.** Well, but let us continue our discussion as we head toward our lodging, for it is night.

->End of the first Dialogue.<-

# Second Dialogue

## MARICONDA

<image-desc>A square woodcut illustration of an emblem depicting a male figure within a circular border, set inside a square frame with floral scrollwork. The figure is interacting with a flaming yoke, a symbol of divine servitude that paradoxically brings spiritual lightness.</image-desc>

Go, see a flaming yoke wrapped in bonds, around which is written: **LIGHTER THAN THE BREEZE.** <note>original Latin: "LEVIUS AURA"</note> This is meant to signify how divine love <term>divine love: *amor diuino*, the spiritual force that draws the soul toward God</term> does not burden, nor does it drag its servant, captive, and slave down to the depths; rather, it exalts him, lifts him up, and magnifies him above any kind of worldly liberty.

**CESARINO.** I pray you, let us quickly read the verse, so that we may consider its meaning with more order, propriety, and brevity—provided nothing else is found within it. 

**MARICONDA.** It says thus:

Who woke my mind to a different love,
Who makes every other goddess seem base and vain to me,
In whom beauty and sovereign goodness
Manifest themselves most uniquely;
She is the one I saw emerge from the forest,
My Diana, who hunts me, <note>Diana: The Roman goddess of the hunt and the moon. In this context, she represents a high, divine beauty that "hunts" the soul of the seeker.</note>
Among beautiful nymphs upon the Campanian breeze, <note>Campanian breeze: referring to the region of Campania in Italy, the area surrounding Naples where Bruno was born.</note>
For whom I said to Love: "I surrender to her."
And he said to me: "O fortune, O lover,
O welcome comfort from your fate:
For she alone, among the many and many
Held in the womb of life and death,
Who most adorns the world with holy graces,
You obtained through study and through fate,
In the court of love,
Such a highly happy captive,
That you envy no other man or god who goes free."

<header>Second Dialogue.</header>

...which are illuminated by only a few. Diana, the splendor of **intelligible species**, <note>intelligible species: In Neoplatonic philosophy, these are the pure, non-material forms or ideas that the mind can perceive, as opposed to physical objects known through the senses.</note> is a huntress of herself, because with her beauty and grace she wounded him first, and then bound him to herself, and holds him under her command more content than he could have ever been otherwise. He describes her among **beautiful nymphs**—that is, among the multitude of other species, forms, and ideas—and upon the **Campanian breeze**, meaning that intellect and spirit which manifested itself at Nola, <note>Nola: Giordano Bruno’s birthplace near Naples, Italy. He often refers to himself as "the Nolan."</note> which lies upon the plain of the Campanian horizon. To her he surrendered; she, more than any other, was praised to him by Love, who wishes him to consider himself so fortunate for her sake—for she, among all those present or absent from the eyes of mortals, most highly adorns the world and makes man glorious and beautiful. Hence he says his mind has awakened to an **excellent love**, which takes the place of every other concern—that is, the care and observance of any other species, which is base and vain.

Now, in saying that he has awakened his mind to a "high love," he offers us an example of how to magnify the heart as much as possible through thoughts, studies, and works, and not to occupy ourselves with base things beneath our capacity. This is what happens to those who—whether through greed, negligence, or some other worthlessness—remain attached to unworthy things during this brief span of life.

**CESARE:** It is necessary that there be artisans, mechanics, laborers, servants, footmen, ignoble—

<header>Second Dialogue.</header>

...vile, poor, pedants, and other similar types; for otherwise there could not be **philosophers**, contemplatives, cultivators of minds, masters, captains, nobles, illustrious ones, the rich, the wise, and others who are **heroic** and similar to the gods. Therefore, why should we strive to corrupt the state of nature, which has distinguished the universe into major and minor things, superior and inferior, illustrious and obscure, worthy and unworthy? This is true not only outside of us, but even within us, in our very substance, reaching even to that part of our substance which is declared to be immaterial. Just as among the **intelligences** <term>intelligences: In Renaissance philosophy, these are spiritual beings or celestial movers that govern the order of the cosmos.</term> some are subject and others preeminent—some serve and obey, while others command and govern.

Therefore, I believe this should not be used as an example <note>The speaker warns that encouraging everyone to pursue "high love" indiscriminately might lead to social or cosmic disorder.</note> so that subjects, wishing to be superiors, and the ignoble, wishing to be equal to the noble, do not come to pervert and confound the order of things. Otherwise, there would eventually result a certain neutrality and **beastly equality**, such as is found in certain deserted and uncultivated republics. Do you not see, moreover, into what ruin the sciences have fallen for this reason: that the **pedants** <note>Bruno uses "pedant" as a derogatory term for those who focus on the surface level of grammar and rhetoric rather than the deep truths of philosophy.</note> have wished to be philosophers, to treat natural things, and to meddle in determining divine matters? Who does not see how much evil has happened and happens by having awakened minds to "high loves" when they are not suited for them? Who has good sense and does not see the profit made by **Aristotle**, who was a master of letters... <note>original: "maestro de lettere" — Bruno likely implies that Aristotle's greatness lay in his logic and language, but that his followers (the pedants) have misused his authority to stifle new philosophical inquiry.</note>

# Second Dialogue.

...humanly to Alexander <note>Likely Alexander of Aphrodisias (fl. 200 AD), a celebrated commentator on Aristotle whose logical interpretations Bruno often opposed.</note> when he applied his spirit on high to oppose and wage war against the Pythagorean doctrine and that of the natural philosophers. He wished, with his logical reasoning, to establish definitions, notions, certain "fifth entities" <term>fifth entities: A reference to "quiddities" or abstract essences in Scholastic philosophy which Bruno considered to be mental fictions rather than physical realities.</term>, and other fragments and abortions of fantastic thought as the principles and substance of things. He was more studious of the faith of the common people and the foolish multitude—who are led and guided more by sophisms and appearances found on the surface of things—than of the truth, which is hidden in the substance of things, and is indeed their very substance. He stirred his mind not to become a contemplator, but a judge and passer of sentences on things he had never studied nor well understood. 

So in our times, whatever bit of good and singular value he brings in terms of inventive reason, judgment, and metaphysics, through the ministry of other pedants who labor with the same **LIFT UP YOUR HEARTS** <note>original: "SVRSVM CORDA." A Latin liturgical phrase from the Mass. Bruno uses it ironically here to describe the self-important or pseudo-spiritual attitude of narrow-minded scholars.</note>, new dialects and ways of forming reason are instituted. These are as much more vile than those of Aristotle as Aristotle’s philosophy is perhaps incomparably more vile than that of the ancients <note>Bruno refers here to the "Prisca Theologia" or the philosophy of the "ancients" like Pythagoras and the Egyptians, which he believed was superior to Aristotelian logic.</note>. This has happened simply because certain grammarians, after growing old in the nurseries <note>original: "culine," literally "kitchens," used here as a derogatory term for the lowly or "messy" work of teaching children.</note> of children and the anatomy of phrases and words, have wished to rouse their minds to create new logics and metaphysics, judging and passing sentence upon those which they never stud—

...day and hour they do not understand: wherefore thus these men, with the favor of the ignorant multitude (to whose intellect they are more conformed), will be able to bring down the humanities and reasonings of Aristotle, just as he was the executioner of others' divine philosophies. See, then, what this counsel usually promotes: that all should aspire to the holy splendor, and hold all other undertakings as vile and vain.

**MAR.** <note>Maricondo, one of the primary speakers in this dialogue.</note>

> Laugh, if you are wise, O girl, laugh,
> The Pelignian poet <note>original: "Pelignus poeta." This refers to Ovid, who was born in the Peligni region of Italy. The verse itself is a variation on Martial, Epigrams 2.41.</note> (I believe) had said;
> But he had not said it to all girls:
> And if he had said it to all girls,

So the **LIFT UP YOUR HEARTS** <note>original: "SURSUM CORDA." A Latin liturgical call from the Mass. Bruno uses it here to signify the call to higher intellectual or spiritual contemplation.</note> is not intoned for everyone, but for those who have wings. We see clearly that pedantry <term>pedantry: For Bruno, this is not just being "fussy," but a dangerous intellectual disease where scholars prioritize the study of words and rigid logic over the actual study of nature and the universe.</term> has never been in higher exaltation to govern the world than in our times; it creates as many paths toward true intelligible species <term>intelligible species: In Renaissance philosophy, these are the mental forms or "ideas" through which we understand the essence of things.</term> and objects of the unique, infallible truth as there are individual pedants. Therefore, at this time especially, well-born spirits—armed with truth and enlightened by divine intelligence—must be awakened to take up arms against dark ignorance, climbing the high fortress and eminent tower of contemplation. For these people, it is fitting to hold every other undertaking as vile and vain.

<meta>This page continues the speaker's critique of "pedants"—intellectuals who focus on the trivialities of grammar and logic rather than the vastness of the universe. ### Second Dialogue

These men should not spend their time on light and vain things, for its speed is infinite; since the present rushes by so wonderfully fast, and the future approaches with that same swiftness. What we have lived is nothing; what we are living is a mere point; what we have yet to live is not even a point yet, but it can become a point which will simultaneously *become* and *have been* <note>Bruno is reflecting on the fleeting nature of time, suggesting that the "present" is an infinitesimal moment between a past that is gone and a future not yet arrived.</note>. Meanwhile, one man weaves a memory of genealogies, another attends to deciphering old scripts, and another is occupied with multiplying childish sophisms <term>sophism: a clever but false argument, often used in the Renaissance to mock scholars who favored rigid logic over reality</term>. You will see, for example, a volume full of:

->**HEART** is the source of life, **SNOW** is white: and the **CROW** is the source of white life.<-
<note>original: "COR Est fons vitæ, NIX est alba : et CORNIX est fons vitæ albæ." This is a parody of a syllogism—a form of logical reasoning. The pedant has combined the Latin word "Cor" (Heart) and "Nix" (Snow) to make "Cornix" (Crow), creating a nonsensical "logical" conclusion to mock academic absurdity.</note>

That other one chatters about whether the noun or the verb came first; another whether it was the sea or the springs; another wants to revive obsolete words <note>original: "uocaboli absoleti." Bruno is likely mocking the "Ciceronians" or archaic linguists of his time who tried to resurrect dead vocabulary to sound more prestigious.</note> which, because they were once used for the purpose of an ancient writer, he now wants to make them rise to the stars once more. Another obsesses over false and true orthography <term>orthography: the conventional spelling system of a language</term>; and others still are concerned with various similar frivolities, which are much more worthily despised than understood. Here they fast, here they grow thin, here they become consumptive, here they wrinkle their skin, here they grow long beards, here they rot away, and here they place the anchor of their "highest good." With this, they claim to despise fortune; with this, they build a shelter and place a shield against the lances of fate.

<sig>M.4.</sig>

### Second Dialogue

With such and similar vile thoughts they believe they rise above others, become equal to the gods, and understand the beautiful and the good that philosophy promises. CESARE. It is a great thing indeed that time—which cannot suffice even for necessary things, no matter how diligently guarded—comes for the most part to be spent on superfluous things; or rather, things base and shameful.

One should not laugh at what makes **Archimedes** <note>Archimedes (c. 287–212 BCE), the famous Greek mathematician of Syracuse</note> or others praiseworthy in the eyes of some: that at a time when his city was being turned upside down, everything was in ruin, fire was lit in his room, and enemies were inside his chamber at his back—at whose discretion and will it lay to make him lose his art, his mind, and his life—he had meanwhile lost all sense and intention of saving his life. He had left it behind to pursue, perhaps, the proportion of the curve to the straight line, of the diameter to the circle, or other similar mathematical studies <term>mathesi: derived from the Greek word for "learning," used here to refer specifically to the mathematical sciences</term>. These are as worthy for youths as they are unworthy of one who (if he could) should have grown old and been attentive to things more worthy of being set as the goal of human study. MARICONDO. In regard to this, I like what you yourself said a little while ago: that it is necessary for the world to be full of all sorts of persons, and that the number of the imperfect, the ugly, the poor, the unworthy, and the wicked must be greater; and in conclusion, it ought not to be otherwise than as it is. The long age and old age of Ar-

### Second Dialogue

...[Ar]chimedes, **Euclid** <note>Euclid (c. 300 BCE), the Greek mathematician known as the "father of geometry"</note>, **Priscian** <note>Priscianus Caesariensis, a Latin grammarian of the 6th century</note>, **Donatus** <note>Aelius Donatus, a 4th-century grammarian whose work became the standard elementary Latin textbook</note>, and others who, at the moment of death, were found occupied with numbers, lines, words, concordances, writings, dialects, formal syllogisms, methods, ways of knowing, *organa* <note>original: "organi"; referring to instruments of logic or Aristotle’s collection of logical treatises</note>, and other <term>isagogics: from the Greek "eisagoge," meaning introductory or preparatory studies</term>. Such things were ordained for the service of youth and children, so they might learn and receive the fruits of the mature age of those great men, as it is fitting that these subjects be handled by them in their tender years; to the end that, as they grow more adult, they may arrive without impediment, fit and ready for greater things.

**CESARE.** I am not straying from the point I raised a short while ago: I am speaking of those who make it their study to steal the fame and standing of the ancients by producing new works that are either worse or no better than those already made. They spend their lives on considerations such as putting forward **goat’s wool** <term>lana di capra: a proverbial expression meaning a matter of no importance or a distinction without a difference</term> or the **shadow of an ass** <note>original: "ombra de l'asino"; an ancient Greek idiom referring to a dispute over something entirely worthless</note>. There are others who, throughout their entire lives, study to become exquisite in those subjects that are appropriate for childhood, and for the most part they do so without profit to themselves or to anyone else.

**MARICONDO.** Now enough has been said regarding those who cannot and should not dare to have **THEIR MIND AWAKENED BY A HIGHER LOVE.** <note>original: "AD ALT' AMOR LA MENTE DESTA"</note> Let us come now to consider the voluntary captivity and the pleasant yoke under the dominion of the aforementioned **Diana** <note>In this philosophical context, Diana represents the contemplative life, the reflection of divine truth, or the hunt for intellectual wisdom</note>. I speak of that yoke without which the soul is powerless to climb back to that height from which it fell, for this yoke renders the soul lighter and more agile; and

### Second Dialogue

...the laces make it more expeditious and free. 

**CESARE.** Discuss then. 

**MARICONDO.** To begin, continue, and conclude in an orderly fashion, I consider that everything that lives—in whatever way it lives—must necessarily nourish and feed itself in some manner. Therefore, for the **intellectual nature**, no other pasture is suitable than the intellectual, just as for the body there is none but the corporal. This is because nourishment is taken for no other purpose than to be transformed into the substance of the one being nourished. Since, therefore, the body is not transmuted into spirit, nor the spirit into body (for all transmutation occurs when matter that was under the form of one thing comes to be under the form of another), so spirit and body share no common matter, such that what was the subject of one could become the subject of the other. 

**CESARE.** Certainly, if the soul fed upon the body, it would fare better where matter is most fertile (as **Iamblichus** <note>Iamblichus (c. 245–325 CE), a Syrian Neoplatonist philosopher who wrote extensively on the soul and its relationship to the divine</note> argues). Thus, when a fat and heavy body appears before us, we might believe it to be the vessel of a vigorous, firm, ready, and heroic soul, and say: "O fat soul! O fertile spirit! O beautiful wit! O divine intelligence! O illustrious mind! O blessed <term>hypostasis: the fundamental substance or essential nature of a being</term> fit to provide a feast for lions or a banquet for the **Dogs**!" <note>original: "Dogs"; this may be a literal reference to animals or a punning allusion to the Cynic philosophers, whose name derives from the Greek word for dog</note>. By that logic, an old man—appearing withered, weak, and diminished in strength—would have to be judged as having little wit <note>original: "poco sale," literally "little salt"; an Italian idiom for lacking common sense or intelligence</note>, discourse, or reason. But pray, continue.

<meta>catchword: MAR.</meta>

<summary>Maricondo argues that the mind and body require different types of nourishment because they are made of different substances, while Cesare humorously points out the absurdity of judging intellectual depth by physical size.</summary>
<keywords>intellectual nature, nourishment of the soul, Iamblichus, hypostasis, mind-body dualism, humor, aging and intellect</keywords>

### Second Dialogue

MAR. Now we must say that the lure of the mind is that alone which
is always craved, sought, and embraced by it, and gladly tasted
more than any other thing, through which it is filled, satisfied, 
finds benefit, and becomes better; that is, the truth, 
to which man always aspires in every time, in every age, and 
in whatever state he may find himself; and for which he is 
wont to despise any labor, to attempt every study, to take 
no account of the body, and to hold this life in hatred. 
Because truth is an incorporeal thing; because 
nothing—whether it be physical, or metaphysical, or 
mathematical—is found within the body; because 
you see that the eternal human essence is not in 
the individuals who are born and die. 
It is the specific unity <note>The "specific unity" refers to the universal species or "humanness" that defines all people, rather than the specific traits of one individual.</note> (said **Plato**) and not the nu-
merical multitude that carries the substance 
of things: therefore he called the **Idea** <term>Idea: in Platonic philosophy, these are the eternal, perfect "Forms" or templates of things that exist in a higher reality beyond our physical world</term> one and 
many, stable and mobile: because as an incor-
ruptible species it is something intelligible and one, and 
as it communicates itself to matter and is subject to 
motion and generation, it is something sensible and 
many. In this second way it has more of <term>non-being: a state of constant change or lack of permanence, which philosophers contrasted with the eternal "being" of truth</term> 
than of being: seeing as it is always one thing then another, 
and runs eternally through <term>privation: the philosophical concept of a lack or absence of a form; the state of being "not yet" what something is becoming</term>: in the 
first way it is being and truth. See further 
that the mathematicians take it as granted 
that true figures <note>Perfect geometric shapes like a true circle or an absolute straight line.</note> are not found in natural 
bodies, nor can they be there by power 
of nature or of art. You know also that the ve-
rity of supernatural substances is above 
matter.

--- Page 224 ---
<meta>This page continues the Second Dialogue from the previous section, where Maricondo (MAR.) discusses the intellectual appetite. Having established that truth is the proper "food" for the mind, he now explains how the human soul is naturally equipped to "hunt" for this truth within the physical world.</meta>

### Second Dialogue

It is therefore concluded that whoever seeks the truth must rise above the logic of bodily things. Beyond this, it must be considered that everything which feeds itself possesses a certain mind and a natural memory of its food; it always holds the likeness and form of that food present (especially when the need is greatest). This presence is all the more elevated when the one who aspires is more noble, and the object being sought is more glorious. From this follows a principle: every living thing has an innate intelligence regarding those things belonging to the preservation of the individual and the species, as well as its final perfection. Upon this depends the industriousness of seeking its nourishment through a kind of **hunt**.

It is necessary, then, for the human soul <term>human soul: *anima humana*; in this context, the animating force that connects the physical body to the divine intellect</term> to possess the light, the wit, and the instruments suited to its own hunt. Here, contemplation provides aid; here, logic comes into use as the most fit instrument <term>instrument: or "organon," referring to the traditional view of Logic as a tool for discovery rather than a subject in itself</term> for the **hunt for truth**, used to distinguish, to find, and to judge. Thus, one goes about surveying the **forest of natural things** <note>The "forest" is a common philosophical metaphor for the material world (often called *silva* in Latin), which is dense, dark, and difficult to navigate without the "light" of reason.</note> where so many objects lie under shadow and veil, and where truth is accustomed to keep its caverns and rocky retreats, as if in a thick, dense, and deserted solitude. These places are woven with thorns and enclosed by woody, rough, and leafy plants; it is there that truth hides itself, veils itself, and buries itself with the greatest diligence alongside the most worthy and excellent principles. Just as we are accustomed to conceal the greatest treasures with the utmost diligence and care, so that from the multitude and variety—

<meta>catchword: variety</meta>

<summary>The speaker explains that just as animals have an instinct to find food, the human soul has an innate drive to "hunt" for truth using logic and contemplation. This truth is described as a hidden treasure concealed within the dense "forest" of the natural, physical world.</summary>
<keywords>hunt for truth, logic, contemplation, forest of nature, human soul, intellect, natural philosophy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the Second Dialogue. Following the discussion on the mind's natural appetite for truth, the speaker now lists historical philosophers and their distinct methodologies for "hunting" the divine truth within the material world, picking up from the description of the rocky retreats where truth hides.</meta>

### Second Dialogue

...variety of hunters (of whom some are more exquisite and practiced, others less so) is not discovered without great effort. Here Pythagoras went searching for it through its tracks and vestiges <term>vestiges: *vestigij*; signs or footprints left by a divine presence in the material world</term> imprinted on natural things, which are the **numbers** that show its progress, reasons, modes, and operations in a certain way; because in the number of multitude, the number of measures, and the number of moment or weight <note>Pythagoras believed the universe was structured according to mathematical ratios; "weight" here refers to the gravity or importance of physical bodies.</note>, the truth and the being are found in all things. Here went Anaxagoras and Empedocles who, considering that the omnipotent and all-parent divinity fills the whole, found nothing so minimal that they did not wish truth to be hidden beneath it according to all reasons—though they always proceeded toward where it was predominant and expressed according to a more magnificent and high reason. Here the **Chaldeans** <note>The "Chaldeans" often refers to ancient Babylonian astronomers or the practitioners of "Chaldean Oracles," associated here with a mystical, negative theology.</note> sought it by way of subtraction, not knowing what to affirm of it; and they proceeded without the hounds of demonstrations and syllogisms; but they only forced themselves to delve deep by removing, hoeing, and clearing the woods <note>Continuing the metaphor of the material world as a dense forest.</note> through the power of **negation** of all species and predicates, both comprehensible and secret. Here Plato went about, as if turning the soil, digging, and planting fences, so that the fleeting and fugitive species might remain as if in a net, and be held back by the hedges of **definitions**, considering that superior things exist through participation and according to a mirroring likeness in inferior things, and these [inferior things] exist in those [superior things] according to a greater dignity.

<vocab>Pythagoras, Anaxagoras, Empedocles, Plato, Chaldeans, truth, divinity, numbers, definitions</vocab>

<summary>This page explores the diverse methodologies used by ancient philosophers—such as Pythagoras's numerology and Plato's definitions—to "hunt" for divine truth within the physical forest of nature.</summary>

### Second Dialogue

...dignity and excellence; and that truth exists between the one and the other according to a certain analogy, order, and scale, in which the lowest of the higher order always coincides with the highest of the lower order. <note>This refers to the "Great Chain of Being," a traditional philosophical hierarchy where every level of reality is linked to the one above and below it.</note> And thus progress was made from the lowest of nature to the highest—as if from evil to good, from darkness to light, or from <term>pure potentiality to pure act: *pura potenza al puro atto*; a concept from Aristotle where "potentiality" is the capacity to become something, and "act" is its full realization</term>—by way of the intermediaries.

Here **Aristotle** also boasts that he can reach the desired prey by following the tracks and <term>vestiges: *vestigij*; footprints or physical signs of the divine</term> imprinted on the world, as he wishes to be led from effects to their causes. Although he, for the most part (more so than all the others who have dedicated their study to this "hunt"), has lost the way, for he is hardly able to distinguish the footprints themselves. <note>Bruno often critiqued Aristotle for being too grounded in the material and failing to reach true spiritual or metaphysical heights.</note>

Here certain **theologians**, raised in various sects, seek the truth of nature in all specific natural forms. In these forms, they consider the eternal essence and the specific, substantial perpetuator of the eternal generation and change of things. These essences are called the "building and fabricating gods" <note>original: *dei conditori et fabricatori*; likely referring to the Platonic Demiurge or lesser creative spirits that shape the physical world.</note>, over whom presides the Form of Forms, the fountain of light, truth of truths, and God of gods, through whom all is full of divinity, truth, being, and goodness.

This truth is sought as something inaccessible, as an object that cannot be turned into a mere object, and as something not only incomprehensible but unattainable. Therefore, it seems possible to no one to see the sun—the universal **Apollo** and absolute light—through its supreme and most excellent essence. Instead, one sees his shadow, his **Diana**, which is the world, the universe, the nature that resides in things, and the light that exists within the opacity of...

<vocab>analogia: analogy/proportion, Aristotele: Aristotle, theologi: theologians, natura: nature, Apolline: Apollo (the sun/absolute truth), Diana: Diana (the moon/nature/reflected truth), veritá: truth, entitá: entity/being, bontá: goodness</vocab>

<summary>This page critiques Aristotle’s empirical method of seeking truth through physical effects and introduces the Platonic idea that absolute truth (Apollo) cannot be viewed directly, but only through the reflection of nature (Diana).</summary>

<meta>This page continues the Second Dialogue of *The Heroic Frenzies*. The previous page discussed various philosophical approaches to finding truth, concluding with theologians who seek the "building and fabricating gods" or the essential forms of nature.</meta>

**matter**: that is, matter insofar as it shines in the darkness. Of the many, therefore, who travel by these ways and many others through this **desert forest**, very few are those who happen upon the **fountain of Diana**. Many remain content with hunting wild and less noble beasts, and the majority find nothing to grasp, having cast their nets to the wind, and finding their hands full of flies. <note>Bruno uses "flies" as a metaphor for trivialities or useless thoughts that provide no real spiritual nourishment.</note> 

Very rare, I say, are those **Actaeons** to whom destiny has granted the power to contemplate **Diana** naked; and to become such that, so enamored by the beautiful disposition of the body of nature, and guided by those two lights of the twin splendor of divine goodness and beauty, they are transformed into **stags**, so that they are no longer hunters but the hunted. <note>Bruno reinterprets the myth of Actaeon: the hunter is not punished for seeing the goddess, but is transformed by the experience. By seeing the divine (Diana), the seeker's own lower nature is "hunted" and consumed by the higher truth he has discovered.</note> 

Because the ultimate and final goal of this <term>hunt: *venatione*; Bruno’s central metaphor for the intellectual and spiritual search for truth</term> is to acquire that fleeting and wild prey, through which the predator becomes the prey, and the hunter becomes the hunted. For in all other kinds of hunting for particular things, the hunter captures other things for himself, absorbing them with the "mouth" of his own <term>intellect: *intelligenza*; the faculty of understanding</term>; but in this divine and universal hunt, he comes to grasp things in such a way that he himself remains necessarily included, absorbed, and united. 

Whereby from being common, ordinary, civil, and popular, he becomes wild like a **stag**, and an inhabitant of the desert; he lives divinely beneath the loftiness of the forest, he lives in the natural dwellings of cavernous mountains, where he admires the headwaters of the great riv—

<meta>catchword: —ers, where [original: ni, doue]</meta>

<summary>Bruno uses the myth of Actaeon to describe the ultimate spiritual experience: the philosopher (the hunter) seeks divine truth (Diana), but upon finding it, he is transformed and "consumed" by the object of his search, becoming one with nature and divinity.</summary>

### Second Dialogue

->**Second Dialogue**<-

...where it flourishes untouched and pure from ordinary desires, where divinity converses more freely—that divinity to which so many men on earth have aspired, wishing to taste the celestial life, saying with one voice: *Lo, I have gone far off flying away, and I remained in the wilderness.* <note>original Latin: "ECCE ELONGAVI FVGIENS, ET MANSI IN SOLITVDINE" (Psalm 55:7). Bruno uses this to emphasize the need for spiritual solitude to reach higher truths.</note> Thus the "canine thoughts" of divine things devour this **Actaeon**, making him dead to the common people and the multitude, loosened from the knots of disturbed senses, free from the carnal prison of matter; so that he no longer sees his **Diana** as if through holes and windows. Instead, having thrown the walls to the ground, he is "all eye," facing the whole horizon. Consequently, he sees everything as one; he no longer sees through the distinctions and numbers that the diversity of our senses produces—which, like different <term>rhymes: *rime*; here used metaphorically to mean the varying "tones" or "categories" of sensory perception</term>, cause us to see and learn in a state of confusion.

He sees **Amphitrite**, the source of all numbers, of all species, and of all reasons, which is the <term>Monad: *Monade*; the fundamental unit of reality or the "One" in Pythagorean and Neoplatonic philosophy</term>, the true essence of the being of all things. And if he does not see her in her own essence, in absolute light, he sees her in her offspring which is similar to her, which is her image. For from the **Monad** which is divinity, proceeds this **Monad** which is nature, the universe, and the world; where divinity is contemplated and mirrored like the sun in the moon, through which it illuminates him, while he finds himself in the hemisphere of intellectual substances. This is **Diana**, that "one" which is the same as "being," that "being" which is the same as the "truth," that "truth" which is the <meta>catchword: nature-</meta>

<summary>Bruno explains how the spiritual seeker, like the hunter Actaeon, must retreat from worldly desires to achieve a direct vision of divine unity. He describes the Monad as the ultimate source of reality, which can be contemplated through the mirror of nature (Diana).</summary>
<keywords>Actaeon, Diana, Monad, nature, divinity, mysticism, Amphitrite, intellectual substances, spiritual solitude</keywords>

...intelligible nature, into which the sun and the splendor of the superior nature flow, according as unity is distinguished into the generated and the generator, or the producer and the produced. <note>Bruno is distinguishing between *Natura naturans* (God as the creative force) and *Natura naturata* (nature as the created result), suggesting that the human intellect can grasp the divine through its reflection in the natural world.</note> Thus, by your own reasoning, you may conclude the manner, the dignity, and the most worthy outcome of the hunter and the hunt: hence the **frenzied one** <note>the "heroic enthusiast" or seeker of divine wisdom</note> boasts of being the prey of **Diana**, to whom he surrendered himself. Through her, he considers himself a welcome consort, and a captive more happy and subjugated than any he could envy in another man (who cannot possess as much), or in another god (who possesses it in a manner impossible for a lower nature to attain—and consequently it is neither fitting to be desired, nor can it fall within one's appetite).

**CESARE.** I have well understood what you have said, and you have satisfied me more than moderately. Now it is time to return home. 

**MARICONDO.** Very well.

# Third Dialogue.

**Interlocutors.** { **LIBERIO.** / **LAODONIO.**

->LIBERIO.<-

<image-desc>Ornamental drop cap 'P' containing two mythical birds or dragons intertwined with vine-like foliage.</image-desc>

Resting under the shade of a cypress tree, the **frenzied one** <note>the "heroic enthusiast" or seeker of divine wisdom, whose "frenzy" is a state of spiritual elevation rather than madness</note>, finding his soul pausing from other thoughts—a marvelous thing—it happened that his heart and his eyes spoke to one another, as if they were distinct living beings and substances with their own independent reasoning and senses. They complained to each other, each accusing the other of being the source of that wearying torment which was consuming the soul. 

**LAODONIO.** Tell me if you remember their arguments and their words. 

**LIBERIO.** The heart began the dialogue; making itself heard from within the chest, it broke forth into these words:

<meta>catchword: PRI-</meta>

### FIRST PROPOSAL OF THE HEART TO THE EYES.

How, my eyes, so fiercely does it torment me,
That burning fire which from you descends;
So that it never permits my mortal frame <note>the physical body or earthly existence of the seeker</note>
To cease from guarding such a blaze, that I count as little
The waters of the Ocean and of the slower
Arctic star <note>original: "Artica stella"; a reference to the constellations of the Great or Little Bear, associated with extreme cold</note> the most frozen place,
Hoping that there, in an instant, the flash might be stifled,
Or at least some shadow of escape be promised me?
->You made me a captive<-
By a hand that holds me, and does not want me;
Through you I am within the body, and outside with the sun,
I am the principle of life and I am not alive,
->I do not know what I may be<-
For I belong to this soul, and it is not mine.

---

LAO[DONIO]. Truly, understanding, seeing, and knowing is what kindles desire, and consequently, through the ministry of the eyes, the heart becomes inflamed: and the more a higher and worthier object is present to them, the stronger the fire and the more vivid are the flames. Now, what must that species <note>the "intelligible species" or the mental image/representation of the divine object being contemplated</note> be, by which the heart feels so ignited, that

...who does not hope that any star could temper its heat, even one that is colder for being slower <note>referring to the outer planets or constellations that appear to move slowly and were traditionally associated with cold</note> and enclosed within the arctic circle; nor could the entire moisture of the Ocean dampen its field. How great must be the excellence of that object which has made the heart an enemy to its own being, a rebel against its own soul, and content with such rebellion and enmity, even though it is the captive of a hand that despises it and does not want it? But let me hear if the eyes replied, and what they said. LIBERIO. They, on the contrary, complained about the heart, seeing it as the principle and cause for which they shed so many tears. ### Third Dialogue

> Nature gave two lights <note>referring to the eyes</note>

**LAODONIO.** Certainly the heart, ignited and remorseful, causes tears to rise from the eyes; thus, just as the eyes kindle flames in the heart, the heart in turn waters the eyes with moisture. But I marvel at such strong **exaggeration** <note>original: "exaggeratione"; in this period, it refers to the rhetorical device of hyperbole or poetic overstatement</note> by which they say that the **Nereids** <note>sea nymphs in Greek mythology</note> do not raise their wet brows so high to the rising sun that they could equal these waters. And furthermore, they compare themselves to the **Ocean**, not because these two fountains <note>the eyes</note> are currently pouring out, but because they *could* pour out such and so many rivers that, compared to them, the **Nile** would appear as a small stream divided into seven channels.

**LIBERIO.** Do not marvel at the strong exaggeration and at that <term>power deprived of action</term> <note>original: "potenza priva de l'atto"; a reference to the Aristotelian concept of "potency" versus "actuality"—having the potential to do something without currently doing it</note>; for you will understand everything once you have heard the conclusion of their reasoning. Now hear how the heart first responds to the proposal of the eyes.

**LAODONIO.** Please, let me understand it.

**LIBERIO.**
