## FIRST RESPONSE OF THE HEART TO THE EYES.

Eyes, if an immortal flame is lit within me,
And I am nothing other than burning fire,
If that which approaches me is turned to smoke,
And I see the heavens glowing from my blaze:
How is it that my great heat does not consume you,
But instead the opposite effect is felt in you?
How is it that I drench you, and do not rather cook you,
If my substance is not moisture, but fire?
->Do you, blind ones, believe<-
->That from such a burning fire derives<-
->The double passage, and those two living fountains,<- <note>referring to the tear ducts and the eyes themselves</note>
->Have their origins in **Vulcan**;<- <note>original: "Vulcan"; the Roman god of fire and metalworking, here symbolizing the heat of passion</note>
->How sometimes a thing<-
->Acquires strength from its opposite, if the other resists?<-

---

<image-desc>An ornamental woodcut banner, repeating the design of the one above, with elaborate foliage scrollwork.</image-desc>

See how the heart could not convince itself that a force of a contrary effect <note>i.e., cold tears</note> could proceed from a contrary cause and principle <note>i.e., the burning heat of the heart</note>, until it chooses to affirm that this is possible through the process of **antiparistasis** <term>antiparistasis: a philosophical concept from antiquity where a quality is intensified by being surrounded by its opposite, such as a body of water remaining warm in winter because the cold "compresses" its internal heat</term>, which signifies the vigor that an opposite acquires from that which, by fleeing the other, comes to unite and thicken...

<meta>catchword: itself, in</meta>

<summary>In this section of the dialogue, the Heart responds to the Eyes by questioning how its own fiery passion can produce the "contrary effect" of liquid tears. It concludes that this paradox is explained by the scientific-philosophical concept of antiparistasis, where extreme heat can compress and intensify its opposite.</summary>
<keywords>antiparistasis, Vulcan, fire and water, paradox of tears, Renaissance philosophy, personification of the heart and eyes</keywords>

## FIRST RESPONSE OF THE EYES TO THE HEART.

Alas, heart, your passion so confuses you,
That you have lost the path of every truth.
All that is seen in us, and all that is hidden,
Is the seed of the seas, from which the whole
Neptune <note>The Roman god of the sea, representing the element of water</note> could recover from no other source,
If by chance he were to lose his great empire:
How could a burning flame derive from us,
Who are the twin kinsmen of the sea?
->Are you so devoid of sense,<-
That you believe the flame passes through us,
And leaves such humid gates behind,
To make you feel that immense heat?
->Like light through glass,<-
Do you perhaps believe that it penetrates through us?

--- Page 236 ---
<meta>This page continues the Third Dialogue. Liberio is interpreting the sonnet from the previous page, where the Eyes defended themselves against the Heart. The Eyes claimed to be the source of all water, and Liberio now explains the philosophical and physical mechanics of how fire and water might interact within the lover's body.</meta>

### Third Dialogue.

Here I do not wish to philosophize regarding the **coincidence of opposites** <note>original: "coincidenza de contrarij"; a core concept in Bruno's philosophy where, at the level of the infinite, opposing forces like heat and cold meet and become one</note>, which I have studied in the book *On Cause, Principle, and Unity* <note>original: "libro de principio et vno"; one of Bruno's major metaphysical works published in 1544</note>; and I wish to assume what is commonly assumed: that opposites within the same **genus** <note>original: "geno"; a logical category or class of things</note> are most distant from one another. This is so that the meaning of this response may be more easily understood, wherein the eyes are called seeds or fountains, in whose **virtual power** <note>original: "virtual potenza"; the inherent capacity or potentiality of a thing to produce an effect, even if it is not currently doing so</note> lies the sea. Thus, if **Neptune** <note>The Roman god of the sea, representing the element of water</note> were to lose all his waters, he could call them back into **actuality** <note>original: "in atto"; the state of being fully realized, as opposed to mere potentiality</note> from their power, where they exist as if in an active and material principle.

Therefore, they [the heart's arguments] do not present an urgent necessity when they claim it is impossible for the flame to pass through the eyes' chamber and courtyard to the heart, leaving so much water behind. This is for two reasons: first, because such an impediment in actuality cannot exist unless those hostile barriers are themselves brought into action; second, because insofar as the waters are actually in the eyes, they can grant passage to heat just as they do to light. 

For experience demonstrates that without heating the mirror itself, a luminous ray can ignite some material placed opposite it by way of **reflection** <note>original: "refleſſione"</note>. Furthermore, through a glass, crystal, or other vessel filled with water, the ray passes to ignite something placed beneath it without heating the dense intervening body—just as it is plausible, and indeed true, that it causes dry and parched impressions in the hollows of the **deep sea**. Thus, by a certain similarity, if not by reasons of the same...

<meta>catchword: genus (original: "geno")</meta>

<summary>Liberio explains how the Eyes can represent the element of Water while allowing the Heart's Fire to pass through them, using the analogy of light passing through water or glass without heating the medium itself.</summary>

### Third Dialogue.

...**genus** <note>This completes the sentence from the previous page: "opposites within the same genus."</note>, it can be considered how it is possible that, through the fluid and dark sense of the eyes, the **passion** <note>original: "affetto"; the emotional state or disposition of the soul</note> can be heated and ignited by that light, which by the same logic cannot exist in the **medium** <note>The space or substance between the object of beauty and the person perceiving it</note>. Just as the light of the sun exists in one mode within the intervening air, in another within the external sense, in another within the **common sense** <note>original: "senso commune"; in Renaissance psychology, the internal faculty that integrates data from the five senses</note>, and in another within the **intellect** <note>original: "intelletto"; the highest faculty of the soul</note>: even though one mode of being proceeds from the other.

**LAIO.** Are there further discourses?

**LIBERIO.** Yes, because both the one and the other <note>The Heart and the Eyes</note> seek to understand how the former contains such flames, and the latter such waters. Thus, the heart offers its second proposal.

# Third Dialogue.

-> So that I or you might make clear, <-
-> For its salvation, the soul's cruel fate; <-
-> If both <note>The fire of the heart and the tears of the eyes</note> remain hidden, <-
-> Can the beautiful, merciful deity <note>The beloved, often described in quasi-divine terms in Renaissance Petrarchan poetry</note> ever be moved to pity? <-

<image-desc>A decorative horizontal woodcut divider featuring symmetrical scrolling foliate and arabesque patterns.</image-desc>

L A O <note>Laocoonte</note>. If it is not true, it is very well invented <note>original: "Se non é vero, é molto ben trovato"; a famous Italian idiom suggesting that even if an explanation is a fiction, it is clever, plausible, and serves its purpose well</note>: if it is not so, then the one is very well excused by the other. For where there are two forces of which one is no greater than the other, the action of both must necessarily cease: since the one can resist as much as the other can persist; the latter resists no less than the former can attack.

If, therefore, the sea is infinite and the power of the tears within the eyes is immense, they will never allow the force of the fire hidden in the breast to appear by sparking or flaming out; nor can those eyes send a weeping torrent to the sea if the heart blocks it with equal vigor. Thus it happens that the beautiful deity—because of the lack of any appearance of a tear dripping from the eyes or a spark flying from the breast—cannot be moved to show mercy to the afflicted soul. Now, note the following response from the eyes.

### SECOND RESPONSE OF THE EYES TO THE HEART.

Alas, to pour out into the wavy element <note>The sea</note>,
The rush of us fountains <note>The eyes</note> is entirely in vain;
For a contrary power keeps it hidden,
So that it does not send tumbling down below,
The infinite vigor of the fiery heart
Denies passage to the rivers that are already too high,
Thus no twin path <note>The streams of tears from both eyes</note> runs to the sea,
For nature abhors the covered ground.
-> Now tell us, afflicted heart, <-
-> You who can oppose us with just as much <-
-> Vigor: who shall ever carry the boast, <-

-> If your pain and ours, <-

...these <note>original: "ſto" - likely referring to the infinite "ills" mentioned on the previous page</note> would absorb the other, and it would happen that both would be shown, or at least one in place of the other. Beneath these statements, I leave the natural and ethical philosophy hidden therein to be sought, considered, and understood by whoever is willing and able. 

Only this I do not wish to omit: that it is not without reason that the affection of the heart is called an "infinite sea" by the perception of the eyes. This is because the object of the mind <note>The ultimate Truth or God</note> is infinite, and since no defined object is proposed to the intellect, the will cannot be satisfied by any finite good. Rather, if it finds another good beyond what it already possesses, it craves it and seeks it out. This is because (as the common proverb says) the highest point of a lower species is the lowest point and the beginning of a higher species <note>This refers to the "Great Chain of Being," a traditional idea that the most perfect example of one level of existence (like an intelligent animal) touches the lowest level of the one above it (like a human).</note>. 

Whether one considers the levels of existence according to their forms (which we cannot judge to be infinite) or according to the ways and reasons of those forms—in which way, because the Supreme Good is infinite, we believe it communicates itself infinitely according to the condition of the things to which it spreads—consequently, there is no defined limit to the universe (I speak here of its physical size and mass), there is no defined limit to the intellect, and there is no defined limit to the scope of our affection <note>Desire or love</note>.

**ELPINO:** Then these two powers of the soul <note>The Intellect and the Will</note> are never perfect, nor can they ever be made perfect by their object, if they relate to it infinitely?

**FILOTEO:** That would be the case if this infinity were due to a "negative privation" <note>A defect or a lack of something that should be there</note> or a "privative negation" of an end. However, it exists as a more positive affirmation of an infinite and boundless end <note>Filoteo argues that the soul's lack of a "stopping point" is not a failure, but a sign of its divine capacity to reach for an infinite God.</note>.

**ELPINO:** You wish—

<summary>Filoteo explains that because the "Supreme Good" is infinite, human intellect and desire (the will) can never be satisfied by finite things, making the heart an "infinite sea" of longing. He argues that this lack of a limit is a positive, divine attribute of the soul rather than a defect.</summary>

# Third Dialogue.

<margin>

...you may then say there are two species of infinity. One is **privative** <note>In Renaissance philosophy, this refers to a "negative" infinity, like an endless void or darkness that only exists because light is absent</note>, which can be directed toward something that exists as **potential** <term>potenza: the latent capacity for something to happen or exist</term>, just as darkness is infinite until light is introduced. The other is **perfective**, which concerns the **act** <term>atto: the full realization or "doing" of a thing</term> and perfection; in this way, light is infinite, and its "end" or limit would only be found in privation and darkness. 

In this state, then, as the intellect conceives of light, the good, and the beautiful—as far as the "horizon" of its capacity extends—and as the soul drinks from the <note>original: "nettare diuino"</note> **divine nectar** and the **source of eternal life**, as much as its own vessel can hold; one sees that the light exists far beyond the circumference of the soul's horizon. There, the soul can always go deeper and deeper, penetrating further. This nectar and source of living water is infinitely fertile, so that the soul may always become more and more intoxicated <note>This refers to "divine frenzy" or ecstasy, a state of being overwhelmed by spiritual joy</note>. 

From this, it does not follow that there is any imperfection in the object, nor any lack of satisfaction in the soul's power. Rather, it means the soul's power is encompassed by the object and **blissfully absorbed** by it. Here, the eyes imprint upon the heart—that is, upon the understanding—and stir in the will an infinite torment of sweet love. In this torment there is no pain, because one does not lack what one desires; rather, it is **happiness**, because what is sought is always found there. 

There is no **satiety** <term>satietá: a state of being so full that one becomes bored or disgusted</term> here, because there is always an appetite. Consequently, this "tasting" is not like the consumption of physical food. With the body's food, one loses the taste through satiety; there is no happiness before one tastes, nor after one has tasted, but only in the moment of tasting itself. In that physical act, if a certain limit and end is passed, it leads to annoyance and nausea.

<summary>The author distinguishes between two types of infinity—one of lack (darkness) and one of fullness (light)—to explain why the human soul's desire for the divine is a source of eternal happiness rather than painful longing. Unlike physical hunger, which ends in boredom or disgust, spiritual desire is a "sweet torment" that is perpetually satisfied and renewed.</summary>

<keywords>infinity, intellect, soul, divine nectar, potential and act, satiety, spiritual desire, light and darkness</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical exploration from the Third Dialogue. The previous page established a distinction between two types of infinity: "privative" (defined by lack) and "perfective" (defined by fullness). # Third Dialogue.

See then, by a certain likeness, how the **highest good** <term>sommobene: the Summum Bonum, or the ultimate source of all goodness and joy</term> must be infinite, and the impulse of the **affection** <term>affetto: in this context, the soul's emotional drive or passionate desire</term> toward and around it must also be infinite, so that it does not sometimes cease to be good: just as food, which is good for the body, becomes poison if it lacks moderation. Observe how the moisture of the **Ocean** does not extinguish that flame, and the rigor of the **Arctic circle** does not temper that heat. <note>The author uses elemental extremes—the vast ocean and the frozen north—to illustrate that this spiritual passion is so intense it cannot be neutralized by physical forces.</note> Thus, it is like a captive of a hand that holds it and does not want it; it holds it because it considers it its own, yet does not want it (as if fleeing from it); it becomes all the loftier the more the soul ascends toward it. The more one follows it, the further away it appears by reason of its most eminent excellence, according to that saying: **Man shall approach a deep heart, and God shall be exalted.** <note>original: "ACCEDET HOMO AD COR ALTVM, ET EXALTABITVR DEVS" — a quote from Psalm 64:6-7, often interpreted by Renaissance philosophers to mean that as man searches his own depths, he finds the infinite height of God.</note>

Such happiness of affection begins in this life, and in this state it has its mode of being: wherefore the heart can say it is within the body, and outside with the sun, insofar as the soul, with its twin faculty, carries out two duties: one to give life to and actuate the animate body, the other to contemplate higher things. Because just as she [the soul] is in a **receptive power** <term>potenza receptiua: the capacity to receive influence or light from a higher source</term> from above, so is she toward the body below in an **active power** <term>potenza attiua: the capacity to exert influence or give life to something else</term>. The body is as if dead and a **privative** <note>original: "priuatiua" — referring to something that is defined by what it lacks (like darkness lacks light)</note> thing to the soul, which is its life and perfection; and the soul is as if dead and a privative thing to the superior illuminating intelligence, from which the intellect is ra—

<meta>Catchword: is ra-</meta>

<summary>The author explains that the soul's desire for the divine must be infinite to remain "good," describing the soul as a bridge that simultaneously gives life to the body and contemplates the higher divine light.</summary>
<keywords>highest good, infinite desire, soul-body relationship, contemplation, active and passive power, divine intelligence, Psalm 64</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the Third Dialogue, transitioning from the infinite nature of the soul’s desire to the specific relationship between the heart (the seat of emotion) and the eyes (the seat of perception). # Third Dialogue.

...is established in **habit** <term>habito: a permanent state or disposition of the soul, rather than a fleeting feeling</term>, and formed in act. Therefore, it is said that the **heart** <term>core: the center of passion and life-force</term> is the prince of life, and yet is not "alive." It is said to belong to the **animating soul** <term>alma animante: the part of the soul that gives life to the physical body</term>, and yet the soul does not belong to it: because it is inflamed by **divine love** <term>amor diuino: the yearning for God or the ultimate Truth</term>, and is finally converted into fire, which can ignite whatever comes near it. This occurs because, having drawn **divinity** into itself, it is made divine; consequently, by its own nature, it can cause others to fall in love: just as the splendor of the sun can be admired and magnified in the moon. <note>The author uses the moon as a metaphor for the human soul; it has no light of its own, but when it faces the "Sun" (God), it becomes a luminary that can guide others.</note>

Regarding what pertains to the consideration of the **eyes**, you know that in the present discourse they have two offices: one is to impress upon the heart, the other is to receive the impression from the heart. Likewise, the heart has two offices: one to receive the impressions from the eyes, the other to impress upon them. The eyes grasp the **forms** <term>specie: the mental images or "species" of things perceived by the senses</term> and propose them to the heart; the heart yearns for them and presents its yearning to the eyes. The eyes conceive the light, diffuse it, and kindle the fire in the heart; the heart, heated and inflamed, sends its moisture <note>original: "humore" — likely referring to tears or the spiritual "vapors" of passion</note> back to the eyes, so that they may digest it.

In this way, **cognition** <term>cognitione: the act of knowing or understanding</term> first moves the **affection** <term>affetto: the emotional drive or willpower</term>, and afterwards, the affection moves the cognition. The eyes, when they are moving (active), are dry, because they perform the office of a mirror and a presenter; but when they are moved (passive), they are troubled and altered, because they perform the office of a diligent executor. This is because with the **speculative intellect** <term>intelletto speculatiuo: the faculty of the mind that contemplates truth for its own sake</term>, one first sees the beautiful and the good, and then the

<sig>O.</sig>
<meta>catchword: will (uoluntà)</meta>

<summary>This page explores the reciprocal relationship between the eyes (intellect/perception) and the heart (will/affection), explaining how divine love transforms the heart into a divine fire that reflects God's light to others.</summary>

<meta>This page concludes the Third Dialogue. It moves from the physiological and spiritual relationship between the heart and eyes to a philosophical discussion on the nature of desire and divine fulfillment. It resolves the paradox of how the soul can both possess its object and continue to yearn for it.</meta>

# Third Dialogue.

...the **will** <term>uoluntà: the faculty of the soul that chooses and desires an object</term> appetites it, and then the industrious **intellect** <term>intelletto: the power of the mind to understand and seek truth</term> procures, follows, and seeks it. The tearful eyes signify the difficulty of the separation of the desired thing from the desirer; which, so that it does not cause surfeit or boredom, offers itself as if through infinite study, which always possesses and always seeks. This is because the happiness of the gods is described as the act of *drinking*, not as having *already drunk* the **nectar**; as *tasting*, not as having *already tasted* the **ambrosia**. It involves having a continuous affection for the food and drink, and not being sated and without desire for them. Hence, they possess **satiety** <term>satietà: the state of being full or satisfied</term> as if in motion and apprehension, not as in stillness and comprehension; they are not full without appetite, nor are they desiring without being, in a certain manner, full.

LAO. A sated hunger, a hungering satiety. <note>original Latin: "Esuries satiata, satietas esuriens." This phrase captures the Neoplatonic idea that in the divine realm, desire and satisfaction coexist perfectly, preventing the stagnation of boredom.</note>

LI. Exactly so.

LA. From this I can understand how—without blame, but with great truth and insight—it has been said that **divine love** weeps with inexpressible groans, because in possessing all, it loves all; and in loving all, it possesses all.

LIB. But many glosses would be needed if we wished to understand the divine love that is the **deity** <term>deità: the supreme essence of the divine</term> itself. It is more easily understood as divine love insofar as it is found in its effects and in subordinate nature; I do not mean (I say) that love which spreads from the **divinity** <term>diuinità: the divine presence as it flows into the world</term> toward things, but rather the love within things that aspires toward the divinity.

LA. Now, of this and other matters we shall converse at greater leisure later. Let us go.

---

->End of the Third Dialogue.<-

<summary>This page concludes the Third Dialogue by explaining the paradox of divine desire: a state of perpetual "hungering satiety" where the soul is fulfilled yet continues to seek. It distinguishes between the love God has for creation and the love through which creation aspires to return to the divine.</summary>
<keywords>will, intellect, nectar, ambrosia, satiety, divine love, deity, divinity, Lansino, Libanio</keywords>

<meta>This page begins the Fourth Dialogue. The previous page concluded a discussion on the paradoxical nature of divine love as a state of "sated hunger," where the soul is simultaneously satisfied and desiring. We now transition from these abstract principles to a series of allegorical figures: the nine blind men, who represent different stages or causes of the spiritual journey and the "heroic frenzy."</meta>

<image-desc>Ornate woodcut headpiece at the top of the page featuring symmetrical scrollwork and floral motifs.</image-desc>

# Fourth Dialogue.

->*Interlocutors.* { SEVERINO.<-
->{ MINUTOLO.<-

## SEVERINO.

<image-desc>Decorative drop cap 'V' depicting two cherub-like figures within a floral and architectural frame.</image-desc>
You will see, then, the reasoning of nine blind men, who carry nine principles and specific causes for their blindness, though they all share one general cause: a common **frenzy** <term>furore: in this context, a "heroic frenzy" or divine madness that elevates the soul beyond ordinary human limits</term>.

MINUTOLO. Begin with the first.

SEVERINO. The first of these, although blind by nature, nonetheless laments because of love, telling the others that he cannot believe nature was more unkind to them than to him. For while they no longer see, they have at least known what it is to see; they are experienced in the dignity of the sense of sight and the excellence of the **sensible** <term>sensibile: objects or qualities that can be perceived by the senses</term>, which is why they have now become sightless. But he came into the world like a mole—to be seen, but not to see; to yearn for that which he never beheld.

<sig>O.2.</sig>
<meta>catchword: MIN.</meta>

<summary>This page introduces the Fourth Dialogue, presenting an allegory of nine blind men who suffer from different forms of "heroic frenzy." The first man, blind from birth, argues that his condition is worse than those who lost their sight, as he yearns for a beauty he has never even glimpsed.</summary>
<keywords>nine blind men, heroic frenzy, allegory of sight, nature vs. experience, yearning, Severino, Minutolo</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the Fourth Dialogue of the "Heroic Frenzies." Following the introduction of the nine blind men, Severino and Minutolo begin discussing the first of these figures, who represents those born without the "light" of intellect or divine vision, contrasting his state with those who once possessed sight but lost it.</meta>

# Fourth Dialogue.

MINUTOLO. Many have been found who are in love by reputation alone.

SEVERINO. They (he says) have at least this happiness: they retain that divine image in the sight of the mind <note>original: "conspectto de la mente"</note>, so that, however blind they may be, they still hold in their imagination that which he cannot have. Then in the sestina <term>sestina: a complex poetic form composed of six-line stanzas, though here used for a longer verse</term> he turns to his guide, praying to be led to some precipice, so that he may no longer be a horrid spectacle of nature's disdain. He says, then:

<image-desc>Decorative horizontal woodcut ornament featuring symmetrical scrollwork and floral motifs.</image-desc>

---

### THE FIRST BLIND MAN SPEAKS.

> Happy are you who at times have seen,
> You who now grieve for the lost light,
> Companions who have known the two lights <note>A poetic reference to the eyes</note>.
> These [of mine] were never lit, nor are they extinguished;
> Therefore, my ill is more grievous than you believe,
> And worthy of greater lamentations:
> For there is no one who can assure me
> That nature was more unkind to you than to me.
> ->To the precipice, O leader!<-
> Lead me, if you wish to give me contentment,
> So that my torment may find a remedy,
> For to be seen, and yet not see the light,
> Like a mole I came out into the world,
> And to be an useless weight <term>pondo: weight or burden</term> upon the earth,

<image-desc>Decorative horizontal woodcut ornament, identical in style to the previous one, with floral and scrollwork designs.</image-desc>

<summary>In this opening to the poetic section of the Fourth Dialogue, the first blind man laments his condition of being blind from birth, arguing that his suffering is greater than those who once saw because he lacks even the memory of light. He compares himself to a mole, a creature of the earth that is seen by others but remains in darkness.</summary>

## Fourth Dialogue.

Next follows the other man who, bitten by the serpent of jealousy, has become infected in his visual organ <note>The eyes, which Bruno views as the gateway for both love and the intellect</note>. He wanders without a guide, unless he has jealousy itself as his escort. He begs those standing around him that, if there is no remedy for his ill, they should at least, out of pity, ensure he can no longer have any sense of his suffering; thus making him hidden to himself, just as his light has been made hidden from him, by burying him along with his own misfortune. He says, then:

---

### THE SECOND BLIND MAN SPEAKS.

> From her dread hair Alecto has loosed <note>Alecto is one of the three Furies of Greek mythology; she represents unremitting anger and is often depicted with snakes for hair</note>
> The infernal worm, which with its fierce bite
> Has so fiercely infected my spirit,
> That it has rushed to snatch away my principal sense <note>original: "senso principal"; referring to sight, which Renaissance philosophy held as the most noble sense for perceiving truth</note>,
> Depriving the intellect of its guide:
> So that in vain the soul asks others for help;
> It makes me stumble so on every path,
> That rabid resentment of jealousy.
> 
> If no magic spell,
> Nor sacred plant, nor power of stone <note>A reference to the lapidary tradition where certain gemstones were believed to have medicinal or protective properties</note>,
> Nor divine help procures my escape:
> Let one of you (by God) be so merciful in the meantime,
> As to make me hidden to myself:
> By having my ill buried soon along with me.

---

<term>Dialogue: A philosophical discussion in the style of Plato</term>, <term>Jealousy: Here, a spiritual or intellectual torment that blinds the soul's vision</term>, <term>Alecto: The "unceasing" Fury who punishes crimes of anger</term>, <term>Blind man: An allegorical figure representing a specific state of the soul in search of the divine</term>, <term>Intellect: The faculty of the soul that seeks to understand universal truths</term>, <term>Resentment: A bitter lingering of passion that disrupts reason</term>, <term>Enchantment: The use of supernatural means to cure or cause blindness</term>

<summary>This page introduces the second of the nine blind men, whose blindness is an allegory for the "rabid resentment" of jealousy that infects the spirit and misleads the intellect. He describes his torment as a bite from Alecto's serpent and begs for the mercy of total self-oblivion since no magical or divine remedy can cure him.</summary>
<keywords>Jealousy, Alecto, blindness, intellect, spiritual torment, Furies, Renaissance philosophy, Giordano Bruno</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the Fourth Dialogue of "The Heroic Frenzies." Having previously discussed the first blind man (blind by nature/ignorance) and the second (blinded by the "serpent of jealousy"), the text now introduces the third blind man. His blindness is of a higher order, caused not by a lack of light, but by a sudden, overwhelming encounter with divine beauty.</meta>

# Fourth Dialogue.

Next follows the other man, who says he has become blind because he was suddenly moved from darkness to see a great light; since, being accustomed to looking at ordinary beauties, a celestial beauty—a divine sun—suddenly appeared before his eyes. Thus, his sight was dissolved and the twin light that shines at the prow of the soul (because the eyes are like two lanterns that guide the ship) was extinguished, just as usually happens to one raised in Cimmerian <note>The Cimmerians were an ancient mythical people described by Homer as living in a land of perpetual mist and darkness at the edge of the world.</note> gloom if he suddenly fixes his eyes on the sun. And in the six-line stanza <note>original: "sestina"; though the following poem begins with an octave, Bruno uses the term here to refer to the poetic address.</note> he prays that he be granted free passage to the underworld <note>original: "inferno"</note>, because nothing but darkness is suitable for a "dark subject" <note>An intellectual pun: he is a "subject" who has been made "dark" (blinded), and therefore belongs in a dark realm.</note>. He speaks, then, as follows.

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut horizontal divider featuring symmetrical floral scrollwork and stylized leaf patterns.</image-desc>

### THE THIRD BLIND MAN SPEAKS

-> If the great planet <note>The Sun</note> suddenly appears <-
-> To a man nurtured in profound darkness, <-
-> Or beneath the sky of the Cimmerian people, <-
-> From where the sun spreads its rays far away; <-
-> It extinguishes the shining twin light <-
-> At the soul's prow, and hides as an enemy: <-
-> Thus were my eyes dissolved, accustomed <-
-> To looking at ordinary beauties. <-
-> Make me go to the underworld <note>original: "orco"; referring to Orcus, the Roman god of the underworld or the abyss itself.</note>, <-
-> Why do I wander among the people as if dead? <-

<meta>This page continues the Fourth Dialogue of "The Heroic Frenzies." This page introduces the fourth blind man, who represents a different state of spiritual "blindness" caused by constant contemplation rather than a sudden shock.</meta>

# Fourth Dialogue.

<warning>faded text and ghosting from the reverse page are visible</warning>

-> Why do I, a stump of hell <note>original: "ceppo infernal"; a metaphor for someone who is spiritually rooted in another realm or feels dead to the world of the living.</note>, wander <-
-> Mixed among you living? Why do I breathe <-
-> The unwelcome air, <-
-> Cast into such pain for having seen the highest good? <-

---

<image-desc>A horizontal woodcut typographic ornament featuring symmetrical scrolling acanthus leaves, vines, and floral patterns.</image-desc>

The fourth blind man comes forward, sightless for a similar reason, though not exactly for the same cause as the first; for while that one was blinded by a sudden flash of light, this one has lost the sense of all other lights through frequent and constant gazing, or perhaps by fixing his eyes too intently upon it. He is not called "blind" in relation to that unique light which blinded him; rather, he says of the sense of sight what is known to happen to the sense of hearing. It is a famous fact that those whose ears have become accustomed to great noise and clamor cannot hear lesser sounds, as is the case with the Catadupi people <note>The "Cataduppici" refers to a legendary group mentioned by classical authors like Cicero and Pliny, who lived near the cataracts (waterfalls) of the Nile and were said to be permanently deafened by the roar.</note> who live where the great Nile river falls precipitously from a very high mountain into the plain.

**MIN.** In the same way, all those who have accustomed their bodies and minds to more difficult and great things are not usually bothered by lesser difficulties. And this man surely should not be unhappy with his blindness.

**SEVE.** Certainly not. But he is called a "voluntary blind man," <term>voluntary blind man (uolontario orbo)</term> <note>In Bruno’s philosophy, this is a state where the seeker intentionally ignores worldly distractions to focus solely on the divine.</note> for he is pleased that every other thing remains hidden from him.

<summary>This page introduces the fourth blind man, who is blinded not by a sudden shock, but by the "habit" of gazing at the divine light, rendering him unable to perceive lesser, worldly things.</summary>
<keywords>fourth blind man, Catadupi, Nile cataracts, voluntary blindness, highest good, sensory overload, Giordano Bruno</keywords>

# Fourth Dialogue.

...hidden, as it troubles him by distracting him from beholding that which he solely wishes to behold. And in the meantime, he begs travelers that they deign to keep him from meeting with some mishap, while he walks so focused and captivated by a single primary object. **MIN.** Recite his words. **SEV.**

<image-desc>A horizontal woodcut typographic ornament featuring symmetrical scrolling acanthus leaves, vines, and floral patterns.</image-desc>

---

### THE FOURTH BLIND MAN SPEAKS.

Plunging from the heights into the great depths,
The Nile has drowned out the sense of every other sound
For the joyless people of the Catadupi <note>original: "Cataduppi"; a people mentioned by ancient authors like Cicero, said to live near the cataracts of the Nile and to be permanently deafened by the roar of the water.</note>:
So I, keeping my whole spirit intent
Upon the most vivid light the world holds,
Never sense any of the lesser splendors;
Now, while that light shines on him, let all other things
Be hidden from the voluntary blind man.
->I pray you, from the jolts<-
Of any stone or unreasoning beast,
Make me aware; and whether the path falls or rises,
Lest these wretched bones should tumble
Into a hollow or low place,
While I take my steps without a guide.

<meta>This page continues the Fourth Dialogue of Giordano Bruno’s "The Heroic Frenzies." Following the fourth blind man's plea for guidance, we are introduced to the fifth blind man. While the previous figure was blinded by the intensity of light, this figure is blinded by his own tears—the "contrary moisture" to the fire of his passion.</meta>

# Fourth Dialogue.

For the blind man who follows, it happens that through much weeping his eyes are so dimmed that the visual ray <term>visual ray: In Renaissance optics, vision was often conceived as a ray or spirit emitted from the eye to meet the object being seen.</term> cannot extend itself to compare visible forms; and mainly to see again that light which, to his misfortune because of so many sorrows, he once beheld. Furthermore, it is estimated that his blindness is no longer merely a temporary state <note>original: "dispositionale"; a passing condition as opposed to a permanent habit.</note> but habitual, and entirely privative; because the luminous fire that ignites the soul in the pupil has been suppressed and oppressed for too long and too forcefully by the contrary humor <note>The "humor" here refers to the cold/wet nature of tears, which in Renaissance medicine would extinguish the hot/dry "fire" of the visual spirit.</note>: so much so that even if the weeping were to cease, he is not convinced that he would thereby achieve the longed-for sight. And you shall hear what he says next to the company, asking them to let him pass.

---

<image-desc>A decorative horizontal woodcut divider featuring symmetrical scrolling foliage, acanthus leaves, and floral motifs.</image-desc>

### THE FIFTH BLIND MAN SPEAKS.

> My eyes, forever pregnant with water,
> When will it be that from the visual ray
> The spark might break out from behind so many
> And such dense barriers, and emerge in such a way
> That it might see again those holy lights,
> Which were the beginning of my sweet pain <note>original: "dolce male"; a classic Petrarchan oxymoron describing the bittersweet nature of divine or romantic longing.</note>?
> Alas, I believe it is entirely extinguished,
> Having been for so long oppressed and conquered by its opposite.

<meta>This page continues the Fourth Dialogue of Giordano Bruno’s "The Heroic Frenzies." The previous section concluded the speech of the fifth blind man, who was blinded by the constant "humor" or moisture of his tears. Now, we transition to the sixth blind man, whose condition is even more extreme: he has wept so much that he has entirely exhausted the moisture of his eyes, leaving him in a state of desiccated blindness.</meta>

# Fourth Dialogue.

->Let the blind man pass,<-
->And turn your eyes to these fountains<-
->Which surpass all others united and joined;<-
->And if there is anyone who dares to argue with me,<-
->There is one who makes it certain<-
->That just one of my eyes contains an ocean.<-

---
<image-desc>An ornate horizontal woodcut divider featuring symmetrical scrolling acanthus leaves and floral motifs.</image-desc>

The sixth blind man is sightless because, through excessive weeping, he has shed so many tears that no moisture remains—even the "ice" <note>original: "ghiacio"; this refers to the crystalline or vitreous humor of the eye, which was thought to be a clear, ice-like substance.</note> and the fluid through which the visual ray was transmitted, as through a transparent medium, are gone. It is through this moisture that external light and visible species <term>visible species: In Renaissance optics and Aristotelian philosophy, "species" were the forms or images emitted by objects that traveled through the air to enter the eye.</term> are admitted. The heart was so deeply pierced that all the humid substance (whose duty it is to keep the diverse, various, and contrary parts of the body held together) has been consumed. Thus, there remains in him the loving affection without the outward effect of tears, because the organ is now out of tune <note>original: "stemprato"; literally "untempered." In the theory of the four humors, health was a "temperament" or balance; the victory of one element over others "untempered" the body's instrument.</note> due to the victory of the other elements. Consequently, he remains without sight and without the coherence of his bodily parts. He then proposes to those standing around him that which you are about to hear.

---
<image-desc>An ornate horizontal woodcut divider featuring symmetrical scrolling acanthus leaves and floral motifs, identical to the one above.</image-desc>

### THE SIXTH BLIND MAN SPEAKS.

Eyes, no longer eyes; fountains, no longer fountains,
You have already poured out your entire store of moisture,

<meta>This page continues the Fourth Dialogue of Giordano Bruno’s "The Heroic Frenzies." On the previous page, we saw the transition to the sixth blind man. He is unique among the group because his blindness is not caused by the presence of too much moisture (tears), but by the total absence of it. # Fourth Dialogue.

Which held the body, the spirit, and the soul joined.
And you, visual ice <note>original: "visual ghiaccio"; this refers to the crystalline humor of the eye, which Renaissance thinkers believed was a clear, ice-like substance that transmitted light.</note> which from the outside
Made so many objects known to the soul,
Are consumed by the wounded heart:
Thus toward the infernal shadowy cave
I lead my steps, parched and blind.

->Ah, do not be stingy with me<-
In making me go forth quickly, pitying me,
Since so many rivers in my dark days
I have shed, satisfied only by my weeping;
->Now that every humor is gone,<-
Give me passage toward deep oblivion.

<image-desc>A horizontal ornamental woodcut divider with symmetrical scrolling foliage and floral motifs.</image-desc>

The next one arrives, who has lost his sight from the intense heat which, proceeding from the heart, went first to consume the eyes and then to lick up all the remaining humor <term>humor: In Renaissance medicine, humors were bodily fluids. Here, Bruno refers to the "radical moisture" that was thought to hold the physical body together.</term> of the lover's substance, in such a way that, entirely turned to ash and set on fire, he is no longer himself. This is because by fire—whose power is to dissolve all bodies into their atoms <note>Giordano Bruno was a famous proponent of atomism, the idea that the universe is made of tiny, indivisible particles.</note>—he is converted into a dust that cannot be held together, since it is only by the power of water that the atoms of other bodies are thickened and joined to form a subsisting composite. Despite all this, he is not deprived of the feeling of these most intense flames: therefore, in the sestina <term>sestina: A poetic form. Bruno often uses these poems as "emblems" to be explained by the surrounding prose.</term> he wishes to be given room to pass, so that if someone should come...

# Fourth Dialogue.

...were touched by its flames, he would become such that he would no longer have any sensation of the infernal flames as a hot thing, but rather as cold snow <note>original: "fredda neue"; Bruno is using a common poetic trope of hyperbole: his internal fire is so intense that the fires of Hell would feel like cold snow by comparison.</note>. He says, therefore:

---

### THE SEVENTH BLIND MAN SPEAKS.

THE beauty that through the eyes flowed to the heart
Formed in my breast the deep furnace,
Which first absorbed the visual humor <term>visual humor: The "humors" were bodily fluids. The visual humor (or crystalline humor) was believed to be the fluid in the eye that allowed sight.</term>,
Pouring high its tenacious blaze;
And then devouring every other liquid of mine,
To put the dry element <note>Refers to Earth, the dryest of the four classical elements.</note> at peace;
It has rendered me **un-bindable dust**,
Which dissolves everything into its atoms <note>Giordano Bruno was a famous proponent of atomism, the idea that the universe is made of tiny, indivisible particles.</note>.
If of infinite evil
You have horror, give me room, O people,
Guard yourselves from my scorching fire;
For if contagion of that fire assails you,
You will believe that it is winter
To find yourselves at the fire of hell.

---

The eighth follows, whose blindness is caused by the arrow that Love has made penetrate from the eyes to the heart. Wherefore he laments not only as one blind, but furthermore as one wounded, and burned so deeply, as—

## THE EIGHTH BLIND MAN SPEAKS.

Vile assault, wicked battle, unjust victory,
Sharp point; devouring bait, strong sinew:
Harsh wound, impious daring, cruel burden,
Arrow, fire, and snare of that arrogant god <note>The "arrogant god" is Cupid (Love), often depicted in Renaissance literature as a cruel tyrant who blinds and enslaves his followers.</note>,
Who pierced the eyes, burned the heart, bound the soul,
And made me at once blind, lover, and servant;
So that, deprived of sight by my wound, fire, and knot,
I feel them in every time, place, and manner,
->Men, heroes, and gods,<-
Who are on earth, or nearby—speak, O Jove <note>original: "Gioue"; Jupiter, the king of the gods, often used by Bruno to represent the highest governing principle of the universe.</note>,
Tell me (I pray) when, how, and where
You ever felt, heard, or saw such woes
->Identical, or similar, or so many<-
Among the oppressed, among the damned, or among lovers?

<meta>This page continues the Fourth Dialogue of Giordano Bruno's "The Heroic Frenzies." Having just concluded the lament of the Eighth Blind Man, the text now introduces the Ninth and final Blind Man. This figure is unique because he is silent, representing a state of love or intellectual pursuit so intense that it transcends speech. # Fourth Dialogue.

...he does not speak himself, but his guide provides the reasoning; because it is straightforward, I will not discuss it further, but merely provide the verse <note>original: "sentenza"; in this context, the core meaning or poetic judgment expressed in the following lines.</note>.

---

### THE GUIDE OF THE NINTH BLIND MAN SPEAKS.

**F**ortunate are you, the other blind lovers,
Who can explain the cause of your suffering,
And who may, by the merit of your tears,
Be received with chaste and grateful welcomes;
But for the one I lead, who among you all
Suffers most deeply, the flame is hidden,
Silent, perhaps for lack of boldness
To make his torment clear to his goddess.
->Open, open the way,<-
->Be kind to this vacant face,<-
->O thick crowd of sad impediments,<-
->While the troubled and weary body<-
->Goes knocking at the doors<-
->Of a less painful and deeper death.<-

---

Here are signified nine causes <note>original: "caggioni"; the underlying reasons or philosophical principles.</note> why it happens that the **human mind** is blind toward the **divine object**, and why it cannot fix its eyes upon it. Of these:

**T**HE first, allegorized by the first blind man, is the nature of his own species; for, as much as the level at which he finds himself allows, he nevertheless aspires toward something certainly higher <note>Bruno is describing the Neoplatonic idea that humans are naturally limited by their physical and intellectual "species" or status, yet they feel a drive to reach for the infinite Divine.</note>

<summary>This page introduces the Ninth Blind Man, who remains silent because his passion is too great for words, and begins an allegorical explanation of the nine reasons why the human mind is "blinded" by the divine light.</summary>
<keywords>Ninth Blind Man, divine object, human mind, silence in love, Neoplatonism, allegory of blindness, Giordano Bruno</keywords>

--- Page 259 ---
<meta>This page continues the Fourth Dialogue of Giordano Bruno's "The Heroic Frenzies." The speakers, Minutolo (MI) and Saulino (SE), are discussing the philosophical meaning behind the nine allegorical "blind men." They have just touched upon the first cause of spiritual blindness—the soul's natural limitation within a physical body—and are now transitioning to the second cause, which relates to a form of intellectual jealousy or "zeal."</meta>

...higher than he can grasp. 

**MI.** Because no natural desire is in vain <note>This refers to the Aristotelian principle that nature does not create an impulse without providing a means to satisfy it; therefore, the human desire for the infinite implies that the infinite is reachable.</note>, we can be certain of a more excellent state that belongs to the soul outside of this body, in which it will be possible for it to unite or approach more loftily its object. 

**SE.** You speak very well, for no natural power or impulse is without great reason; indeed, it is the very rule of nature itself which orders all things. Therefore, it is a most true and certain thing to well-disposed minds that the human soul (however it appears while in the body), by the very way it presents itself in this state, makes manifest its being a pilgrim in this region. This is because it aspires to universal truth and goodness, and is not content with that which serves only the purpose and profit of its own species <note>Bruno suggests that because humans seek "universal" truths rather than just biological survival, our souls must be "pilgrims" from a higher, divine realm.</note>.

**T**HE second cause, symbolized by the second blind man, proceeds from some disturbed affection, such as jealousy in the context of love. This jealousy is like a woodworm that has the same subject, enemy, and father; that is, it gnaws the cloth or wood from which it was generated <note>A metaphor for how jealousy is born from love, exists within the lover, and yet destroys the very love that created it.</note>. 

**MI.** This does not seem to me to have a place in **heroic love** <term>heroic love: "Amore eroico," a Neoplatonic concept describing a soul's passionate, intellectual ascent toward the divine, rather than mere physical attraction.</term>. 

**SE.** That is true if we speak of the same reasoning seen in vulgar love; but I mean it according to another reasoning, proportional to that which occurs in those who love truth and goodness. It shows itself when they become so angry against those who wish to adulterate, spoil, or corrupt it, or who in some other unworthy way wish...

<meta>catchword: to treat (tratta-)</meta>

<summary>Minutolo and Saulino discuss how the soul's dissatisfaction with earthly life proves its divine origin, and begin defining "heroic jealousy" as a protective zeal for truth.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the Fourth Dialogue of Giordano Bruno’s "The Heroic Frenzies." The speakers, Minutolo (MI) and Saulino (SE), are concluding their discussion on the second type of spiritual blindness—caused by a "jealous" zeal for the truth—and are beginning to describe the third type, which relates to the blinding light of supernatural metaphysics.</meta>

->### Dialogo Quarto.<-

...to treat it as some have been found to do, who have brought themselves even to death, to torments, and to being ignominiously treated by ignorant peoples and common sects.

**MINVTO-LO.** Certainly, no one truly loves the true and the good without being angry against the multitude; just as no one loves in a common way without being jealous and fearful for the thing beloved.

**SE.** And because of this, he truly becomes blind in many things, and—entirely and utterly according to common opinion—he is foolish and mad.

**MI.** I have noted a passage that says all those who have a sense outside of, and "extravagant" <note>In the literal sense of the Latin *extra-vagari*: wandering outside or beyond the common path.</note> from, the universal sense of other men are foolish and mad. But such extravagance is of two kinds: either by going out while ascending higher than everyone or than the majority are accustomed or able to climb—and these are the inspired ones of **divine frenzy** <term>furore: a state of spiritual "madness" or intoxication where the soul is lifted by divine inspiration above ordinary human reason</term>; or by descending lower, where those are found who have a defect of sense and reason greater than the many, the most, and the ordinary people. In this latter species of madness, lack of sensation, and blindness, the heroic jealous lover will not be found.

**SEV.** Even though it might be said to him that "much learning makes him mad" <note>original: "le molte lettere lo fanno pazzo." This is a reference to the New Testament (Acts 26:24), where the Roman governor Festus tells the Apostle Paul that his great learning has driven him insane.</note>, he cannot truly be insulted by such a remark.

**T**HE third cause, figured in the third blind man, proceeds from the fact that divine truth, according to supernatural reason—called **metaphysics**—showing itself to those few to whom it shows itself, does not come...

<summary>Minutolo and Saulino distinguish between the "madness" of the common fool and the "divine frenzy" of the philosopher, arguing that the pursuit of high truth naturally alienates one from the masses. <meta>This page continues the Fourth Dialogue of Giordano Bruno’s "The Heroic Frenzies." Saulino is distinguishing between the slow, discursive nature of human sciences and the instantaneous illumination of divine light. He responds to Minutolo’s previous point about the "madness" of scholars compared to the inspired frenzy of the heroic lover.</meta>

->### Fourth Dialogue<-

...in the **physical sciences** <term>scienze phisiche: the study of the natural world and material things through observation and logic</term>, that is, those that are acquired by **natural light** <term>lume naturale: the innate human capacity for reason, independent of divine revelation</term>, which, by reasoning from a thing known through sense or reason, proceed to the knowledge of another thing unknown (which discourse is called **argumentation**); but [this divine light comes] suddenly and instantaneously, according to the manner that suits such an **efficient cause** <note>efficient cause: in Aristotelian philosophy, the primary source of change or rest; here, God or the divine intellect acting upon the human soul.</note>. Wherefore a divine author said: MY EYES ARE WEAKENED FROM LOOKING UP TO THE HEIGHTS <note>original: "ATTENVATI SVNT OCVLI MEI SVSPICIENTES IN EXCELSVM." From the Vulgate, Isaiah 38:14.</note>. Thus, no span of time, labor of study, or act of inquiry is required to possess it; but it presents itself as swiftly as the solar light proportionally makes itself present, without delay, to one who turns and opens themselves to it.

**MINVTO-LO.** Do you then mean that scholars and philosophers are no more suited to this light than the most ignorant people?

**SEV-LINO.** In a certain way no, and in a certain way yes. There is no difference when the divine mind, through its providence, comes to communicate itself without the disposition of the **subject** <note>The OCR reads "fuggetto," a common transcription error for "soggetto" (subject), referring to the person receiving the divine light.</note>; I mean when it communicates itself because it seeks and chooses the subject. But there is a great difference when it waits and wishes to be sought, and then, according to its own **good pleasure** <note>bene placito: the sovereign and free will of God.</note>, wishes to be found. In this way, it does not appear to everyone, nor can it appear to any others than those who seek it. Wherefore it is said: THOSE WHO SEEK ME SHALL FIND ME <note>original: "QVI QVÆRVNT ME INVENIENT ME." From Proverbs 8:17.</note>. And in another place: HE WHO THIRSTS, LET HIM COME AND DRINK <note>original: "QVI SITIT, VENIAT, ET BIBAT." A reference to John 7:37 and Revelation 22:17.</note>.

**MIN.** It cannot be denied that the apprehension of the second way occurs in time. **SE.**

<meta>catchword: You</meta>

<summary>Saulino explains that while natural science requires time and logical argumentation, divine illumination is instantaneous. He distinguishes between God choosing a subject spontaneously and God being found by those who actively seek Him through spiritual "thirst."</summary>

<meta>This page continues the Fourth Dialogue of Giordano Bruno’s "The Heroic Frenzies." ->### Fourth Dialogue<-

You do not distinguish between the **disposition** <term>dispositione: the state of readiness or preparation of the soul</term> for the **divine light** <term>diuina luce: the illumination of the mind by God or the infinite truth</term>, and the apprehension of it. Certainly, I do not deny that to prepare oneself requires **time**, discourse, study, and effort; but just as we say that **alteration** <note>In Aristotelian physics, a gradual change in a quality (like water heating up).</note> happens in time, while **generation** <note>The instantaneous moment a new form or being is brought into existence.</note> occurs in an instant; and as we see that windows are opened with time, yet the sun enters in a single moment: so it happens proportionally in this case.

The fourth [blindness] signified in the following is not truly unworthy, like that which comes from the **habit** <term>consuetudine: a settled or regular tendency or practice, especially one that is hard to give up</term> of believing the false opinions of the **common people** <term>uolgo: the "vulgar" or unrefined masses, often contrasted with those who seek higher truth</term>, who are far removed from the opinions of the **philosophers**; or it derives from the study of vulgar philosophies which are esteemed as true by the multitude the more they approach **common sense** <note>Meaning sensory perception or the shared, uncritical beliefs of the masses.</note>. And this habit is one of the greatest and most powerful obstacles that can be found: because (as **Al-Ghazali** <note>original: "Alcazele." An 11th-century Persian theologian and philosopher.</note> and **Averroes** <note>original: "Auerrore." A 12th-century Andalusian philosopher known for his commentaries on Aristotle.</note> exemplified) it happens to them just as it does to those who, from childhood and youth, are accustomed to eating poison, and they have become such that it has turned into sweet and proper nourishment for them; and, on the contrary, they loathe things that are truly good and sweet according to common nature. But it is most worthy when it is founded upon the habit of gazing at the true light (which habit cannot come into use for the multitude, as has been said). This **heroic blindness** <term>cecitá heroica: a state where the soul is "blinded" to the material world because it is entirely focused on divine light</term> is such that by it worthily...

<summary>Saulino explains that while preparing for divine illumination takes time and effort, the actual experience of enlightenment is instantaneous. He contrasts the "blindness" of the ignorant, who are habituated to false opinions, with "heroic blindness," a state of spiritual focus that transcends common perception.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion between Minutolo (MIN.) and Severino (SEV.) regarding how the human intellect perceives the divine. It clarifies that "immediate vision" of God does not mean becoming God, but rather seeing through a clearer medium, stripped of the "density" of physical or clouded perceptions.</meta>

->### Fourth Dialogue<-

our eye (when we see) does not receive the light of the fire and gold in its substance, but in its likeness: so the intellect, in whatever state it finds itself, does not receive divinity substantially—otherwise there would be substantially as many gods as there are intelligences—but in its likeness, by which they are not formally gods, but **denominatively divine** <note>Meaning they are called "divine" by association or participation, rather than being the source of divinity themselves.</note>; while divinity and divine beauty remain one and exalted above all things. **SEV.** You speak well; but despite your speaking well, there is no need for me to retract, for I have not said the opposite: but I must declare and explain. Therefore, first I declare that immediate vision, as spoken of and understood by us, does not remove that sort of medium which is the **intelligible species** <term>specie intelligibile: the mental representation or "likeness" through which the mind understands an object</term>, nor that which is the light; but it removes that which is proportional to the thickness and density of the **diaphane** <term>diaphano: a transparent medium, like air or water, through which light passes</term>, or even a completely opaque body coming between: as happens to one who sees through water that is more or less turbid, or through stormy and foggy air; who would be understood to see as if without a medium if he were granted to look through air that is pure, lucid, and clear. All of which you have explained where it says: **HE BREAKS FORTH FROM SO MANY AND SO DENSE BARRIERS.** But let us return to our main point.

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Minutolo and Severino. They are discussing how human perception, being subject to constant change and the flux of the material elements, struggles to grasp the eternal, unchanging nature of divine truth.</meta>

->### Fourth Dialogue<-

...continuous motion, change, and alteration; the operations of which must follow the condition of its faculty, which is a consequence of the condition of its nature and being. How can you expect immobility, stability, entity, and truth to be grasped by that which is always becoming something else, and always makes and is made into another thing and in another way? What truth, what portrait can remain painted and impressed where the pupils of the eyes dissolve into water, the water into vapor, the vapor into flame, the flame into air, and this into yet another thing, the subject of sense and cognition wandering endlessly through the wheel of mutations <note>Bruno refers to the constant transformation of the four elements (water, air, fire, earth) and the "wheel of change" that defines the physical world.</note> unto infinity? **MIN.** Motion is **otherness** <note>original: "alteritá"; the state of being different or in flux</note>; that which moves is always one thing and then another. That which is such always conducts itself and operates in different ways, because the concept and the **affection** <term>affetto: in this context, the emotional or spiritual state of the soul that follows its nature</term> follow the logic and condition of the subject. And that which looks at one thing and then another, in different ways, must necessarily be entirely blind to that beauty which is always one and uniquely itself, and is the very same unity, entity, and identity. **SEV.** It is so.

<meta>This page continues the discussion from the "Seventh Blindness." ->### Fourth Dialogue<-

...intellect. These are those who possess love before they possess understanding; hence it happens to them that all things appear according to the color of their **affection** <term>affection (affettione): a technical term in Renaissance philosophy referring to a person’s emotional state or internal disposition, which "colors" their perception of reality</term>; for he who wishes to learn the truth by way of contemplation must be most purified in his thought.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;**MIN.** In truth, one see that just as there is a diversity of contemplators and inquirers—in that some (according to the habits of their first and fundamental disciplines) proceed by way of numbers, others by way of figures, others by way of orders or disorders, others by way of composition and division, others by way of separation and congregation, others by way of inquiry and doubt, others by way of discourse and definition, others by way of interpretations and deciphering of voices, words, and dialects: whence some are mathematical philosophers, others metaphysicians, others logicians, others grammarians. So too is there a diversity of contemplators who, with different affections, set themselves to study and apply their attention to written sentences: from which it even results that the same light of truth, expressed in one and the same book through the very same words, comes to serve the purposes of sects so numerous, diverse, and contrary <note>Bruno is likely referring to how different religious denominations (sects) all use the same Bible to support conflicting doctrines.</note>. **SEV.** Because of this, it must be said that the affections are very powerful in hindering the apprehension of the truth, even though those suffering from them <note>original: "gli patienti"; those undergoing or affected by these internal states</note> cannot perceive it; just as happens to a stupi...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the various types of "blindness" that affect the soul in its search for the divine. ->### Fourth Dialogue<-

...of a sick man who does not say his palate is embittered, but that the food is bitter.
Now, this kind of blindness is noted by that man whose eyes are altered and deprived of their natural state by what has been sent and impressed from the heart; this is powerful enough not only to alter the senses, but also all the other faculties of the soul, as the present figure demonstrates.

As signified by the eighth <note>The "eighth" refers to the eighth type of lover/blind man described in this section of Bruno's work.</note>, just as the surpassing <term>intelligible object (oggetto intelligibile): in Renaissance philosophy, a truth or concept that can only be grasped by the mind, not the physical senses</term> has blinded the intellect, so the surpassing sensible object placed before this man has corrupted his sense.
So it happens to him who sees Jove in his majesty: he loses his life, and consequently loses his senses <note>Bruno refers to the myth of Semele, who was consumed by fire when she insisted on seeing her lover, Jupiter (Jove), in his true divine form.</note>. Thus it happens that whoever gazes too high is sometimes overwhelmed by majesty. Furthermore, when the divine form <term>species (specie): the essential appearance or "image" of a thing that enters the mind</term> comes to penetrate, it passes through like an arrow; hence theologians say the divine word is more piercing than any point of sword or knife <note>original: "piu penetratiuo che qualfiuoglia punta di spada ó di coltello." This is a reference to the Biblical verse Hebrews 4:12.</note>. From this derives the formation and impression of its own **vestige** <term>vestige (uestigio): a footprint or trace left by a divine presence</term>, upon which nothing else can be impressed or sealed; for where such a form is confirmed, a foreign or new one cannot succeed without the first yielding. Consequently, he can say that he no longer has the faculty to receive anything else, if he has that which fills him, or scatters him through the necessary lack of proportion <note>The human mind is finite; when it encounters the infinite "proportion" of the divine, it is often overwhelmed or "shattered" because it cannot contain it.</note>.

The ninth cause is noted by the ninth person, who is blind through lack of confidence, through dejection...

--- Page 269 ---
<meta>This page concludes the Fourth Dialogue, finishing the discussion on the various forms of spiritual blindness caused by divine love. It specifically addresses the ninth form of blindness, which arises from the reverence and awe the soul feels toward the infinite beauty of the divine, leading to a "holy silence" and the tradition of negative theology.</meta>

...of the spirit, which is governed and brought about by great love, because alongside daring it fears to cause offense. For this reason, the Song of Songs says: **TURN YOUR EYES FROM ME, FOR THEY HAVE MADE ME FLEE.** <note>original: "AVERTE OCVLOS TVOS A ME, QVIA IPSI ME AVOLARE FECERE." This is a quote from the Song of Solomon 6:5.</note> And so, he suppresses his eyes to avoid seeing what he most desires and takes the greatest pleasure in seeing, just as he restrains his tongue from speaking with the one he most longs to talk to, for fear that a flawed gaze or a faulty word might diminish him or in some way cause him to fall out of favor. This usually arises from the soul perceiving that the excellence of the object <note>The "object" refers to the divine or the beloved being contemplated.</note> far exceeds its own potential capacity. Because of this, the most profound and divine theologians say that God is more honored and loved through silence than through words; just as He is seen more by closing one’s eyes to the <term>represented forms (specie representate): the mental images or concepts we form of things</term> than by opening them. For this reason, the <term>negative theology (theologia negatiua): a tradition that describes God by what He is not, rather than what He is, acknowledging His infinite nature</term> of Pythagoras and Dionysius <note>Referring to Pseudo-Dionysius the Areopagite, whose "Mystical Theology" emphasized the unknowability of God.</note> is much more celebrated than the demonstrative theology of Aristotle and the <term>Scholastic doctors (scolastici dottori): medieval university teachers who used logic to explain faith</term>. **MIN.** Let us continue our discussion as we travel along the path. **SEV.** As you please.

->End of the Fourth Dialogue.<-

<meta>This page continues the narrative from the opening of the Fifth Dialogue. Laodomia is recounting the journey of the nine blind lovers who left Campania to find a beauty equal to that of the two sisters, or at least a source of mercy that the sisters denied them.</meta>

...less; with the intention that there might be discovered in her that mercy and pity which was not found in your breast, armed as it is with fierceness. For they judged this to be the only remedy that could divert them from that cruel captivity.

On the third day after their solemn departure, passing near **Mount Circeo** <note>A promontory on the coast of Italy, south of Rome, traditionally identified as the home of the sorceress Circe from Homer’s *Odyssey*.</note>, they were pleased to go and see those antiquities of the caves and temples <term>phani</term> <note>original: "phani," referring to sacred shrines or temples dedicated to a deity.</note> of that goddess. Having arrived there, they all became as if inspired—moved by the majesty of the desolate place, the windy, high, and craggy cliffs, the murmuring of the sea waves breaking in those cavities, and many other circumstances of the place and the season. Among them, one (whom I shall name to you) who was more daring than the rest, expressed these words: 

"Oh, would to heaven that in these times there were present, as there was in other more fortunate centuries, some sorceress <term>saga</term> <note>original: "saga," a term for a wise-woman or enchantress possessing secret knowledge of nature.</note> Circe, who with plants, minerals, poisons, and incantations was powerful enough to place a bridle, as it were, upon nature. I truly believe that she, however fierce she might be, would nonetheless take pity on our misfortune. Greatly urged by our supplicating laments, she would condescend either to provide us a remedy, or else to grant us a welcome revenge against the cruelty of our enemy."

### Fifth Dialogue.

...nature of the figure and its description, of which I shall tell you another time. Wherefore, struck by great wonder and touched by some hope that some propitious deity (who had placed this before them) wished to determine the state of their fortune, they said with one voice that they could incur nothing worse than death, which they estimated as a lesser evil than living in such great passion. Therefore they entered within, finding no door that was barred against them, nor any porter who questioned their cause; until they found themselves in a most rich and ornate hall, where in that royal majesty (you might say that **Apollo** <note>In Greek and Roman mythology, the god of the sun, light, and prophecy.</note> had been found by **Phaeton** <note>The son of Apollo who, according to myth, journeyed to the Palace of the Sun to seek recognition from his father.</note>) there appeared she who is called his daughter. With her appearance, they saw vanish the images of many other deities who were serving them. There, received and comforted with a gracious countenance, they stepped forward; and overcome by the splendor of that majesty, they bowed their knees to the ground, and all together, with that diversity of notes dictated by their differing intellects, they offered their prayers to the goddess. By whom, in conclusion, they were treated in such a way that—blind, wandering, and unfortunately burdened by toil—they have crossed all seas, passed all rivers, overcome all mountains, and traversed all plains for the space of ten years. Miserable, now we are (though late) aware <note>original: "auisti"; aware or advised</note>,

Surely because she believes
That no woman is seen
Under the mantle of heaven with so many attainments.

Not to withdraw from painful struggles,

From below to above,

Heaven has destined that she be found.

### Fifth Dialogue.

...after having merely gazed upon it, they referred and deferred it out of respect and reverence to one lady alone. She, at last—not so much to put her own glory to the test, but rather out of pity and a desire to aid these unfortunate souls—handled it with hesitation; and then, it spontaneously opened by itself. 

How can I describe to you the nature and the magnitude of the Nymphs' applause? How can you believe me capable of expressing the extreme joy of the nine blind men? When they heard the vessel was open, they felt themselves sprinkled with the longed-for waters; they opened their eyes and beheld the two suns. They found themselves possessed of a double happiness: first, the recovery of their previously lost sight; and second, the newly discovered light, which alone could show them the image of the supreme good <term>supreme good: "sommo bene," a philosophical concept referring to the ultimate goal of human life, often identified with the divine or absolute Truth</term> on earth. 

As I ask, how could I possibly express that joy and jubilation <note>original: "tripudio"; an ecstatic dance or song of joy</note> of voices, spirit, and body, which they themselves, all together, could not fully articulate? For a time, one could see so many "furious revelers" <term>furious revelers: "furiosi debaccanti," referring to a state of divine frenzy or Platonic madness (furore) similar to the inspired rites of the Bacchantes</term>, like people who think they are dreaming, or those who cannot believe what they see plainly before them. This continued until the impetus of their fury <note>original: "furore"; here meaning a divine or poetic inspiration</note> was somewhat calmed, and they arranged themselves in the form of a circle <note>original: "ruota"; a wheel or circular formation</note>, where...

# SONG OF THE
## enlightened ones.
<note>In Italian, "Illuminati." This refers to the nine lovers who, after a long spiritual journey of "heroic frenzy," have finally been granted intellectual and spiritual sight.</note>

"I no longer envy the firmament, oh Jove,"
Says Father Ocean with a proud brow;
->Since I am so content<-
By what I enjoy within my own empire.

"What pride is this of yours?" Jove responds,
"What has been added to your riches?
->Oh god of the restless waves,<-
Why does your foolish daring rise so high?"

"You have," said the god of the waters, "in your power
The flaming sky, where the burning
->Zone lies, in which the eminent<-
<note>The "burning zone" refers to the ecliptic or the zodiac, the path where the planets—the "eminent choir"—are seen to move.</note>
Choir of your planets you can see.

Among those, all the world admires the sun,
Which, I tell you, does not shine as much
->As she who makes me<-
A more glorious god of the great mass.
<note>The "great mass" (gran mole) refers to the universe or the structure of the world. Ocean argues that the beauty found on Earth surpasses even the sun in Jove's heavens.</note>

And I embrace within my vast breast
Among others, that country where the happy
->Thames may be seen,<-
<note>original: "Tameſi." Bruno lived in England from 1583 to 1585. This is a direct reference to the River Thames in London.</note>
Which has the pleasant choir of the loveliest nymphs."
<note>The "nymphs" are a poetic reference to the ladies of the English court. Bruno frequently used this mythological imagery to praise the beauty and intellect of the women he encountered in London, particularly Queen Elizabeth I.</note>
